# Daily Summary: 2025-05-26

### DASH: Input-Aware Dynamic Layer Skipping for Efficient LLM Inference with Markov Decision Policies
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17420v1)
- **Authors**: Ning Yang, Fangxin Liu, Junjie Wang, Tao Yang, Kan Liu, Haibing Guan, Li Jiang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable performance across a wide range of NLP tasks. However, their substantial inference cost poses a major barrier to real-world deployment, especially in latency-sensitive scenarios. To address this challenge, we propose \textbf{DASH}, an adaptive layer-skipping framework that dynamically selects computation paths conditioned on input characteristics. We model the skipping process as a Markov Decision Process (MDP), enabling fine-grained token-level decisions based on intermediate representations. To mitigate potential performance degradation caused by skipping, we introduce a lightweight compensation mechanism that injects differential rewards into the decision process. Furthermore, we design an asynchronous execution strategy that overlaps layer computation with policy evaluation to minimize runtime overhead. Experiments on multiple LLM architectures and NLP benchmarks show that our method achieves significant inference acceleration while maintaining competitive task performance, outperforming existing methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DASH, a novel dynamic layer-skipping framework designed to accelerate the inference process for large language models (LLMs). DASH adaptively selects which layers to compute based on input characteristics, formulating the layer-skipping process as a Markov Decision Process (MDP) at the token level. The framework includes a compensation mechanism that injects differential rewards to mitigate performance degradation caused by skipping layers and an asynchronous execution scheme to minimize runtime overhead by overlapping skip-decision computation with forward computation. Experimental results on multiple LLM architectures and NLP benchmarks demonstrate significant inference acceleration with competitive task performance compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a critical problem in the LLM field: the high computational cost of inference. The proposed DASH framework introduces several interesting ideas:

*   **Token-level Dynamic Skipping:**  Moving beyond static or heuristic-based skipping, DASH allows for fine-grained, input-aware decisions about layer execution.  This is a potentially significant advantage, allowing the model to adapt to the specific needs of each input.

*   **MDP Formulation:**  Framing layer skipping as an MDP is a sound approach, allowing the framework to learn optimal policies for balancing speed and accuracy.

*   **Compensation Mechanism:**  The inclusion of a mechanism to mitigate performance degradation from skipping is a crucial element.  The differential reward approach is designed to guide the policy model towards making informed decisions and ensuring minimal performance drop.

*   **Asynchronous Execution:** The asynchronous execution strategy cleverly addresses the potential latency overhead introduced by the dynamic layer-skipping decision process, aiming to make DASH practical for real-time scenarios.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *integration* of these components (token-level MDP, compensation mechanism, asynchronous execution) into a unified framework.  While some individual elements might have been explored in isolation, the combination and the specific design choices for each component present a novel contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The experimental results demonstrate a clear improvement over existing layer-skipping methods on various LLM architectures and tasks. The ablation study helps to disentangle the effects of different components within DASH.
    *   The paper provides a comprehensive analysis of the problem, justifying the design choices made in DASH.  The insights into input-output similarity and layer redundancy provide a strong foundation for the approach.
    *   The method is relatively easy to implement and apply to various LLMs, which facilitates adoption.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper doesn't deeply analyze the transferability of the scoring model trained in one task to another. The experimental setup of this specific aspect is weak. It only measures accuracy in one task with scoring models trained in a few other tasks, instead of showing the general trend across many tasks.
    *   The evaluation metrics for generation tasks using perplexity as a reward is not the most standard way. This makes it more difficult for readers to judge the effectiveness of the method.
    *   The limitation section mentions instability on the first few layers, which might hinder the overall acceleration potential. It would be beneficial to provide specific techniques or ways to fix this issue.
    *   The paper doesn't explore the memory or energy consumption of the new method, but focuses on FLOPs and speed-up.

**Potential Influence:**

DASH has the potential to influence the field by providing a more adaptive and efficient approach to LLM inference. The MDP-based framework could inspire further research into dynamic computation strategies. The asynchronous execution strategy is particularly promising for deploying LLMs in latency-sensitive applications.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM efficiency. DASH's comprehensive framework with its dynamic layer-skipping approach, compensation mechanisms, and asynchronous execution, represents a step forward in addressing the challenge of LLM inference cost. While there are weaknesses, the novelty and the clear empirical improvements over existing methods justify a positive assessment.

### Debiasing CLIP: Interpreting and Correcting Bias in Attention Heads
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17425v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Jie Yeo, Rui Mao, Moloud Abdar, Erik Cambria, Ranjan Satapathy
- **Abstract**: Multimodal models like CLIP have gained significant attention due to their remarkable zero-shot performance across various tasks. However, studies have revealed that CLIP can inadvertently learn spurious associations between target variables and confounding factors. To address this, we introduce \textsc{Locate-Then-Correct} (LTC), a contrastive framework that identifies spurious attention heads in Vision Transformers via mechanistic insights and mitigates them through targeted ablation. Furthermore, LTC identifies salient, task-relevant attention heads, enabling the integration of discriminative features through orthogonal projection to improve classification performance. We evaluate LTC on benchmarks with inherent background and gender biases, achieving over a $>50\%$ gain in worst-group accuracy compared to non-training post-hoc baselines. Additionally, we visualize the representation of selected heads and find that the presented interpretation corroborates our contrastive mechanism for identifying both spurious and salient attention heads. Code available at https://github.com/wj210/CLIP_LTC.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called LOCATE-THEN-CORRECT (LTC) to address biases in multimodal models, specifically CLIP. LTC identifies spurious attention heads in Vision Transformers through mechanistic insights and mitigates them via targeted ablation. Additionally, it pinpoints salient, task-relevant attention heads and integrates discriminative features via orthogonal projection to enhance classification performance. The effectiveness of LTC is demonstrated on benchmarks containing background and gender biases, showing significant improvements in worst-group accuracy compared to existing baselines.  The paper offers interpretability by visualizing the representations of selected heads, validating the contrastive mechanism used for identifying both spurious and salient attention heads.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the "locate-then-correct" strategy. While attention head analysis and debiasing are established research areas, the paper's approach of specifically targeting *both* spurious and salient heads, using a contrastive method for *automated* head identification, and then employing distinct correction methods (ablation vs. projection) is a relatively unique combination. The fact that it provides greater interpretability than some fine-tuning approaches is also a bonus. Most works apply orthogonal projection on either the overall image feature or overall text feature but this work does it at the attention heads level.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from several factors: 1) Practicality: By operating on specific attention heads, the method is computationally more efficient than full fine-tuning. It's also training-free, which adds to its ease of use. 2) Performance: The reported gains in worst-group accuracy demonstrate a real-world improvement in robustness and fairness. 3) Interpretability: The visualization and analysis of attention head representations contribute to a better understanding of how biases manifest within the model.  This could inform the design of better, less biased architectures or training procedures. 4) The empirical findings (e.g., that methods debiasing the overall representation are less effective, that LLMs are unreliable for the task). This could influence subsequent research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and framing.
    *   Well-motivated approach with a strong rationale.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple datasets and bias types.
    *   Detailed ablation studies demonstrating the contribution of individual components.
    *   Interpretability component that provides insights into the model's behavior.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method relies on identifying subgroups (GN) in the data, which might be challenging or impractical in some real-world scenarios. Even though a zero-shot prediction can provide the group labels, the accuracy of prediction can affect the performance.
    *   While the paper addresses background and gender bias, the generalizability of the approach to other types of biases is not fully explored.
    *   The method is specific to CLIP models and Vision Transformers; its applicability to other multimodal architectures might be limited.
    *   The use of LLMs for knowledge injection, while effective, introduces a dependency on external models and their potential biases.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and fair multimodal models. The LTC framework offers a practical and interpretable approach to bias mitigation that could be adopted by researchers and practitioners working in this area. The insights into attention head behavior could also inform future model design.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper provides a solid contribution to the field of bias mitigation in multimodal learning. It combines novel elements with strong experimental results and interpretability. However, it also has some limitations regarding generalizability and reliance on subgroup identification.

### UniTTS: An end-to-end TTS system without decoupling of acoustic and semantic information
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17426v1)
- **Authors**: Rui Wang, Qianguo Sun, Tianrong Chen, Zhiyun Zeng, Junlong Wu, Jiaxing Zhang
- **Abstract**: The emergence of multi-codebook neutral audio codecs such as Residual Vector Quantization (RVQ) and Group Vector Quantization (GVQ) has significantly advanced Large-Language-Model (LLM) based Text-to-Speech (TTS) systems. These codecs are crucial in separating semantic and acoustic information while efficiently harnessing semantic priors. However, since semantic and acoustic information cannot be fully aligned, a significant drawback of these methods when applied to LLM-based TTS is that large language models may have limited access to comprehensive audio information. To address this limitation, we propose DistilCodec and UniTTS, which collectively offer the following advantages: 1) This method can distill a multi-codebook audio codec into a single-codebook audio codec with 32,768 codes while achieving a near 100\% utilization. 2) As DistilCodec does not employ a semantic alignment scheme, a large amount of high-quality unlabeled audio (such as audiobooks with sound effects, songs, etc.) can be incorporated during training, further expanding data diversity and broadening its applicability. 3) Leveraging the comprehensive audio information modeling of DistilCodec, we integrated three key tasks into UniTTS's pre-training framework: audio modality autoregression, text modality autoregression, and speech-text cross-modal autoregression. This allows UniTTS to accept interleaved text and speech/audio prompts while substantially preserving LLM's text capabilities. 4) UniTTS employs a three-stage training process: Pre-Training, Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), and Alignment. Source code and model checkpoints are publicly available at https://github.com/IDEA-Emdoor-Lab/UniTTS and https://github.com/IDEA-Emdoor-Lab/DistilCodec.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniTTS, an end-to-end text-to-speech (TTS) system that avoids decoupling acoustic and semantic information during training. It leverages a novel audio codec called DistilCodec, which distills a multi-codebook codec into a single-codebook one with high codebook utilization.  UniTTS integrates three pre-training tasks: audio modality autoregression, text modality autoregression, and speech-text cross-modal autoregression.  The system is trained in three stages: pre-training, supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and alignment, and is built on top of the Qwen2.5-7B large language model. The authors claim improved naturalness and emotional expressiveness compared to conventional TTS systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the DistilCodec approach for efficiently representing audio with a single codebook, enabling a more direct connection between the LLM and acoustic features.  The integration of the codec within a complete end-to-end LLM-based TTS framework, complete with a multi-stage training approach (pre-training tasks, SFT, and alignment using DPO), is also novel.  While individual components like codebook distillation or LLM-based TTS have been explored, their combination and the specific training paradigm represent a worthwhile contribution. The approach of not decoupling acoustic and semantic information entirely is a departure from some current trends and might offer advantages in capturing nuances.
* **Significance:**  The potential significance stems from the system's capacity to generate more natural and emotionally expressive speech, especially regarding prosody and affective nuances. By training DistilCodec with a large dataset of diverse audio content, not limited to speech, the resulting model may handle universal sounds better than other TTS systems which may use semantic distillation.  The end-to-end nature could also simplify the training and deployment process compared to systems with more complex multi-stage decoding. If the reported results can be consistently reproduced across various datasets and languages, it could significantly impact the field. The potential for LLMs to utilize universal audio data is also appealing.
* **Strengths:** The DistilCodec concept is promising. The use of universal audio data is beneficial. The end-to-end system integration with Qwen2.5-7B can allow for easier incorporation of textual information. The comprehensive three-stage training framework is beneficial.
* **Weaknesses:** The paper needs more rigorous evaluation with standard TTS benchmarks and more objective metrics.  While MOS scores are presented, a more thorough comparison against other state-of-the-art systems across a wider range of metrics (e.g., word error rate when ASR is used on the generated speech, measures of prosodic similarity to the target text, ABX preference tests) is crucial. The ablation studies, while helpful, should have explored individual pre-training objectives in greater depth, especially the universal audio autoregressive task. The reliance on Qwen2.5-7B may limit the system's accessibility given licensing or availability constraints, a point worth addressing. While they show the results of different prompts, it may be better to use the standard TTS prompt, and show results across different prompts.
* **Impact:** The paper shows promise and the results are compelling. However, its impact depends on whether the presented results can be replicated by others and how the model performs on various scenarios. The open-source availability of the code and model checkpoints will facilitate further research and adoption.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach to end-to-end TTS with a unique codec distillation method, and addresses a limitation in other systems by using universal audio. However, the impact requires more extensive evaluations. The improvements shown could stem from the Qwen2.5-7B LLM itself, and requires further analysis on the benefits of DisilCodec.

### T$^2$: An Adaptive Test-Time Scaling Strategy for Contextual Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17427v1)
- **Authors**: Zhengyi Zhao, Shubo Zhang, Zezhong Wang, Huimin Wang, Yutian Zhao, Bin Liang, Yefeng Zheng, Binyang Li, Kam-Fai Wong, Xian Wu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in Contextual Question Answering (CQA). However, prior approaches typically employ elaborate reasoning strategies regardless of question complexity, leading to low adaptability. Recent efficient test-time scaling methods introduce budget constraints or early stop mechanisms to avoid overthinking for straightforward questions. But they add human bias to the reasoning process and fail to leverage models' inherent reasoning capabilities. To address these limitations, we present T$^2$: Think-to-Think, a novel framework that dynamically adapts reasoning depth based on question complexity. T$^2$ leverages the insight that if an LLM can effectively solve similar questions using specific reasoning strategies, it can apply the same strategy to the original question. This insight enables to adoption of concise reasoning for straightforward questions while maintaining detailed analysis for complex problems. T$^2$ works through four key steps: decomposing questions into structural elements, generating similar examples with candidate reasoning strategies, evaluating these strategies against multiple criteria, and applying the most appropriate strategy to the original question. Experimental evaluation across seven diverse CQA benchmarks demonstrates that T$^2$ not only achieves higher accuracy than baseline methods but also reduces computational overhead by up to 25.2\%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "T2: An Adaptive Test-Time Scaling Strategy for Contextual Question Answering":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces T2, a novel framework designed to dynamically adapt the reasoning depth of Large Language Models (LLMs) in Contextual Question Answering (CQA) tasks, based on the question's complexity.  The core idea is that if an LLM can effectively solve similar questions with specific strategies, it can apply comparable strategies to the original question. T2 works by: 1) Decomposing the question into structural elements; 2) Generating similar example questions with potential reasoning strategies; 3) Evaluating these strategies using multiple criteria; and 4) Applying the most appropriate strategy to the original question.  The authors demonstrate that T2 achieves superior accuracy and reduces computational overhead compared to baseline methods across various CQA benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a novel approach to test-time scaling (TTS) for CQA.  While TTS is not entirely new, the key novelty lies in the adaptive reasoning depth based on question complexity determined through the use of similar examples and a multi-criteria selection process. Instead of pre-categorizing question complexity or adding pre-defined human biases to the model reasoning process, T2 leverages the model's existing reasoning capabilities to adapt to different inputs. This is a significant departure from prior work that relies on fixed scaling strategies or human-guided interventions.
*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial problem in LLM-based CQA systems: the accuracy-efficiency dilemma. Current systems often employ either direct answer generation (fast but inaccurate for complex questions) or elaborate reasoning (accurate but inefficient for simple questions). T2 offers a potential solution by enabling concise reasoning for straightforward questions while maintaining detailed analysis for complex ones.  The reported experimental results showing improved accuracy and reduced computational overhead highlight the potential impact of T2 in real-world applications. The reduction in computational requirements is particularly significant given the rising deployment costs of LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined framework:** T2 is clearly presented and well-motivated, with a detailed explanation of its four key steps.
    *   **Adaptive reasoning:** The ability to dynamically adjust computational effort based on question complexity is a significant advantage.
    *   **Multi-criteria selection:** The inclusion of multiple criteria, such as coverage and uniqueness, in strategy evaluation enhances the robustness of the approach.
    *   **Extensive experimental evaluation:** T2 is evaluated across seven diverse CQA benchmarks, demonstrating its effectiveness and generalizability.
    *   **Demonstrated improvements:** The paper demonstrates superior accuracy and reduced computational overhead compared to other TTS approaches. The performance boost is particularly clear for simpler questions where overthinking is eliminated.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on high-quality examples:** The performance of T2 depends on the availability of high-quality examples for similar question matching. In scenarios with limited annotated examples or novel questions, the framework's effectiveness might be limited. In those cases, the reliance on LLM generated synthetic questions might also add bias to the system.
    *   **Textual focus:** The current implementation focuses primarily on textual reasoning tasks. Extending T2 to multimodal reasoning contexts would require additional modifications.
    *   **Complexity of example generation and selection:** While effective, the example generation and selection process introduces a new layer of complexity.
*   **Potential influence:**  T2 has the potential to influence the development of more adaptive and efficient LLM-based systems. Its adaptive reasoning strategy could be applied to other tasks beyond CQA.  The framework also contributes to a deeper understanding of how to leverage LLMs' inherent reasoning capabilities and intelligently allocate computational resources.
*   **Score justification:** While T2 offers a compelling approach to adaptive reasoning and demonstrates promising results, there are still some weaknesses to overcome. Its reliance on high-quality examples, textual focus, and the complexity of example generation and selection limit the real-world application of the framework.

### VEAttack: Downstream-agnostic Vision Encoder Attack against Large Vision Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17440v1)
- **Authors**: Hefei Mei, Zirui Wang, Shen You, Minjing Dong, Chang Xu
- **Abstract**: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in multimodal understanding and generation, yet their vulnerability to adversarial attacks raises significant robustness concerns. While existing effective attacks always focus on task-specific white-box settings, these approaches are limited in the context of LVLMs, which are designed for diverse downstream tasks and require expensive full-model gradient computations. Motivated by the pivotal role and wide adoption of the vision encoder in LVLMs, we propose a simple yet effective Vision Encoder Attack (VEAttack), which targets the vision encoder of LVLMs only. Specifically, we propose to generate adversarial examples by minimizing the cosine similarity between the clean and perturbed visual features, without accessing the following large language models, task information, and labels. It significantly reduces the computational overhead while eliminating the task and label dependence of traditional white-box attacks in LVLMs. To make this simple attack effective, we propose to perturb images by optimizing image tokens instead of the classification token. We provide both empirical and theoretical evidence that VEAttack can easily generalize to various tasks. VEAttack has achieved a performance degradation of 94.5% on image caption task and 75.7% on visual question answering task. We also reveal some key observations to provide insights into LVLM attack/defense: 1) hidden layer variations of LLM, 2) token attention differential, 3) M\"obius band in transfer attack, 4) low sensitivity to attack steps. The code is available at https://github.com/hfmei/VEAttack-LVLM
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the VEAttack paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VEAttack, a novel white-box attack targeting the vision encoder component of large vision-language models (LVLMs). Unlike existing attacks that operate in a task-specific, full-model white-box setting, VEAttack aims for task-agnostic generalization by minimizing the cosine similarity between clean and perturbed visual features extracted by the vision encoder. It does this without accessing the large language model (LLM) component, task information, or labels. The authors theoretically justify and empirically demonstrate that VEAttack can effectively degrade the performance of LVLMs across various tasks, such as image captioning and visual question answering, while significantly reducing computational costs compared to full-model attacks. The paper also uncovers several interesting observations related to LVLM vulnerabilities, including hidden layer variations, token attention differentials, and transfer attack patterns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the task-agnostic nature of the attack. While white-box attacks on LVLMs are not entirely new, previous approaches typically focus on optimizing perturbations for a specific task and require full model access. VEAttack addresses the limitations of these existing attacks by concentrating solely on the vision encoder and using a loss function (cosine similarity) that promotes generalization across different downstream tasks. The theoretical analysis, providing a lower bound on feature perturbation in aligned features, enhances the novelty.
* **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution by offering an efficient and generalizable attack method for LVLMs. The reduction in computational cost and the elimination of task/label dependence are crucial in the context of LVLMs, which are designed for diverse applications. The discovered observations regarding hidden layer variations and transfer attack phenomenon contribute towards better understanding of inner workings of the large vision language models and their robustness and vulnerabilities. The paper's findings could spur further research in both attack and defense strategies for LVLMs. However, it would be even more significant if the authors proposed a countermeasure or at least discussed possible defense strategies given their detailed vulnerability analysis.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Task-Agnosticism:** A key strength is the task-agnostic design of VEAttack, making it applicable to a broader range of LVLM applications.
    * **Computational Efficiency:** VEAttack significantly reduces the computational burden compared to full-model attacks.
    * **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a theoretical analysis to support its design choices, enhancing the rigor of the work.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results demonstrating the effectiveness of VEAttack across various tasks and models.
    * **Interesting Observations:** The findings related to hidden layer variations and transfer attack pattern provide new insights into LVLM vulnerabilities.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **White-Box Assumption:** VEAttack still operates in a white-box setting, which may limit its applicability in real-world scenarios. While it addresses a key limitation in the field, more practical attacks would be appreciated.
    * **Limited Defense Analysis:** The paper lacks a detailed discussion or evaluation of potential defense mechanisms against VEAttack.  This limits the practical impact of the work.
    * **Focus on Vision Encoder:** While the focus on the vision encoder is a strength in terms of efficiency, it might overlook potential vulnerabilities in the LLM or cross-modal alignment components.
    * **Incremental Contribution:** While the paper presents a novel approach, it builds upon existing knowledge of adversarial attacks and vision-language models.

**Justification for Score:**

VEAttack presents a significant and novel approach to attacking LVLMs by targeting the vision encoder in a task-agnostic manner. The paper offers a combination of theoretical grounding, empirical validation, and the discovery of novel LVLM behavior. Although the white-box setting and the limited defense analysis are weaknesses, the research meaningfully advances the field of adversarial robustness for multimodal AI systems. The gains in computational efficiency make the approach more practical than existing methods. However, the lack of defense evaluation and the somewhat incremental nature of the advancement (building on existing attack paradigms) prevent it from earning a higher score.

### PawPrint: Whose Footprints Are These? Identifying Animal Individuals by Their Footprints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17445v1)
- **Authors**: Inpyo Song, Hyemin Hwang, Jangwon Lee
- **Abstract**: In the United States, as of 2023, pet ownership has reached 66% of households and continues to rise annually. This trend underscores the critical need for effective pet identification and monitoring methods, particularly as nearly 10 million cats and dogs are reported stolen or lost each year. However, traditional methods for finding lost animals like GPS tags or ID photos have limitations-they can be removed, face signal issues, and depend on someone finding and reporting the pet. To address these limitations, we introduce PawPrint and PawPrint+, the first publicly available datasets focused on individual-level footprint identification for dogs and cats. Through comprehensive benchmarking of both modern deep neural networks (e.g., CNN, Transformers) and classical local features, we observe varying advantages and drawbacks depending on substrate complexity and data availability. These insights suggest future directions for combining learned global representations with local descriptors to enhance reliability across diverse, real-world conditions. As this approach provides a non-invasive alternative to traditional ID tags, we anticipate promising applications in ethical pet management and wildlife conservation efforts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PAWPRINT and PAWPRINT+, two new datasets specifically designed for individual animal identification using footprint images. PAWPRINT features controlled-environment footprints (sand, clay), while PAWPRINT+ expands to more diverse natural terrains (wood, dirt, snow, asphalt, etc.). The authors benchmarked modern deep learning architectures (CNNs, Transformers) and classical local features (SIFT, ORB) on these datasets. Results show that deep learning performs well in controlled settings, but local features show robustness in more challenging, variable environments. The paper suggests hybrid approaches combining the strengths of both methods.  The authors argue that this approach provides a non-invasive means for ethical animal tracking with benefits for pet management and wildlife conservation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Dataset Creation:** The primary strength is the creation and public release of the PAWPRINT and PAWPRINT+ datasets. A publicly accessible, labeled dataset for individual animal footprint identification is a valuable contribution. Existing datasets are often proprietary or focus on species-level, rather than individual-level, identification. The inclusion of both controlled and natural environments increases the applicability of the datasets to real-world scenarios.
    * **Benchmarking:**  The paper systematically benchmarks a range of popular deep learning models and classical methods. This provides a solid foundation for future research in this area and offers a comparison point for new techniques. The analysis of performance across different environmental conditions is particularly insightful.
    * **Focus on Individual Identification:** The emphasis on individual animal identification is important.  While species-level classification is useful, identifying individual animals is crucial for monitoring specific populations, tracking lost pets, and understanding animal behavior.
    * **Ethical Considerations:** The paper explicitly addresses ethical considerations in data collection, particularly regarding the well-being of the animals.
    * **Hybrid Approach Suggestion:** The conclusion that a hybrid approach (combining deep learning and local features) could be beneficial is a reasonable and potentially fruitful direction for future research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scale:** While a valuable contribution, the datasets, while diverse, are relatively small, with 2,020 footprints from 13 dogs and 7 cats. The limited diversity of animals could limit the generalizability of the results. Larger and more diverse datasets are crucial for developing robust animal identification systems.
    * **Dataset Composition:** While the authors strive for realism, the footprints are still likely cleaner and better defined than many that would be found in a truly uncontrolled real-world setting. Further inclusion of partial or degraded prints would enhance the challenge and relevance. The inclusion of family groups is beneficial but potentially introduces bias if not handled carefully in the analysis.
    * **Limited Novelty in Methods:** The methods used (existing deep learning architectures, SIFT, ORB) are well-established. The paper's novelty lies primarily in the dataset creation and evaluation of these methods on the specific task of individual footprint identification. However, it doesn't propose novel architectures or training techniques specifically designed for this task.
    * **Metric Learning Results are Questionable:** The paper indicates significant decline in performance with the metric learning approach on PawPrint+, where ArcFace with VGG-16 achieved high accuracy (38.45%), but an extremely poor precision (3.20%), indicating issues with false positive predictions. Without properly addressing such concerns, it does not paint a reliable picture.
    * **Limited Depth of Error Analysis:** While the paper mentions family-related confusions, a more detailed error analysis (e.g., confusion matrices) would provide deeper insights into the types of errors the models are making and inform future model development.

* **Significance:**
    * The creation of a publicly available dataset will encourage further research in non-invasive animal identification.
    * The benchmarking results provide a baseline for comparing new methods.
    * The insights into the challenges of generalizing across different environmental conditions highlight the need for robust and adaptable algorithms.
    * The paper contributes to the growing area of animal biometrics and its potential applications in conservation and pet management.

**Overall:**

The paper's main contribution lies in the creation and release of the PAWPRINT/PAWPRINT+ datasets. While the methods used are not particularly novel, the systematic evaluation and analysis on this new dataset are valuable. The paper raises interesting points about the trade-offs between deep learning and local features in different environments. However, the limited dataset size and lack of significant methodological innovation somewhat limit its overall impact.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

A score of 6.5 reflects the following:

* **Positive Contributions (worth +1.5 points):** The significant and positive contribution of publicly releasing a novel dataset addressing a specific and timely problem justifies adding 1.5 points to a "base score" of 5.
* **Dataset Value (worth +0.5 points):** Considering the effort invested in creating and annotating the dataset, with its diversity and the challenges it presents, it deserves a score of +0.5 for its overall value and real-world applicability.
* **Benchmarking Importance (worth +0.5 points):** The structured benchmarking approach offers a clear, concise, and effective comparison of established methods, helping to propel future research efforts in this area.
* **Dataset Size Limitation (-0.5 points):** A deduction of -0.5 points is due to the relatively small size of the dataset, which may restrict the ability of the tested deep learning models to achieve their full potential.
* **Methodological Novelty Limitation (-1 point):** The lack of methodological innovations, as the study mainly applied existing methods, leads to a reduction of -1 point.

The publicly available datasets, benchmarking of existing techniques, and analysis of challenges are worthwhile contributions, but the lack of methodological novelty, dataset size, and questionable performance in metric learning prevents it from receiving a higher score. The datasets provide a necessary tool for future research, which contributes significantly to the value of the paper.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### LeTS: Learning to Think-and-Search via Process-and-Outcome Reward Hybridization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17447v1)
- **Authors**: Qi Zhang, Shouqing Yang, Lirong Gao, Hao Chen, Xiaomeng Hu, Jinglei Chen, Jiexiang Wang, Sheng Guo, Bo Zheng, Haobo Wang, Junbo Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities in reasoning with the emergence of reasoning models like OpenAI-o1 and DeepSeek-R1. Recent research focuses on integrating reasoning capabilities into the realm of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) via outcome-supervised reinforcement learning (RL) approaches, while the correctness of intermediate think-and-search steps is usually neglected. To address this issue, we design a process-level reward module to mitigate the unawareness of intermediate reasoning steps in outcome-level supervision without additional annotation. Grounded on this, we propose Learning to Think-and-Search (LeTS), a novel framework that hybridizes stepwise process reward and outcome-based reward to current RL methods for RAG. Extensive experiments demonstrate the generalization and inference efficiency of LeTS across various RAG benchmarks. In addition, these results reveal the potential of process- and outcome-level reward hybridization in boosting LLMs' reasoning ability via RL under other scenarios. The code will be released soon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LeTS: Learning to Think-and-Search via Process-and-Outcome Reward Hybridization."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses limitations in existing Reinforcement Learning (RL) methods for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), specifically the reliance on outcome-based rewards that neglect the quality of intermediate "think-and-search" steps. The authors propose "LeTS," a novel RL framework that combines outcome-level rewards with rule-based process-level rewards to improve the reasoning and retrieval abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). LeTS incorporates two key process-level reward modules: a knowledge redundancy reward to penalize retrieving previously seen information, and a knowledge match reward to identify and reward correct actions in underperforming rollouts by contrasting them with strong rollouts. The authors evaluate LeTS on various RAG benchmarks, demonstrating improved performance, generalization, and inference efficiency compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the combination of outcome-level and process-level rewards specifically tailored for the "think-and-search" paradigm of RAG. While using process-level rewards in RL is not entirely new, the authors present a well-designed approach that addresses the specific weaknesses of existing outcome-based methods in the RAG context. In particular, addressing knowledge redundancy and matching good actions of underperforming rollouts is novel.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from its potential to enhance the reasoning capabilities of LLMs in RAG. Current RAG systems often struggle with complex or ambiguous queries that require multi-hop reasoning. By providing more granular supervision, LeTS may allow LLMs to more effectively leverage external knowledge and reduce hallucinations. The gains in performance and efficiency documented in the experiments support this claim, and suggest that process-level rewarding schemes can contribute to more robust models under various settings.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of relying solely on outcome-based rewards in RAG.
    *   **Well-Designed Approach:** The process-level reward modules are thoughtfully designed to address specific issues (knowledge redundancy and match rewards), while a reward rescaling mechanism allows the combination of the level rewards.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are performed on diverse RAG benchmarks and using various backbone models (Qwen2.5-7B/3B, base/Instruct) demonstrating strong performance, good generalization capabilities, and faster inference.
    *   **Ablation Studies and Analysis:** The ablation studies and case studies provide evidence for the effectiveness of the process-level reward modules and the ability of the model to handle redundant retrievals.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Rule-Based Rewards:** The process-level rewards are rule-based, which means they require careful design and may not generalize perfectly to all RAG tasks. The complexity of designing effective process-level rewards can be a limitation.
    *   **Scalability to Larger Models:** The paper acknowledges that the approach has not been validated on much larger language models, and that cost constraints limited the scalability. The cost constraints involved in training large language models remain an important issue to deal with.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper can be a valuable contribution to the field of RAG by demonstrating the benefits of incorporating more granular supervision into RL training. Future research could explore the application of LeTS to other reasoning tasks or investigate alternative process-level reward functions, potentially learned rewards.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-motivated, technically sound, and empirically validated approach to improving the reasoning and retrieval abilities of LLMs in RAG. It addresses a clear limitation of existing methods, proposes a novel solution with carefully designed process-level rewards, and provides strong evidence of its effectiveness. While the reliance on rule-based rewards and the lack of validation on very large language models are limitations, the paper demonstrates a substantial improvement over existing methods.

### Self-Training Large Language Models with Confident Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17454v1)
- **Authors**: Hyosoon Jang, Yunhui Jang, Sungjae Lee, Jungseul Ok, Sungsoo Ahn
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown impressive performance by generating reasoning paths before final answers, but learning such a reasoning path requires costly human supervision. To address this issue, recent studies have explored self-training methods that improve reasoning capabilities using pseudo-labels generated by the LLMs themselves. Among these, confidence-based self-training fine-tunes LLMs to prefer reasoning paths with high-confidence answers, where confidence is estimated via majority voting. However, such methods exclusively focus on the quality of the final answer and may ignore the quality of the reasoning paths, as even an incorrect reasoning path leads to a correct answer by chance. Instead, we advocate the use of reasoning-level confidence to identify high-quality reasoning paths for self-training, supported by our empirical observations. We then propose a new self-training method, CORE-PO, that fine-tunes LLMs to prefer high-COnfidence REasoning paths through Policy Optimization. Our experiments show that CORE-PO improves the accuracy of outputs on four in-distribution and two out-of-distribution benchmarks, compared to existing self-training methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of existing confidence-based self-training methods for large language models (LLMs), particularly in the context of reasoning. Current methods rely on answer-level confidence, potentially overlooking errors within the reasoning paths themselves.  The authors propose CORE-PO, a new self-training method that incorporates reasoning-level confidence, measured via P(True), and uses policy optimization to fine-tune LLMs to prefer high-confidence reasoning paths.  The paper demonstrates that CORE-PO improves performance on several reasoning benchmarks, both in-distribution and out-of-distribution, compared to baseline self-training approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating reasoning-level confidence into self-training is reasonably novel. Prior works predominantly focused on answer-level metrics.  The use of P(True) as a measure of reasoning-level confidence, while not entirely new in itself (P(True) was previously explored in the context of factual consistency and trustworthiness of LLMs), is effectively applied in this specific self-training framework to improve reasoning accuracy.  The novelty is, therefore, in the specific combination of reasoning-level confidence, P(True) implementation, and policy optimization within a self-training regime.

* **Significance:**  The paper has potential significance. Improving reasoning capabilities is a key goal for LLMs, and the demonstrated improvements across several benchmarks suggest CORE-PO offers a promising approach.  The out-of-distribution generalization results are particularly encouraging, indicating the method's potential to improve robustness. Furthermore, the analysis highlighting the pitfalls of relying solely on answer-level confidence provides valuable insight for future research in this area. The work also offers a practical methodology (CORE-PO) that can be adopted and extended by others.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing methods and motivates the need for reasoning-level confidence.
    * **Empirical Validation:**  The extensive experiments across multiple datasets provide strong empirical evidence to support the claims.
    * **Analysis and Ablation:** The ablation studies, particularly those comparing monolithic vs. statement-wise P(True) and fine-tuning with ground-truth answers, contribute to a deeper understanding of the method's behavior.
    * **Good Writing and Clarity:**  The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **P(True) Limitations:** While P(True) is a useful measure, its reliability is influenced by the inherent calibration issues of LLMs. Overconfidence in P(True) could still lead to suboptimal training. The paper acknowledges this limitation but could have provided further discussion.
    * **Computational Cost:**  Self-training with policy optimization is computationally demanding, which might limit its accessibility. The paper could address these costs more thoroughly, by relating it to the gains achieved with CORE-PO.
    * **Incremental Improvement:** The improvements, while consistent, are not necessarily revolutionary. In some cases, the gains over SC-PO and SR-PO are relatively small. The value of each improvement needs to be discussed.
* **Impact:** The paper's impact will depend on how widely CORE-PO is adopted and extended by the community. If CORE-PO proves to be a building block for further advancements in self-training for reasoning, its impact will be greater.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of self-training for LLMs. It offers a novel perspective by shifting the focus from answer-level confidence to reasoning-level confidence, supported by solid empirical evidence. However, the improvements are incremental rather than breakthrough, and the reliance on P(True) introduces potential limitations. Also, it builds on and combines pre-existing methods in self-consistency training and policy optimization.

### Hydra: Structured Cross-Source Enhanced Large Language Model Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17464v1)
- **Authors**: Xingyu Tan, Xiaoyang Wang, Qing Liu, Xiwei Xu, Xin Yuan, Liming Zhu, Wenjie Zhang
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) enhances large language models (LLMs) by incorporating external knowledge. Current hybrid RAG system retrieves evidence from both knowledge graphs (KGs) and text documents to support LLM reasoning. However, it faces challenges like handling multi-hop reasoning, multi-entity questions, multi-source verification, and effective graph utilization. To address these limitations, we present Hydra, a training-free framework that unifies graph topology, document semantics, and source reliability to support deep, faithful reasoning in LLMs. Hydra handles multi-hop and multi-entity problems through agent-driven exploration that combines structured and unstructured retrieval, increasing both diversity and precision of evidence. To tackle multi-source verification, Hydra uses a tri-factor cross-source verification (source trustworthiness assessment, cross-source corroboration, and entity-path alignment), to balance topic relevance with cross-modal agreement. By leveraging graph structure, Hydra fuses heterogeneous sources, guides efficient exploration, and prunes noise early. Comprehensive experiments on seven benchmark datasets show that Hydra achieves overall state-of-the-art results on all benchmarks with GPT-3.5, outperforming the strong hybrid baseline ToG-2 by an average of 20.3% and up to 30.1%. Furthermore, Hydra enables smaller models (e.g., Llama-3.1-8B) to achieve reasoning performance comparable to that of GPT-4-Turbo.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Hydra: Structured Cross-Source Enhanced Large Language Model Reasoning".

**Summary:**

The paper presents Hydra, a training-free framework that enhances large language model (LLM) reasoning by integrating knowledge graphs (KGs), document semantics, and source reliability. Hydra tackles challenges in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), including multi-hop reasoning, multi-entity questions, multi-source verification, and effective graph utilization. The framework employs agent-driven exploration to combine structured and unstructured retrieval, increasing the diversity and precision of evidence.  A tri-factor cross-source verification mechanism balances topic relevance with cross-modal agreement. By leveraging graph structure, Hydra fuses heterogeneous sources, guides exploration, and prunes noise early in the process. Experimental results on seven KBQA benchmark datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance with GPT-3.5, outperforming the ToG-2 baseline significantly. The paper also shows that Hydra enables smaller models (e.g., Llama-3.1-8B) to achieve comparable reasoning performance to that of much larger models like GPT-4-Turbo.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its unified approach to integrating structured (KG) and unstructured (text) knowledge sources within a RAG framework. While hybrid RAG systems exist, Hydra introduces several key innovations:
    *   The **tri-factor cross-source verification** mechanism is a valuable contribution. This approach is novel because it doesn't solely rely on the LLM's semantic understanding but incorporates source trustworthiness and consistency to improve the quality of evidence.
    *   The **agent-driven exploration** approach effectively addresses both multi-hop and multi-entity questions by combining structured and unstructured retrieval, increasing the diversity and precision of evidence. The LLM guided refinement allows targeted expansion of the knowledge sources.
    *   The technique of **graph structure utilization** is a strong feature, enabling efficient exploration, guiding retrieval and pruning noise at an early stage.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of RAG and LLM reasoning.
    *   The strong experimental results across seven KBQA datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of Hydra in improving LLM accuracy. The significant outperformance compared to ToG-2 (a robust hybrid RAG baseline) is noteworthy.
    *   The ablation studies are comprehensive and insightful. They highlight the benefits of each component of the Hydra framework, such as the tri-factor score and agentic source selection. Also demonstrating that Hydra works with various LLM backbones and boosts performance even when used with smaller models demonstrates clear significance.
    *   The careful ablation study provides detailed knowledge source compositions, KG completeness with source contributions, ablation and evaluation of LLMs, search and prompting to support significant contributions to its impact.
    *   The error analysis allows for in-depth view into improvements that can be made to reduce hallicunations of specific models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper emphasizes the "training-free" nature of Hydra. While this is advantageous, it would be interesting to see how performance can be further improved with fine-tuning to a specific dataset. This would allow the evaluation of the benefits of both training-free and with fine-tuning.
    *   The experimental section focuses predominantly on KBQA tasks. It could be more impactful to explore this technique with other complex reasoning tasks such as claim verification or multi-document summarization.
    *   The source code needs to be released to reproduce the methods reported in this work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The framework can influence future research in:
    *   The tri-factor verification approach is likely to be adopted and adapted by other researchers in RAG systems. The cross-source verification is particularly vital in complex questions with diverse evidence.
    *   Hydra's graph-aware exploration and pruning methods provide valuable insights into how to effectively leverage KGs for LLM reasoning.
    *   It provides an effective paradigm to combine both structured and unstructured retrieval to improve complex reasoning across a variety of LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The Hydra framework presents a significant advance in RAG systems, showcasing a novel and effective approach to integrating diverse knowledge sources for enhanced LLM reasoning. The empirical evidence is strong, and the ablation studies offer valuable insights. While the limitations mentioned above exist, the overall impact of Hydra on the field of LLM reasoning is substantial.

### Efficient compression of neural networks and datasets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17469v1)
- **Authors**: Lukas Silvester Barth, Paulo von Petersenn
- **Abstract**: We compare, improve, and contribute methods that substantially decrease the number of parameters of neural networks while maintaining high test accuracy. When applying our methods to minimize description length, we obtain very effective data compression algorithms. In particular, we develop a probabilistic reformulation of $\ell_0$ regularized optimization for nonlinear models that does not require Monte-Carlo sampling and thus improves upon previous methods. We also improve upon methods involving smooth approximations to the $\ell_0$ norm, and investigate layerwise methods. We compare the methods on different architectures and datasets, including convolutional networks trained on image datasets and transformers trained on parts of Wikipedia. We also created a synthetic teacher-student setup to investigate compression in a controlled continuous setting. Finally, we conceptually relate compression algorithms to Solomonoff's theory of inductive inference and empirically verify the prediction that regularized models can exhibit more sample-efficient convergence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of efficiently compressing neural networks and datasets while maintaining high accuracy. It compares and improves several methods for reducing the number of parameters, including a probabilistic reformulation of lo regularization, improvements to smooth lo norm approximations, and layerwise methods. The authors introduce techniques like Probabilistic Minimax Pruning (PMMP), Threshold Adaptive Mask Determination (TAMADE), and Random Gradient Pruning to enhance existing compression methods.  They evaluate these techniques on convolutional networks trained on image datasets, transformers trained on text, and a synthetic teacher-student setup. Finally, the paper conceptually links compression algorithms to Solomonoff's theory of inductive inference, providing empirical verification of improved sample efficiency for regularized models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel contributions:
    *   A probabilistic reformulation of lo-regularized optimization that avoids Monte Carlo sampling.
    *   A new layerwise pruning method based on the minimax approach.
    *   Improvements to smooth lo norm approximation methods.
    *   The Random Gradient Pruning method to remove spurious weights.
    *   The Threshold Adaptive Mask Determination (TAMADE) algorithm for automatic threshold finding.
    *   The application of lo regularization to improve transformer compression.
    *   The empirical validation of Solomonoff induction's prediction about sample-efficient convergence.

While some of these methods build upon existing work (e.g., lo regularization, smooth approximations), the specific reformulations, combinations, and the introduction of TAMADE and Random Gradient Pruning represent significant advances. The combination of the algorithms provides the most significant novelty compared to simply running the underlying algorithms.

*   **Significance:** Efficient compression is crucial for deploying large neural networks on resource-constrained devices, reducing energy consumption, and managing large datasets. The paper's successful parameter reduction while preserving (and in some cases, improving) test accuracy has substantial practical implications. Furthermore, the connection to Solomonoff induction provides a theoretical grounding for the observed empirical results, adding to the conceptual importance of the work. The systematic comparison across diverse architectures (CNNs, Transformers) and datasets increases its generalizability.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper meticulously evaluates the proposed methods against a wide array of related techniques across several datasets and architectures.
    *   **Theoretical grounding:** The link to Solomonoff induction adds a deeper understanding to the practical techniques.
    *   **Open Source Code:** Making the code publicly available enhances reproducibility and adoption.
    *   **Practical improvements:**  Techniques like TAMADE directly address practical issues in hyperparameter tuning.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:**  While effective, some of the methods (e.g., PMMP) involve complex formulations and optimization procedures. Further simplification could improve usability.
    *   **PMMP performance:** The probabilistic minimax pruning method does not consistently outperform other methods, requiring further refinement.
    *   **Limitations of the theoretical claims**: The theoretical connections to Solomonoff induction are interesting but still relatively abstract. The paper provides interesting empirical data but it cannot really validate its claims of more *efficient* convergence.
    *   **Transformer experiment dataset**: The choice of only using a small dataset can limit the generalizability of the results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and deployable neural networks. The theoretical insights could guide future research in model compression and regularization. The practical tools and open-source implementation are valuable for practitioners.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**  The paper offers a strong contribution to the field. It presents several novel and useful techniques for neural network compression. The thorough experimental evaluation, open-source code, and theoretical grounding support the paper's claims. It is clearly significant to the field. However, some methods, especially PMMP, require further improvement, and the experimental scope can be broadened.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### SLearnLLM: A Self-Learning Framework for Efficient Domain-Specific Adaptation of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17470v1)
- **Authors**: Xiang Liu, Zhaoxiang Liu, Peng Wang, Kohou Wang, Huan Hu, Kai Wang, Shiguo Lian
- **Abstract**: When using supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to adapt large language models (LLMs) to specific domains, a significant challenge arises: should we use the entire SFT dataset for fine-tuning? Common practice often involves fine-tuning directly on the entire dataset due to limited information on the LLM's past training data. However, if the SFT dataset largely overlaps with the model's existing knowledge, the performance gains are minimal, leading to wasted computational resources. Identifying the unknown knowledge within the SFT dataset and using it to fine-tune the model could substantially improve the training efficiency. To address this challenge, we propose a self-learning framework for LLMs inspired by human learning pattern. This framework takes a fine-tuning (SFT) dataset in a specific domain as input. First, the LLMs answer the questions in the SFT dataset. The LLMs then objectively grade the responses and filter out the incorrectly answered QA pairs. Finally, we fine-tune the LLMs based on this filtered QA set. Experimental results in the fields of agriculture and medicine demonstrate that our method substantially reduces training time while achieving comparable improvements to those attained with full dataset fine-tuning. By concentrating on the unknown knowledge within the SFT dataset, our approach enhances the efficiency of fine-tuning LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SLearnLLM: A Self-Learning Framework for Efficient Domain-Specific Adaptation of Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a framework called SLearnLLM to improve the efficiency of domain-specific fine-tuning (SFT) of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The core idea is inspired by human learning: instead of fine-tuning on the entire SFT dataset, SLearnLLM focuses on the "unknown knowledge" within the dataset. This is achieved by having the LLM answer questions from the SFT dataset, self-assessing the correctness of its answers using Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting, filtering out questions it answers correctly, and then fine-tuning only on the "incorrect-answered" QA pairs. The authors demonstrate that this approach can significantly reduce training time while achieving comparable performance improvements compared to fine-tuning on the full SFT dataset, using experiments in the agriculture and medicine domains with the Qwen models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of focusing fine-tuning on areas where the model struggles is intuitively appealing and aligns with efficient learning principles. The novelty lies in the practical implementation of this idea by leveraging the LLM's own reasoning capabilities to identify its knowledge gaps. The CoT prompting to self-assess is a key component that distinguishes it from blindly using a subset of the training data. However, the core idea of selective fine-tuning isn't entirely new; other methods explore similar themes. The combination of the self-assessment and targeted fine-tuning constitutes a meaningful incremental contribution.

*   **Significance:** The potential for improved efficiency in LLM adaptation is significant. As LLMs grow in size and computational demands, techniques to reduce training costs become crucial. If SLearnLLM can consistently demonstrate substantial time savings without sacrificing performance, it offers a practical advantage for adapting LLMs to specialized domains. The results show some benefits in terms of the efficiency, especially for larger models. This can have potential influence on the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The approach is intuitively motivated and based on established learning principles.
    *   The self-assessment process using CoT prompting is a clever way to identify the LLM's knowledge gaps without relying on external annotations.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the potential for significant training time reduction with comparable performance.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed framework and experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on CoT prompting for self-assessment might introduce biases or inaccuracies in the filtering process. The effectiveness depends on how well the model can reliably evaluate its own reasoning. The results need further exploration on the quality of the data being filtered out.
    *   The experiments are limited to two domains (agriculture and medicine) and Qwen models. It would be beneficial to evaluate the framework's performance on a wider range of domains and LLMs to assess its generalizability.
    *   The selection of 1,000 QA pairs for the validation dataset is relatively small. A larger validation set would provide more robust performance estimates.
    *   The evaluation metrics could be strengthened by including metrics beyond accuracy, such as measures of calibration or robustness.

*   **Impact:** If the method proves to be generalizable across domains and model architectures, and if the self-assessment process is robust and reliable, SLearnLLM could become a valuable tool for efficient LLM adaptation. However, broader validation is needed before assessing the true impact on the field.

**Justification of Score:**

SLearnLLM presents a novel and practical approach to improving the efficiency of domain-specific LLM adaptation. While the core concept of selective fine-tuning isn't entirely novel, the innovative use of LLM self-assessment through CoT prompting and the demonstration of tangible training time reductions warrant recognition. The experimental validation, though limited in scope, provides initial evidence of the framework's effectiveness. Given the significance of efficient LLM adaptation and the solid contributions made by the paper, I believe a score reflecting this is appropriate.

- **Score**: 7/10

### FinRAGBench-V: A Benchmark for Multimodal RAG with Visual Citation in the Financial Domain
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17471v1)
- **Authors**: Suifeng Zhao, Zhuoran Jin, Sujian Li, Jun Gao
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) plays a vital role in the financial domain, powering applications such as real-time market analysis, trend forecasting, and interest rate computation. However, most existing RAG research in finance focuses predominantly on textual data, overlooking the rich visual content in financial documents, resulting in the loss of key analytical insights. To bridge this gap, we present FinRAGBench-V, a comprehensive visual RAG benchmark tailored for finance which effectively integrates multimodal data and provides visual citation to ensure traceability. It includes a bilingual retrieval corpus with 60,780 Chinese and 51,219 English pages, along with a high-quality, human-annotated question-answering (QA) dataset spanning heterogeneous data types and seven question categories. Moreover, we introduce RGenCite, an RAG baseline that seamlessly integrates visual citation with generation. Furthermore, we propose an automatic citation evaluation method to systematically assess the visual citation capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). Extensive experiments on RGenCite underscore the challenging nature of FinRAGBench-V, providing valuable insights for the development of multimodal RAG systems in finance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FinRAGBench-V: A Benchmark for Multimodal RAG with Visual Citation in the Financial Domain":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FinRAGBench-V, a new benchmark dataset for evaluating multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems in the finance domain. The key feature of this benchmark is its focus on integrating both textual and visual information (charts, tables) and requiring systems to provide visual citations to support their answers.  The benchmark includes a large-scale, bilingual corpus of financial documents, a manually verified question-answering dataset, and an automatic evaluation method for visual citation quality. The authors also present a baseline RAG system (RGenCite) and conduct extensive experiments, highlighting the challenges faced by current multimodal LLMs in this context.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a significant gap in financial RAG research by focusing on multimodal data and verifiable citations. Existing benchmarks are often text-centric or lack retrieval support, making FinRAGBench-V a timely and valuable contribution. The introduction of an automatic visual citation evaluation method is particularly novel.
*   **Significance:** The financial domain heavily relies on visual data. A benchmark that evaluates the ability of RAG systems to understand and reason about this data, while also providing traceable results, is crucial for building reliable and trustworthy financial applications.
*   **Comprehensive Dataset:** The dataset is large-scale, bilingual (Chinese and English), and diverse in terms of document types and question categories. This breadth increases the benchmark's realism and ability to test various RAG capabilities.
*   **Thorough Experimentation:**  The authors conduct extensive experiments with various retrievers and generation models, providing valuable insights into the current limitations of MLLMs. These results offer a clear roadmap for future research directions.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Baseline System Simplicity:** While RGenCite provides a useful baseline, it appears relatively simple and might not fully exploit the potential of multimodal RAG. Developing more sophisticated baselines that leverage cross-modal attention or reasoning could be beneficial.
*   **Annotation Bias (Potential):** The generation of QA pairs initially using GPT-4o, even with manual verification, might introduce some bias towards that model's capabilities or knowledge. While manual verification mitigates this risk, it doesn't eliminate it entirely.
*   **Cost and Accessibility:**  The paper mentions the significant cost of using commercial APIs. While the dataset is likely to be made public, the evaluation requires access to proprietary MLLMs.  This could limit the accessibility of the benchmark to researchers without sufficient resources.

**Potential Influence on the Field:**

FinRAGBench-V has the potential to significantly influence the development of multimodal RAG systems in the financial domain. By providing a challenging and realistic benchmark, it can drive innovation in:

*   **Multimodal Retrieval:**  Encouraging the development of retrievers that effectively capture and integrate both textual and visual information.
*   **Cross-Modal Reasoning:**  Promoting the creation of generation models that can reason effectively about charts, tables, and text, and how these relate to each other.
*   **Explainability and Trustworthiness:** Pushing the field towards RAG systems that provide transparent and verifiable results through visual citations.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper introduces a well-constructed and highly relevant benchmark that addresses a critical need in financial RAG research. While there are some limitations regarding baseline simplicity and potential biases, the strengths of the benchmark significantly outweigh its weaknesses. The detailed experiments and novel evaluation methods make this a valuable contribution that will likely spur future advancements in the field.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** FinRAGBench-V presents a significant advancement in financial RAG benchmarking. It addresses a clear gap by incorporating multimodality and visual citations. The dataset's scale and diversity are commendable, and the experiments provide useful insights. However, the limitations related to baseline sophistication and potential annotation biases, and resource requirements prevents a higher score. I justify this score with a consideration that the limitations that exist are recognized by the authors, and are valid areas for continued study on the subject of RAG-based MLLMs in the financial space.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### The Coherence Trap: When MLLM-Crafted Narratives Exploit Manipulated Visual Contexts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17476v1)
- **Authors**: Yuchen Zhang, Yaxiong Wang, Yujiao Wu, Lianwei Wu, Li Zhu
- **Abstract**: The detection and grounding of multimedia manipulation has emerged as a critical challenge in combating AI-generated disinformation. While existing methods have made progress in recent years, we identify two fundamental limitations in current approaches: (1) Underestimation of MLLM-driven deception risk: prevailing techniques primarily address rule-based text manipulations, yet fail to account for sophisticated misinformation synthesized by multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that can dynamically generate semantically coherent, contextually plausible yet deceptive narratives conditioned on manipulated images; (2) Unrealistic misalignment artifacts: currently focused scenarios rely on artificially misaligned content that lacks semantic coherence, rendering them easily detectable. To address these gaps holistically, we propose a new adversarial pipeline that leverages MLLMs to generate high-risk disinformation. Our approach begins with constructing the MLLM-Driven Synthetic Multimodal (MDSM) dataset, where images are first altered using state-of-the-art editing techniques and then paired with MLLM-generated deceptive texts that maintain semantic consistency with the visual manipulations. Building upon this foundation, we present the Artifact-aware Manipulation Diagnosis via MLLM (AMD) framework featuring two key innovations: Artifact Pre-perception Encoding strategy and Manipulation-Oriented Reasoning, to tame MLLMs for the MDSM problem. Comprehensive experiments validate our framework's superior generalization capabilities as a unified architecture for detecting MLLM-powered multimodal deceptions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of detecting manipulated multimedia content, particularly focusing on disinformation generated by multimodal large language models (MLLMs). It argues that current detection methods are limited because they (1) underestimate the sophistication of MLLM-generated deceptive narratives and (2) rely on unrealistic misalignment artifacts that are easily detectable. To overcome these limitations, the authors propose a new adversarial pipeline to generate high-risk disinformation by constructing the MLLM-Driven Synthetic Multimodal (MDSM) dataset. This dataset involves altering images using advanced editing techniques and pairing them with MLLM-generated deceptive texts that maintain semantic consistency with the visual manipulations. The paper then presents the Artifact-aware Manipulation Diagnosis via MLLM (AMD) framework, which features Artifact Pre-perception Encoding and Manipulation-Oriented Reasoning to improve detection accuracy. Experimental results demonstrate the framework's superior generalization capabilities in detecting MLLM-powered multimodal deceptions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects. First, it explicitly recognizes and addresses the emerging threat of MLLM-driven disinformation, which goes beyond simple rule-based text manipulations. Second, the creation of the MDSM dataset is a valuable contribution, as it provides a more realistic and challenging benchmark for evaluating manipulation detection methods. The AMD framework is also novel, utilizing artifact pre-perception and manipulation-oriented reasoning to exploit MLLM capabilities for improved detection. The novelty is relatively high because it directly tackles a limitation of existing approaches to this problem.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is substantial. With the increasing prevalence of AI-generated content, the ability to detect sophisticated disinformation is becoming crucial. The paper's focus on MLLM-driven manipulation is timely and relevant. The MDSM dataset could serve as a catalyst for further research in this area. The AMD framework demonstrates a promising approach for detecting such manipulations, potentially leading to more robust detection systems.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies and addresses the limitations of existing methods.
    *   The MDSM dataset provides a valuable resource for the research community.
