Experiments on vision-language benchmarks demonstrate that this decoupled framework outperforms recent LVLMs, especially on visually intensive geometric math problems.  The code is available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of decoupling visual interpretation and linguistic reasoning is not entirely new, as modular approaches have been explored in other areas. However, the specific application to LVLMs for geometric problem solving and the proposed outcome-rewarded joint-tuning strategy are reasonably novel. The paper highlights a critical flaw in many current LVLMs: their poor visual interpretation, even when the images are simple. By explicitly addressing this bottleneck, the paper offers a practical and potentially more efficient alternative to monolithic end-to-end training.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its practical implications. It suggests a way to leverage the existing strengths of specialized vision models and advanced LLMs, rather than attempting to train a single model to excel in both areas. The decoupled approach allows for more modularity and potentially easier updates. For instance, as LLMs evolve, the vision component can remain the same and the LLM can simply be swapped out for a more powerful one. The performance gains, especially on visually demanding geometric tasks, are compelling. Also, the demonstration of a performance gain over other LVLMs, including some very large models shows that this method has merit.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a weakness of current LVLMs in visual interpretation.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** The decoupled framework and the joint-tuning strategy are clearly explained.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents experimental results that support the claim that the decoupled framework outperforms existing LVLMs.
    *   **Practicality:** The approach offers a more cost-effective and modular way to improve vision-language reasoning, as it relies on existing specialized models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation primarily focuses on geometric math problems. While this is a strong area for the proposed method, it's important to understand how well it generalizes to other types of visual-language reasoning tasks.
    *   **Reliance on Quality of Visual Descriptions:** The LLM's reasoning heavily relies on the quality and completeness of the visual descriptions generated by the VL model. The paper addresses this through the joint-tuning strategy, but further analysis of the limitations of the VL model's descriptions would be valuable.
    *   **Potential for Information Loss:** Decoupling could introduce information loss, as the LLM doesn't directly access the raw visual features. The degree to which information loss occurs isn't fully explored.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The approach can be viewed as an incremental improvement rather than a revolutionary breakthrough. While the paper demonstrates performance gains, it doesn't fundamentally alter the underlying architecture of LVLMs.

* **Justification of score:**

Given the factors above, I assign a score of 7. The paper presents a reasonably novel and practical approach to improving visual-language reasoning by decoupling visual interpretation and linguistic reasoning. While the core idea of modularity isn't entirely new, its application to LVLMs, particularly for geometric math problems, and the proposed joint-tuning strategy are noteworthy. The paper's strength lies in its clear problem definition, well-defined methodology, and empirical validation. While the paper has weaknesses, they do not significantly detract from the overall contribution. The paper also has good clarity in that it highlights what would be future areas of exploration to continue improving this approach. The decoupled nature should make this a much more adaptable method than many of the monolithic training methods of other LVLMs.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Distilling LLM Agent into Small Models with Retrieval and Code Tools
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17612v1)
- **Authors**: Minki Kang, Jongwon Jeong, Seanie Lee, Jaewoong Cho, Sung Ju Hwang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel at complex reasoning tasks but remain computationally expensive, limiting their practical deployment. To address this, recent works have focused on distilling reasoning capabilities into smaller language models (sLMs) using chain-of-thought (CoT) traces from teacher LLMs. However, this approach struggles in scenarios requiring rare factual knowledge or precise computation, where sLMs often hallucinate due to limited capability. In this work, we propose Agent Distillation, a framework for transferring not only reasoning capability but full task-solving behavior from LLM-based agents into sLMs with retrieval and code tools. We improve agent distillation along two complementary axes: (1) we introduce a prompting method called first-thought prefix to enhance the quality of teacher-generated trajectories; and (2) we propose a self-consistent action generation for improving test-time robustness of small agents. We evaluate our method on eight reasoning tasks across factual and mathematical domains, covering both in-domain and out-of-domain generalization. Our results show that sLMs as small as 0.5B, 1.5B, 3B parameters can achieve performance competitive with next-tier larger 1.5B, 3B, 7B models fine-tuned using CoT distillation, demonstrating the potential of agent distillation for building practical, tool-using small agents. Our code is available at https://github.com/Nardien/agent-distillation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes "Agent Distillation," a novel framework for transferring task-solving capabilities from large language model (LLM)-based agents to smaller language models (sLMs) that utilize retrieval and code execution tools. Unlike traditional chain-of-thought (CoT) distillation, Agent Distillation aims to clone the full "reason-act-observe" behavior of LLM agents.  The paper introduces two techniques to improve distillation: a "first-thought prefix" that primes agentic reasoning and a "self-consistent action generation" method to enhance the robustness of sLMs during testing. Experiments across factual and mathematical reasoning tasks demonstrate that sLMs distilled using this approach can achieve performance levels competitive with larger models trained using CoT distillation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a compelling shift from distilling static reasoning traces (CoT) to distilling interactive, agentic behavior. This is a significant step forward, particularly for tasks requiring external knowledge or precise computation. While the individual components (retrieval, code execution) are not novel *per se*, their integration into a unified distillation framework *is* and addresses a critical limitation of standard CoT distillation. The first-thought prefix and self-consistent action generation, while relatively simple, are effective techniques that improve the quality and robustness of the distilled agents.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work lies in enabling practical, tool-using agents based on small language models. This addresses a key challenge in the field: the computational expense of large LLMs, which hinders their deployment in resource-constrained environments. The experimental results are impressive, showing that even very small models (0.5B parameters) can achieve competitive performance.  If these results can be replicated and scaled, the work can significantly accelerate the adoption of agent-based systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation: The paper convincingly argues for the need to distill agentic behavior beyond static reasoning.
    *   Well-designed framework: Agent Distillation provides a coherent and effective approach to cloning LLM agent behavior.
    *   Effective techniques: The first-thought prefix and self-consistent action generation demonstrably improve performance and robustness.
    *   Strong experimental results: The paper presents thorough evaluations across various tasks and model sizes, demonstrating the efficacy of the proposed method.
    *   Well-written and clearly articulated.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited model family: The experiments focus primarily on the Qwen2.5 model series. Generalization across diverse model architectures should be investigated further.
    *   Reliance on simulation: While using wikipedia as the knowledge base offers experimental control, there are differences between simulated vs real-world environment.
    *   Scalability: While distilling the capabilities in small sLMs, it is unclear if these sLMs would scale well given its inherently smaller number of parameters.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the direction of research on language agents and knowledge distillation. It highlights the importance of interactive behavior and tool use for creating effective agents, and offers a promising approach for building practical, small-scale agent systems. The approach can influence other areas of research within LLMs such as safety, knowledge distillation, tool usage, and reinforcement learning.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by addressing the need for efficient and capable tool-using agents. The proposed framework, Agent Distillation, along with its two refinement techniques, demonstrates a clear improvement over traditional CoT distillation. While the paper is not without limitations (e.g., limited model family, dependence on simulation), the strengths outweigh the weaknesses, and the potential impact is substantial.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### MMMG: a Comprehensive and Reliable Evaluation Suite for Multitask Multimodal Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17613v1)
- **Authors**: Jihan Yao, Yushi Hu, Yujie Yi, Bin Han, Shangbin Feng, Guang Yang, Bingbing Wen, Ranjay Krishna, Lucy Lu Wang, Yulia Tsvetkov, Noah A. Smith, Banghua Zhu
- **Abstract**: Automatically evaluating multimodal generation presents a significant challenge, as automated metrics often struggle to align reliably with human evaluation, especially for complex tasks that involve multiple modalities. To address this, we present MMMG, a comprehensive and human-aligned benchmark for multimodal generation across 4 modality combinations (image, audio, interleaved text and image, interleaved text and audio), with a focus on tasks that present significant challenges for generation models, while still enabling reliable automatic evaluation through a combination of models and programs. MMMG encompasses 49 tasks (including 29 newly developed ones), each with a carefully designed evaluation pipeline, and 937 instructions to systematically assess reasoning, controllability, and other key capabilities of multimodal generation models. Extensive validation demonstrates that MMMG is highly aligned with human evaluation, achieving an average agreement of 94.3%. Benchmarking results on 24 multimodal generation models reveal that even though the state-of-the-art model, GPT Image, achieves 78.3% accuracy for image generation, it falls short on multimodal reasoning and interleaved generation. Furthermore, results suggest considerable headroom for improvement in audio generation, highlighting an important direction for future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MMMG, a new comprehensive and human-aligned benchmark for evaluating multitask multimodal generation models.  MMMG covers four modality combinations (image, audio, interleaved text and image, and interleaved text and audio) and includes 49 tasks with carefully designed evaluation pipelines. A key focus is on tasks presenting generation challenges while enabling reliable automatic evaluation through models and programs. The benchmark is validated against human evaluation, achieving high agreement. The paper presents benchmark results for 24 multimodal generation models, identifying strengths and weaknesses, such as the limitations of current models in multimodal reasoning and interleaved generation, and areas for improvement in audio generation. Code and data are released publicly.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its holistic approach to multimodal generation evaluation.  Existing benchmarks tend to focus on individual modalities or use language models as judges without thorough validation. MMMG distinguishes itself by offering a suite of tasks across *multiple* modalities and combinations, coupled with rigorously validated evaluation methods that better align with human judgment. The introduction of several new tasks (29) and the systematic examination of model capabilities via 937 instructions further contribute to its novelty.

*   **Significance:** MMMG addresses a crucial gap in the field.  As multimodal generative AI rapidly evolves, robust and reliable evaluation becomes paramount. The benchmark allows for fine-grained analysis of model performance, aiding in targeted improvements. The high human alignment is a significant strength, as it mitigates the potential for misleading results from poorly correlated automated metrics.  The benchmark's ability to differentiate between top-tier models (highlighted by a performance gap 0.318, much larger than other benchmarks) positions it as a valuable tool for both model developers and researchers.
    However, the reliance on proprietary models for evaluation (e.g., GPT-4o, Gemini 2.5) limits broader reproducibility and accessibility within the academic community. This is a notable weakness.  Also, while the benchmark includes many tasks, one can always question whether MMMG truly represents the full landscape of multimodal tasks now and in the future. The tasks and instructions, while carefully designed, might be influenced by the authors' biases, even if unintended. Finally, the real-world correlation is only done for text-to-image. It would be helpful to show real-world correlations for other modalities if possible.

*   **Justification for Score:**  MMMG is a solid contribution to the field, advancing the state-of-the-art in multimodal evaluation, but has limitations.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Large language model as user daily behavior data generator: balancing population diversity and individual personality
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17615v1)
- **Authors**: Haoxin Li, Jingtao Ding, Jiahui Gong, Yong Li
- **Abstract**: Predicting human daily behavior is challenging due to the complexity of routine patterns and short-term fluctuations. While data-driven models have improved behavior prediction by leveraging empirical data from various platforms and devices, the reliance on sensitive, large-scale user data raises privacy concerns and limits data availability. Synthetic data generation has emerged as a promising solution, though existing methods are often limited to specific applications. In this work, we introduce BehaviorGen, a framework that uses large language models (LLMs) to generate high-quality synthetic behavior data. By simulating user behavior based on profiles and real events, BehaviorGen supports data augmentation and replacement in behavior prediction models. We evaluate its performance in scenarios such as pertaining augmentation, fine-tuning replacement, and fine-tuning augmentation, achieving significant improvements in human mobility and smartphone usage predictions, with gains of up to 18.9%. Our results demonstrate the potential of BehaviorGen to enhance user behavior modeling through flexible and privacy-preserving synthetic data generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BehaviorGen, a framework that uses large language models (LLMs) to generate synthetic user behavior data.  It aims to address the challenges of privacy concerns and limited data availability in human behavior modeling. BehaviorGen simulates user behavior based on profiles and real-world events, supporting data augmentation and replacement strategies for behavior prediction models. The framework is evaluated across scenarios such as pretraining augmentation, fine-tuning replacement, and fine-tuning augmentation, showing improvements in human mobility and smartphone usage prediction tasks. The authors argue that BehaviorGen offers a flexible and privacy-preserving approach to enhance user behavior modeling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to generate synthetic behavioral data isn't entirely novel.  Existing works (cited in the paper itself) have explored LLMs for human behavior simulation.  The specific contribution lies in the BehaviorGen framework's application within a two-stage model paradigm (pretraining and fine-tuning) and its systematic evaluation across different data augmentation/replacement scenarios. While other work cited here generated synthetic data, a significant amount of effort and time has been spent on fine-tuning the model to improve its realism as well as providing additional data as needed to fill gaps. The implementation of format restrictions, segmented generation, and role settings does provide minor novel contributions to this work.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practically important problem: the scarcity of high-quality, privacy-compliant user behavior data. If BehaviorGen genuinely delivers on its promise, it could have a positive impact on downstream applications reliant on such data.  The systematic evaluation across different usage scenarios is a strength, as it provides insights into the potential benefits and limitations of synthetic data generation in various contexts.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper tackles a relevant and increasingly important problem.
    *   The BehaviorGen framework offers a structured approach for synthetic data generation, considering both population diversity and individual personality.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate tangible improvements in behavior prediction tasks using the generated synthetic data. The systematic study of the various scenarios, including the ablation study, bolsters the findings.
    *   The study of privacy limitations is a welcome and necessary addition.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper's claims regarding novelty are somewhat overstated. The core idea of using LLMs for behavioral data generation is not entirely new.  The contributions are more incremental, related to the specific framework design and evaluation.
    *   While the experiments show improvements, it's crucial to acknowledge the potential biases introduced by the LLMs themselves.  The paper briefly touches on this but could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of bias mitigation strategies. A more detailed analysis of the *types* of errors or discrepancies still present in the synthetic data would be valuable.
    *   The paper makes strong claims about privacy preservation but acknowledges the need for privacy-preserving mechanisms on top of just synthetic data. This might be misconstrued that BehaviorGen is a complete "privacy shield", which is not the case.
    *   The analysis in Section 5.4 (Case Study: Intent Distribution Analysis) is limited to only two users and, due to this small sample, is difficult to generalize to all users in a comprehensive manner.
*   **Impact:** The paper's impact hinges on the practicality and generalizability of the BehaviorGen framework.  If the framework can be easily adapted and deployed in real-world applications without sacrificing performance or introducing significant biases, it could influence the development of more privacy-aware and data-efficient behavior modeling systems.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper addresses a relevant problem and demonstrates promising results, its novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking.  The systematic evaluation and practical benefits of BehaviorGen warrant recognition, but the potential for bias and the overstatement of novelty slightly temper the score. The paper could benefit from a more nuanced discussion of the limitations, particularly concerning the LLM's inherent biases and more extensive exploration of results.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Runaway is Ashamed, But Helpful: On the Early-Exit Behavior of Large Language Model-based Agents in Embodied Environments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17616v1)
- **Authors**: Qingyu Lu, Liang Ding, Siyi Cao, Xuebo Liu, Kanjian Zhang, Jinxia Zhang, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: Agents powered by large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong planning and decision-making capabilities in complex embodied environments. However, such agents often suffer from inefficiencies in multi-turn interactions, frequently trapped in repetitive loops or issuing ineffective commands, leading to redundant computational overhead. Instead of relying solely on learning from trajectories, we take a first step toward exploring the early-exit behavior for LLM-based agents. We propose two complementary approaches: 1. an $\textbf{intrinsic}$ method that injects exit instructions during generation, and 2. an $\textbf{extrinsic}$ method that verifies task completion to determine when to halt an agent's trial. To evaluate early-exit mechanisms, we introduce two metrics: one measures the reduction of $\textbf{redundant steps}$ as a positive effect, and the other evaluates $\textbf{progress degradation}$ as a negative effect. Experiments with 4 different LLMs across 5 embodied environments show significant efficiency improvements, with only minor drops in agent performance. We also validate a practical strategy where a stronger agent assists after an early-exit agent, achieving better performance with the same total steps. We will release our code to support further research.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Runaway is Ashamed, But Helpful: On the Early-Exit Behavior of Large Language Model-based Agents in Embodied Environments" explores methods to improve the efficiency of LLM-based agents operating in embodied environments. The authors introduce two complementary early-exit strategies: Intrinsic Early Exit (injecting self-termination instructions into the agent's prompt) and Extrinsic Early Exit (using a verification module to monitor task completion). They introduce two new metrics, Redundancy Steps and Progress Degradation, to evaluate the effectiveness of these strategies. Experiments conducted on various embodied and gaming environments demonstrate that early-exit mechanisms significantly reduce unnecessary interactions and improve efficiency with minimal performance drops. The paper further investigates a practical application where a stronger agent can take over from a weaker agent after an early exit, leading to enhanced overall performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Strengths:*

1.  **Novelty:** The paper addresses an underexplored area: improving the efficiency of LLM-based agents in embodied environments through early-exit mechanisms. While early exiting is not entirely new in other contexts, its specific application to LLM agents in embodied tasks and the proposed strategies (intrinsic and extrinsic) offer a novel perspective.
2.  **Practical Relevance:** The issue of LLM-based agents getting stuck in unproductive loops is a significant practical problem, particularly in real-world applications. The proposed methods offer a direct solution to this problem, potentially saving computational resources, energy, and preventing physical damage.
3.  **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct experiments on a variety of datasets spanning different environments and LLMs. The introduction of Redundancy Steps and Progress Degradation provides a more nuanced evaluation of early-exit performance than traditional metrics like success rate alone.
4.  **Practical Application:** The idea of using a stronger agent to continue exploration after an early exit by a weaker agent showcases the practical benefits of the proposed mechanism.
5.  **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written, and the figures clearly illustrate the proposed methods and their impact.

*Weaknesses:*

1.  **Limited Generalization:** The datasets, while diverse, are still limited in scope. The paper acknowledges this limitation and suggests future work to evaluate the approach on web navigation or app execution tasks.  While the paper tests different LLMs, the conclusion that the approach works regardless of the model architecture might be too bold.
2.  **Prompt Sensitivity:** The reliance on prompt engineering for Intrinsic Early Exit introduces potential instability and sensitivity to prompt variations.  Although the paper explores prompt variants, a more robust mechanism for ensuring reliable self-termination would be desirable. The paper does not address the risk of malicious actors using LLM agents, which is an increasingly important consideration.
3.  **Performance Gains:** While the results show positive trends, the reported performance gains in some environments are marginal. In some cases, early exit leads to performance degradation. A clearer delineation of when the strategies are most effective would strengthen the paper.
4.  **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:**  The paper is primarily empirical. Adding some theoretical justification for the effectiveness of the proposed approaches could improve the paper.
5.  **Limited Integration:** The paper acknowledges that they do not fine-tune the models, restricting their experiments to plug-and-play approaches. Including fine-tuning would improve the results of the proposed approaches.

*Significance:*

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM-based agents by addressing the efficiency bottleneck in embodied environments. It provides a practical framework for developing self-aware agents that can recognize and avoid unproductive interactions. The proposed metrics provide a useful tool for evaluating early-exit strategies. While the approach has limitations, it represents a significant step towards building more efficient and reliable LLM agents for real-world applications.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper's novelty and practical relevance warrant a relatively high score. The introduction of early-exit mechanisms and metrics is a significant contribution. However, the limitations related to generalization, prompt sensitivity, performance gains, and the purely empirical nature of the research, prevent it from achieving a higher score. Although limited, the results are promising, and the paper opens avenues for future research in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Navigate the Unknown: Enhancing LLM Reasoning with Intrinsic Motivation Guided Exploration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17621v1)
- **Authors**: Jingtong Gao, Ling Pan, Yejing Wang, Rui Zhong, Chi Lu, Qingpeng Cai, Peng Jiang, Xiangyu Zhao
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a pivotal method for improving the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, prevalent RL approaches such as Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and Group-Regularized Policy Optimization (GRPO) face critical limitations due to their reliance on sparse outcome-based rewards and inadequate mechanisms for incentivizing exploration. These limitations result in inefficient guidance for multi-step reasoning processes. Specifically, sparse reward signals fail to deliver effective or sufficient feedback, particularly for challenging problems. Furthermore, such reward structures induce systematic biases that prioritize exploitation of familiar trajectories over novel solution discovery. These shortcomings critically hinder performance in complex reasoning tasks, which inherently demand iterative refinement across ipntermediate steps. To address these challenges, we propose an Intrinsic Motivation guidEd exploratioN meThOd foR LLM Reasoning (i-MENTOR), a novel method designed to both deliver dense rewards and amplify explorations in the RL-based training paradigm. i-MENTOR introduces three key innovations: trajectory-aware exploration rewards that mitigate bias in token-level strategies while maintaining computational efficiency; dynamic reward scaling to stabilize exploration and exploitation in large action spaces; and advantage-preserving reward implementation that maintains advantage distribution integrity while incorporating exploratory guidance. Experiments across three public datasets demonstrate i-MENTOR's effectiveness with a 22.39% improvement on the difficult dataset Countdown-4.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces i-MENTOR, a novel reinforcement learning (RL) method designed to enhance the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses limitations in existing RL approaches like PPO and GRPO, which often struggle due to sparse outcome-based rewards and inadequate exploration mechanisms. i-MENTOR proposes three key innovations: (1) trajectory-aware exploration rewards to mitigate bias and improve efficiency, (2) dynamic reward scaling to stabilize exploration and exploitation, and (3) advantage-preserving reward implementation to maintain the integrity of advantage distributions while incorporating exploratory guidance.  The method is evaluated across three public datasets, demonstrating improved performance, particularly on challenging problems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of trajectory-aware rewards with dynamic scaling and advantage preservation. While individual components like intrinsic motivation for RL and reward shaping exist, the integration, particularly in the context of LLM reasoning, is a significant contribution. The trajectory-aware reward tackles the specific problems of varying sequence lengths and computational overload in LLMs, making the exploration more efficient. The dynamic reward scaling addresses a practical problem: reward instability in LLM training, which previous methods have not adequately addressed. The advantage-preserving reward injection is clever, avoiding conflicts with standard RL updates.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in applying RL to LLMs: effective exploration in complex reasoning tasks. The experiments demonstrate performance gains over strong baselines (PPO and GRPO) on various datasets, which highlights i-MENTOR's practical utility. The substantial improvement on Countdown-4, a difficult reasoning task, is particularly significant, suggesting that i-MENTOR is effective at tackling problems where existing methods falter. The detailed ablation study is strong, systematically evaluating the contributions of each component of the method and demonstrating that all three components are needed for optimal performance.  The qualitative case study, while small in scope, provides intuitive evidence of the improvements in the model's reasoning process.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem statement: clearly articulates the challenges of sparse rewards and inadequate exploration in LLM reasoning with RL.
    *   Novel and well-justified method: The proposed i-MENTOR method is technically sound and motivated by the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation: The paper presents extensive experiments on multiple datasets, including ablation studies and comparisons to strong baselines.
    *   Clear presentation: The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the method and results.
    *   Addresses a Practical LLM Training Issue.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Computational Cost: While the paper argues that the i-MENTOR method is computationally efficient, it does not provide a detailed analysis of the computational overhead compared to the baselines. A more rigorous analysis of computational cost would strengthen the paper.
    *   Limited Parameter Tuning Details: The paper states that fixed hyperparameters were used for all datasets for a fairer comparison. However, a discussion of the sensitivity of the method to different hyperparameter settings would be beneficial. The addition of the sensitivity analysis of two parameters strengthens the paper.
    *   Limited Qualitative Analysis: A more extensive qualitative analysis of the reasoning process of LLMs trained with i-MENTOR would provide a deeper understanding of the benefits of the method. While the paper presents a case study, a more detailed analysis of the reasoning steps would be valuable.
    *   Reliance on Specific Datasets. While the performance is notable, validation across a greater variety of tasks or a discussion of the limitations and assumptions for transferability of the proposed method might enhance the practical value of the work.

*   **Potential Influence:** i-MENTOR has the potential to influence future research on RL for LLMs by providing a more effective way to address the challenges of exploration and sparse rewards.  The core ideas could be adapted and extended to other LLM tasks beyond reasoning.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and well-justified method for improving LLM reasoning through reinforcement learning. The comprehensive experimental evaluation demonstrates the effectiveness of the method, and the ablation study provides insights into the contributions of each component. While the paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of computational cost and more extensive qualitative analysis, its strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The potential impact of i-MENTOR on the field of LLM research warrants a score of 8. The approach addresses limitations of current RL implementations and improves exploration in a way that demonstrably and measurably improves results, making it a significant and valuable contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Enhancing Large Vision-Language Models with Layout Modality for Table Question Answering on Japanese Annual Securities Reports
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17625v1)
- **Authors**: Hayato Aida, Kosuke Takahashi, Takahiro Omi
- **Abstract**: With recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) and growing interest in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), the ability to understand table structures has become increasingly important. This is especially critical in financial domains such as securities reports, where highly accurate question answering (QA) over tables is required. However, tables exist in various formats-including HTML, images, and plain text-making it difficult to preserve and extract structural information. Therefore, multimodal LLMs are essential for robust and general-purpose table understanding. Despite their promise, current Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs), which are major representatives of multimodal LLMs, still face challenges in accurately understanding characters and their spatial relationships within documents. In this study, we propose a method to enhance LVLM-based table understanding by incorporating in-table textual content and layout features. Experimental results demonstrate that these auxiliary modalities significantly improve performance, enabling robust interpretation of complex document layouts without relying on explicitly structured input formats.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the problem of table question answering (QA) in Japanese annual securities reports, focusing on the challenges of extracting accurate information from tables presented in various formats (HTML, images, etc.).  The authors propose a method to enhance Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) by incorporating not only image modality but also text and layout features derived from the HTML table structure. They reformulate the NTCIR-18 U4 Table QA dataset into a simpler task, TableCellQA, focusing on direct cell value extraction to isolate the model's ability to understand table structure.  They show that incorporating layout and text modalities significantly improves performance over image-only baselines. They also experimented with structured table formats and compared their approach to other state-of-the-art models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the specific application of LVLMs to the TableCellQA task within the context of Japanese securities reports and in the incorporation of layout information along with image and text modalities. While the individual components (LVLMs, layout embeddings, application to tables) aren't entirely new, the combination and focused evaluation on this particular task has merit. The reformulation of the NTCIR dataset into TableCellQA for isolating structural understanding is a sensible simplification that allows a clearer understanding of the contribution of each modality. The evaluation of different structural table formats (HTML, JSON, Markdown) is also valuable. However, the approach isn't drastically different from similar works (e.g., LayTextLLM, LayoutLM), limiting the potential impact.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and practical problem: extracting structured data from financial documents.  The empirical results demonstrate tangible improvements by adding layout and text to image-based LVLMs. This is significant because it provides a pathway to better automate financial data analysis, which is heavily reliant on tabular data.  The release of the TableCellQA dataset will be beneficial for the research community, allowing others to benchmark and compare their models on this task. However, the work's scope is somewhat limited to direct cell value extraction. While this simplification helps isolate structure understanding, it does exclude more advanced reasoning needed in some real-world financial tasks. The paper shows interesting results about task-specific pre-training decreasing performance which is valuable, but could benefit from a deeper analysis to better understand the underlying causes.
* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem definition and motivation.
    * Sensible reformulation of the NTCIR-18 dataset.
    * Thorough evaluation with ablation studies isolating the contribution of each modality.
    * Analysis of different structured table formats.
    * Useful insights into the importance of text and layout for table understanding.
    * Planned public release of the dataset.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The core method is not radically new, building upon existing techniques in layout embedding and LVLMs.
    * The TableCellQA task is a simplified version of real-world table QA, limiting the generalizability of the findings. More complex reasoning and unit conversions, or more involved financial reasoning, are not explicitly addressed.
    * Limited analysis of why task-specific pre-training did not improve performance, which could have increased the paper's insights.
    * The paper could benefit from a deeper analysis of error cases and model limitations beyond just the observation of OCR errors.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence researchers working on table understanding and financial document processing. It provides a useful benchmark dataset (TableCellQA) and practical insights into the role of different modalities. However, given the incremental nature of the improvements and the limitations of the task, its long-term influence will be modest.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid, well-executed study that makes a worthwhile contribution to the field of table understanding. The combination of existing techniques (LVLMs, layout embeddings) is applied effectively to a relevant problem (financial document processing), and the results demonstrate tangible improvements.  The dataset release will be valuable.  However, the paper's novelty is somewhat limited by its reliance on established methods, and the simplification of the task (TableCellQA) restricts the scope of its findings. A deeper exploration of model limitations, error analysis, and the implications of task-specific pre-training would have significantly strengthened the paper. Therefore, the score is a 6.5, indicating a valuable but not transformative contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### GIM: Improved Interpretability for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17630v1)
- **Authors**: Joakim Edin, Róbert Csordás, Tuukka Ruotsalo, Zhengxuan Wu, Maria Maistro, Jing Huang, Lars Maaløe
- **Abstract**: Ensuring faithful interpretability in large language models is imperative for trustworthy and reliable AI. A key obstacle is self-repair, a phenomenon where networks compensate for reduced signal in one component by amplifying others, masking the true importance of the ablated component. While prior work attributes self-repair to layer normalization and back-up components that compensate for ablated components, we identify a novel form occurring within the attention mechanism, where softmax redistribution conceals the influence of important attention scores. This leads traditional ablation and gradient-based methods to underestimate the significance of all components contributing to these attention scores. We introduce Gradient Interaction Modifications (GIM), a technique that accounts for self-repair during backpropagation. Extensive experiments across multiple large language models (Gemma 2B/9B, LLAMA 1B/3B/8B, Qwen 1.5B/3B) and diverse tasks demonstrate that GIM significantly improves faithfulness over existing circuit identification and feature attribution methods. Our work is a significant step toward better understanding the inner mechanisms of LLMs, which is crucial for improving them and ensuring their safety. Our code is available at https://github.com/JoakimEdin/gim.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GIM: Improved Interpretability for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of faithful interpretability in large language models (LLMs), focusing on the phenomenon of "self-repair." It identifies a novel form of self-repair occurring within the attention mechanism, where softmax redistribution masks the influence of important attention scores.  This leads to traditional ablation and gradient-based methods underestimating the significance of contributing components.  To counter this, the authors introduce Gradient Interaction Modifications (GIM), a technique that modifies backpropagation to account for self-repair. GIM combines temperature-adjusted softmax gradients, layernorm freezing, and gradient normalization. The authors conduct extensive experiments across multiple LLMs and diverse tasks, demonstrating that GIM improves faithfulness compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The identification of attention self-repair as a distinct problem undermining LLM interpretability is a significant contribution.  While prior work has discussed self-repair more broadly, pinpointing its manifestation within the attention mechanism and linking it to softmax redistribution constitutes a novel finding. While the combination of existing backpropagation modifications is not entirely new, their application is specifically targeted at addressing the newly identified self-repair issue within the attention mechanism.

* **Significance:** Faithful interpretability is crucial for building trustworthy and reliable LLMs. By addressing the self-repair problem, this paper provides a significant step towards better understanding LLM inner workings. The paper's claim of improved faithfulness across multiple models and tasks, if rigorously supported by the experimental results (and assuming the datasets are reasonably representative of common LLM use cases), is significant. Such work could enable more informed model improvement, identification of vulnerabilities, and safer deployment.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper precisely articulates the attention self-repair problem and its detrimental effects on existing interpretability methods. The mathematical formulation is a strength.
    *   **Targeted Solution:** GIM is specifically designed to address the identified issue, combining existing and potentially new techniques in a principled way.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** The authors performed experiments across several LLMs, diverse tasks, and comparing against multiple baselines. This adds robustness to the findings, assuming sufficient experimental controls and statistical rigor.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The inclusion of an ablation study to demonstrate the individual contributions of TSG, Layernorm Freeze, and Grad Norm is beneficial. It allows for a more nuanced understanding of how each component contributes to the overall improvement.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** While the paper tests multiple LLMs, model architectures are rapidly evolving. The extent to which GIM generalizes to very large models, different architectures (e.g., MoE models), and future generations of LLMs remains an open question.
    *   **Dependency on Hyperparameter Tuning:** The paper mentions a "sweetspot" for temperature selection in TSG. This implies that GIM might require careful hyperparameter tuning for each model-dataset pair, increasing its practical application complexity. If too sensitive, this could limit its adoption.
    *   **Faithfulness Metric Limitations:** Faithfulness metrics, such as comprehensiveness and sufficiency, can be imperfect proxies for true explanation quality. The paper's reliance on these metrics may not fully capture the value of the proposed method. While standard in the field, this remains a valid critique of such studies.
     *   **Computational Cost:** While GIM modifies backpropagation, the extensive ablation studies and model comparisons suggest non-negligible computational cost. A detailed analysis of the computational overhead of GIM in terms of memory and speed would be beneficial.

* **Potential Influence:** If the paper's claims of improved faithfulness hold up under further scrutiny, GIM could become a valuable tool for LLM researchers and practitioners. It could influence the design of more interpretable models, the development of new interpretability techniques, and the evaluation of existing methods. The contribution might foster new research avenues focusing on different kinds of self-repair mechanism that occurs in LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of identifying attention self-repair, the significance of addressing it for improved LLM interpretability, the clearly defined problem, targeted solution, extensive evaluation, and ablation study, the paper represents a noteworthy contribution to the field.

However, there are valid concerns related to generalizability, hyperparameter sensitivity, limitations of the faithfulness metrics used, and the computational cost.

Balancing these factors, a score of **7** is appropriate. It acknowledges the paper's significant contributions while recognizing the limitations that warrant further investigation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReqBrain: Task-Specific Instruction Tuning of LLMs for AI-Assisted Requirements Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17632v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammad Kasra Habib, Daniel Graziotin, Stefan Wagner
- **Abstract**: Requirements elicitation and specification remains a labor-intensive, manual process prone to inconsistencies and gaps, presenting a significant challenge in modern software engineering. Emerging studies underscore the potential of employing large language models (LLMs) for automated requirements generation to support requirements elicitation and specification; however, it remains unclear how to implement this effectively. In this work, we introduce ReqBrain, an Al-assisted tool that employs a fine-tuned LLM to generate authentic and adequate software requirements. Software engineers can engage with ReqBrain through chat-based sessions to automatically generate software requirements and categorize them by type. We curated a high-quality dataset of ISO 29148-compliant requirements and fine-tuned five 7B-parameter LLMs to determine the most effective base model for ReqBrain. The top-performing model, Zephyr-7b-beta, achieved 89.30\% Fl using the BERT score and a FRUGAL score of 91.20 in generating authentic and adequate requirements. Human evaluations further confirmed ReqBrain's effectiveness in generating requirements. Our findings suggest that generative Al, when fine-tuned, has the potential to improve requirements elicitation and specification, paving the way for future extensions into areas such as defect identification, test case generation, and agile user story creation.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

The paper introduces ReqBrain, an AI-assisted tool that utilizes a fine-tuned large language model (LLM) to generate authentic and adequate software requirements. The authors curate a dataset of ISO 29148-compliant requirements and fine-tune several 7B-parameter LLMs, identifying Zephyr-7b-beta as the most effective.  The study compares ReqBrain against its untuned baseline, ChatGPT-4, and human-authored requirements, using both automated NLP metrics and human evaluations. The results demonstrate ReqBrain's ability to generate requirements that are indistinguishable from human-authored ones and compliant with ISO 29148 standards.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a practical application of fine-tuned LLMs for a specific task in requirements engineering, which is generating software requirements. While the idea of using LLMs for requirements engineering is not entirely novel, the paper makes a few key contributions:

*   **Specific Focus on Authenticity and Adequacy:** The authors explicitly target the generation of *authentic* and *adequate* requirements, defining these terms clearly and operationalizing them through specific evaluation criteria (ISO 29148 compliance, consistency, completeness). This focus goes beyond simply generating text that resembles requirements.
*   **High-Quality Dataset and Rigorous Fine-Tuning:** The creation of a high-quality dataset for fine-tuning the LLM is a valuable contribution.  The careful curation of ISO 29148-compliant requirements from various sources, and filtering the noise improves the dataset.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper employs a mixed-methods approach, combining automated NLP metrics (BERT and FRUGAL scores) with human evaluations. This provides a more holistic assessment of ReqBrain's performance. Comparisons with ChatGPT-4 and human authored requirements are valuable.
*   **Open-Source Resources:** The authors commit to releasing the 'instruct' dataset and fine-tuned LLMs, which facilitates further research and development in this area.
*   **Practical Application:** ReqBrain has potential practical applications for streamlining the requirements elicitation and specification process, especially for situations where tacit knowledge is a barrier. It provides a starting point to improve requirements engineering.

However, the paper has some limitations:

*   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses primarily on the early phases of requirements engineering (elicitation and specification) and doesn't address other critical aspects such as validation or change management.
*   **Domain Specificity:** The success of ReqBrain likely depends on the specific domain and the quality of the training data. It is unclear how well the model generalizes to significantly different domains or types of systems without additional fine-tuning.
*   **Small Sample Size:** Although the authors state their sample size is two-to-three times larger than that from the power analysis, the power analysis recommends a sample size of 32-64. The four human evaluators represent a limited cross-section of the domain.
*   **Incremental Contribution:** While the paper demonstrates the effectiveness of fine-tuning, the gains over a general-purpose LLM like ChatGPT-4 (without fine-tuning) may not be transformative. The metrics do not provide a dramatic difference between ReqBrain and ChatGPT-4.
*   **Lack of Usability Study:** While they note they are conducting an industrial case study in terms of usability, the absence of those results currently weakens the practical application.

The paper represents a solid, incremental contribution to the field. It is significant to advance AI-assisted requirements, but more validation in a practical application is needed to increase the impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Why Diffusion Models Don't Memorize: The Role of Implicit Dynamical Regularization in Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17638v1)
- **Authors**: Tony Bonnaire, Raphaël Urfin, Giulio Biroli, Marc Mézard
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have achieved remarkable success across a wide range of generative tasks. A key challenge is understanding the mechanisms that prevent their memorization of training data and allow generalization. In this work, we investigate the role of the training dynamics in the transition from generalization to memorization. Through extensive experiments and theoretical analysis, we identify two distinct timescales: an early time $\tau_\mathrm{gen}$ at which models begin to generate high-quality samples, and a later time $\tau_\mathrm{mem}$ beyond which memorization emerges. Crucially, we find that $\tau_\mathrm{mem}$ increases linearly with the training set size $n$, while $\tau_\mathrm{gen}$ remains constant. This creates a growing window of training times with $n$ where models generalize effectively, despite showing strong memorization if training continues beyond it. It is only when $n$ becomes larger than a model-dependent threshold that overfitting disappears at infinite training times. These findings reveal a form of implicit dynamical regularization in the training dynamics, which allow to avoid memorization even in highly overparameterized settings. Our results are supported by numerical experiments with standard U-Net architectures on realistic and synthetic datasets, and by a theoretical analysis using a tractable random features model studied in the high-dimensional limit.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Why Diffusion Models Don't Memorize: The Role of Implicit Dynamical Regularization in Training" investigates the mechanisms that prevent diffusion models (DMs) from simply memorizing training data, allowing them to generalize.  Through numerical experiments on both realistic (CelebA) and synthetic datasets, combined with theoretical analysis using a random feature model, the authors identify two key timescales:  Tgen (time to begin generating high-quality samples) and Tmem (time beyond which memorization emerges). They find that Tmem increases linearly with the size of the training set (n), while Tgen remains relatively constant, creating a generalization window (T∈ [Tgen, Tmem]).  The paper argues that this widening window is due to implicit dynamical regularization, where the training dynamics favor smooth interpolations of the population score rather than overfitting to the empirical score, even in overparameterized regimes. The paper's theoretical model links these timescales to the spectrum of a random feature correlation matrix, providing an analytical understanding of the observed phenomenon.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by explicitly highlighting and dissecting the role of *training dynamics* in preventing memorization in DMs. While other works have examined architectural biases, capacity constraints, and learning rate effects, this work directly links the *temporal evolution* of the learned score function to the generalization performance, explaining the memorization-generalization phase transition. Identifying and quantifying the timescales Tgen and Tmem, and demonstrating their relationship to dataset size and model capacity, provides new insights. The use of a tractable random features model to theoretically validate these observations strengthens the paper's claims.
* **Significance:** Understanding generalization in DMs is a crucial problem. This paper tackles this question head-on and provides a clear, experimentally validated argument about the role of implicit dynamical regularization. The findings have implications for training strategies – early stopping within the generalization window to avoid memorization becomes a key takeaway.  The theoretical framework offers a basis for further exploration of generalization in score-based generative models.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive approach:** Combines numerical experiments, theoretical analysis, and connections to existing literature.
    * **Clear identification of key timescales:** Tgen and Tmem provide useful metrics for understanding the generalization behavior of DMs.
    * **Strong experimental validation:** Results on both CelebA and synthetic data support the theoretical model.
    * **Tractable theoretical model:**  The use of random feature models allows for an analytical understanding of the training dynamics, linking it to spectral properties.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Random Feature Model limitations:** While useful for theoretical analysis, the RF model is a simplified representation of real-world DMs.  The extent to which the specific relationships observed in the RF model (e.g., the exact functional dependence of Tgen and Tmem on model parameters) fully translate to more complex architectures (e.g., U-Nets) is a point that requires some caution.
    * **Focus on SGD:** The primary focus is on SGD as the optimizer.  While the supplementary materials show that similar trends appear with Adam, further investigation of the interplay between different optimizers and the identified timescales would be valuable.
    * **Limited Scope of Theoretical Results:** While the RFNN model provides analytical tractability, the expressiveness of RFNNs is limited, requiring a leap to draw parallels with findings from more complex architectures such as U-Nets.
* **Potential Impact:** The paper will likely influence future research on generalization in DMs, shifting the focus toward the dynamics of training and the interplay between different regularization mechanisms.  The concept of a "generalization window" could become a widely adopted framework for understanding and improving DM training. The work also creates a more solid understanding of what factors drive memorization in DMs, leading towards more effective ways to prevent this from occurring.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a significant and novel contribution to the understanding of generalization in diffusion models. It clearly identifies and quantifies the importance of implicit dynamical regularization through well-designed experiments and insightful theoretical analysis. While the reliance on a simplified random feature model is a limitation, it provides a valuable analytical perspective. The work offers actionable insights for training DMs and a solid foundation for future research. Therefore, a score of 7 is justified, reflecting a substantial contribution with clear potential to influence the field, but acknowledging the limitations of the theoretical framework and the need for further validation with more complex architectures and optimizers.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### PreMoe: Lightening MoEs on Constrained Memory by Expert Pruning and Retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17639v1)
- **Authors**: Zehua Pei, Ying Zhang, Hui-Ling Zhen, Xianzhi Yu, Wulong Liu, Sinno Jialin Pan, Mingxuan Yuan, Bei Yu
- **Abstract**: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures enable scaling large language models (LLMs) to vast parameter counts without a proportional rise in computational costs. However, the significant memory demands of large MoE models hinder their deployment across various computational environments, from cloud servers to consumer devices. This study first demonstrates pronounced task-specific specialization in expert activation patterns within MoE layers. Building on this, we introduce PreMoe, a novel framework that enables efficient deployment of massive MoE models in memory-constrained environments. PreMoe features two main components: probabilistic expert pruning (PEP) and task-adaptive expert retrieval (TAER). PEP employs a new metric, the task-conditioned expected selection score (TCESS), derived from router logits to quantify expert importance for specific tasks, thereby identifying a minimal set of critical experts. TAER leverages these task-specific expert importance profiles for efficient inference. It pre-computes and stores compact expert patterns for diverse tasks. When a user query is received, TAER rapidly identifies the most relevant stored task pattern and reconstructs the model by loading only the small subset of experts crucial for that task. This approach dramatically reduces the memory footprint across all deployment scenarios. DeepSeek-R1 671B maintains 97.2\% accuracy on MATH500 when pruned to 8/128 configuration (50\% expert reduction), and still achieves 72.0\% with aggressive 8/32 pruning (87.5\% expert reduction). Pangu-Ultra-MoE 718B achieves 97.15\% on MATH500 and 81.3\% on AIME24 with 8/128 pruning, while even more aggressive pruning to 4/64 (390GB memory) preserves 96.95\% accuracy on MATH500. We make our code publicly available at https://github.com/JarvisPei/PreMoe.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PreMoe: Lightening MoEs on Constrained Memory by Expert Pruning and Retrieval":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PreMoe, a framework designed to reduce the memory footprint of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models for deployment in memory-constrained environments. PreMoe leverages the observation that experts in MoE layers exhibit task-specific specialization.  The framework comprises two key components: Probabilistic Expert Pruning (PEP), which quantifies expert importance for specific tasks using a novel Task-Conditioned Expected Selection Score (TCESS), and Task-Adaptive Expert Retrieval (TAER), which pre-computes and stores compact expert patterns for diverse tasks, enabling efficient inference by loading only the relevant subset of experts. Experiments on multiple MoE architectures (DeepSeek-R1, Pangu-Ultra-MoE, and Pangu-Pro-MoE) demonstrate the effectiveness of PreMoe in maintaining performance while significantly reducing memory requirements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific combination of techniques: task-conditioned expert pruning *and* task-adaptive retrieval. While pruning and dynamic loading of model components are not entirely new concepts, the use of the proposed TCESS metric and the integration of expert pruning and retrieval with strong empirical evidence provides a novel approach. CMoE is a related work in terms of activating selective parameters, which the authors have also mentioned.

*   **Significance:** The practical significance of PreMoe is considerable. Large MoE models are computationally expensive, hindering their deployment in resource-constrained environments. PreMoe offers a pathway to deploy these models on devices with limited memory, potentially democratizing access to advanced AI capabilities. The empirical results demonstrating minimal performance degradation despite significant memory reduction strengthen this argument. It also improves the efficiency of the training process.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across multiple MoE architectures and datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of PreMoe in various settings. The consistent performance retention even with aggressive pruning is particularly compelling.
    *   **Principled Approach:**  The TCESS metric provides a principled and interpretable way to quantify expert importance, grounding the pruning process in the router's probabilistic outputs.
    *   **Task-Adaptive Retrieval:** The task-adaptive expert retrieval mechanism offers an efficient solution for on-demand loading of task-specific experts, reducing computational overhead at inference time.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed framework and its components.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Task Patterns:** The effectiveness of TAER relies on the representativeness of the pre-computed task patterns. The paper acknowledges that novel queries significantly different from stored tasks might lead to suboptimal performance. The mechanism to adapt to queries not represented by stored patterns needs to be further discussed.
    *   **L2 Distance Matching:** The choice of L2 distance for matching query TCESS patterns to stored patterns might not always capture the full semantic similarity between tasks.
    *   **I/O Overhead:** While PreMoe significantly reduces memory footprint, the overhead of loading expert subsets from storage during inference could still be a bottleneck for latency-sensitive applications.
    *   **Synthetic data:** The authors used both the question and the corresponding reasoning output to collect the expert patterns. It is ideal to collect and generate real-world reasoning output of the full models.

*   **Potential Influence:**  PreMoe has the potential to influence research in several areas:

    *   **Efficient MoE Deployment:**  The framework can serve as a foundation for developing more efficient and deployable MoE models.
    *   **Dynamic Model Adaptation:**  The task-adaptive expert retrieval mechanism can inspire research on dynamic model adaptation techniques.
    *   **Interpretability of MoE Models:**  The task-specific expert specialization analysis can contribute to a better understanding of the inner workings of MoE models.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I would assign this paper a score of **7**.  The paper presents a novel combination of techniques for efficient MoE deployment with compelling empirical results. The limitations, particularly the reliance on pre-computed task patterns and potential I/O overhead, prevent a higher score. However, the potential for influencing future research in efficient model deployment and dynamic adaptation justifies a strong positive evaluation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Understanding Pre-training and Fine-tuning from Loss Landscape Perspectives
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17646v1)
- **Authors**: Huanran Chen, Yinpeng Dong, Zeming Wei, Yao Huang, Yichi Zhang, Hang Su, Jun Zhu
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have revealed that the loss landscape of large language models resembles a basin, within which the models perform nearly identically, and outside of which they lose all their capabilities. In this work, we conduct further studies on the loss landscape of large language models. We discover that pre-training creates a "basic capability" basin, and subsequent fine-tuning creates "specific capability" basins (e.g., math, safety, coding) within the basic capability basin. We further investigate two types of loss landscapes: the most-case landscape (i.e., the landscape along most directions) and the worst-case landscape (i.e., the landscape along the worst direction). We argue that as long as benign fine-tuning remains within the most-case basin, it will not compromise previous capabilities. Similarly, any fine-tuning (including the adversarial one) that stays within the worst-case basin would not compromise previous capabilities. Finally, we theoretically demonstrate that the size of the most-case basin can bound the size of the worst-case basin and the robustness with respect to input perturbations. We also show that, due to the over-parameterization property of current large language models, one can easily enlarge the basins by five times.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the loss landscape of large language models (LLMs) to understand pre-training and fine-tuning dynamics, specifically the alignment brittleness phenomenon. The authors propose that pre-training creates a "basic capability" basin in the loss landscape, while fine-tuning creates "specific capability" basins within the initial one. They analyze "most-case" and "worst-case" loss landscapes, arguing that benign fine-tuning staying within the "most-case" basin preserves prior capabilities, while adversarial fine-tuning that remains within the "worst-case" basin wouldn't compromise prior capabilities. The paper includes theoretical arguments relating the size of the basins to robustness against perturbations and demonstrates, empirically, that the basin sizes can be enlarged.  They discuss the vulnerability of LLMs to input-space jailbreaking in relation to the worst-case landscape. Finally, they propose a Gaussian-augmented optimizer to enlarge these basins.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Perspective:**  The paper offers a loss landscape perspective on the alignment brittleness of LLMs, providing a geometric interpretation for phenomena like catastrophic forgetting and jailbreaking. This is a valuable contribution as it allows for more intuitive understanding and potential avenues for mitigation.
    *   **Clear Conceptual Framework:** The definitions of "most-case" and "worst-case" landscapes are useful abstractions for analyzing model behavior during fine-tuning.
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:**  The paper attempts to provide theoretical underpinnings for observed empirical phenomena, linking basin size to robustness guarantees.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes experiments visualizing loss landscapes for different LLMs (Llama, Qwen, Mistral) under different fine-tuning conditions, which supports the theoretical claims. The addition of an algorithm to improve basin size by using the Gaussian Augmented Optimizer also supports their arguments.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper identifies a crucial consideration in LLM alignment: maintaining parameters within specific capability basins during fine-tuning to avoid catastrophic forgetting. The proposed basin-enlarging optimization method could potentially lead to more robust models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplifying Assumptions:**  The "most-case" landscape assumption relies on the empirical observation that random directions yield similar results.  This requires more rigorous justification or exploration of scenarios where this assumption breaks down.
    *   **Theoretical Looseness:** The theoretical bounds, while interesting, are likely loose in practice.  The connection between parameter-space robustness and input-space robustness (jailbreaking) is presented with some strong assumptions (onto embedding layers) that may not fully hold or be relevant in all contexts.
    *   **Limited Experimental Scope:** The empirical evaluation, while demonstrating the concepts, is limited in scale. The basin-enlarging experiments use a relatively small model (Qwen2.5-0.5B) and further validation with larger models is required.
    *   **Lack of Novelty of Experimental Settings:** The settings for different configurations were copied from existing works. This does not provide any novel experimental results or insights into the models and how to improve them.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The effectiveness of theoretical guarantees is low. The proposed improvement strategy is constrained to offline and small batch sizes, creating less space for practical implication.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The novelty lies primarily in applying the loss landscape perspective, to the problem of alignment brittleness in LLMs. While loss landscape analysis is not new to machine learning, its application to understanding and addressing the specific challenges of LLM alignment is a valuable contribution. However, the experimental results lack novelties, further lowering the score.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a conceptual contribution by framing alignment brittleness in terms of the loss landscape geometry. The theoretical analysis, while somewhat limited in scope, provides a foundation for understanding and potentially mitigating these issues. The empirical results support the core ideas, but more extensive validation is needed.

Score: 6

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable, novel perspective on a significant problem (alignment brittleness). However, the theoretical grounding could be stronger and the empirical validation more extensive. The practical implications of the basin enlargement algorithm need to be investigated more extensively. The score reflects a solid contribution with room for improvement in the theoretical and empirical rigor.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Does Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Really Reduce Harmfulness from Jailbreaking?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17650v1)
- **Authors**: Chengda Lu, Xiaoyu Fan, Yu Huang, Rongwu Xu, Jijie Li, Wei Xu
- **Abstract**: Jailbreak attacks have been observed to largely fail against recent reasoning models enhanced by Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. However, the underlying mechanism remains underexplored, and relying solely on reasoning capacity may raise security concerns. In this paper, we try to answer the question: Does CoT reasoning really reduce harmfulness from jailbreaking? Through rigorous theoretical analysis, we demonstrate that CoT reasoning has dual effects on jailbreaking harmfulness. Based on the theoretical insights, we propose a novel jailbreak method, FicDetail, whose practical performance validates our theoretical findings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Does Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Really Reduce Harmfulness from Jailbreaking?":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning truly reduces the harmfulness of jailbreaking attacks on Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors argue that while CoT reasoning might make LLMs more resistant to *risk* (i.e., jailbreak success), it can simultaneously increase *harmfulness* when a jailbreak is successful, because CoT leads to more detailed and actionable harmful outputs. The paper provides a theoretical framework defining harmfulness as a function of semantic topic and level of detail.  It proposes a novel jailbreaking method, FICDETAIL, designed to bypass CoT defenses by gradually enriching a fictional story with harmful details.  Empirical evaluations using FICDETAIL demonstrate that CoT reasoning models can still be successfully jailbroken and that the resulting outputs can be more harmful. The paper also introduces two new metrics, Helpful Rate (HPR) and Detailed Context Comparing Harmfulness (DCCH), designed to better measure harmfulness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing an Important Question:** The paper tackles a crucial question regarding the security of LLMs, moving beyond simple risk assessments to consider the nature and impact of harmful outputs.
*   **Theoretical Framework:** The development of a formal harmfulness model is a valuable contribution, providing a more nuanced way to think about LLM safety. This framework helps in reasoning about the trade-offs inherent in CoT and jailbreaking.
*   **Novel Jailbreak Method:** The FICDETAIL method is innovative and effective, demonstrating that CoT defenses are not foolproof. The gradual enrichment approach is a clever way to evade alignment mechanisms.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The empirical results support the theoretical analysis, showing that CoT models can indeed be jailbroken and generate more harmful outputs. The extensive experiments across multiple models and attacks are a strong point.
*   **New Metrics:** Introducing HPR and DCCH is a positive step towards more comprehensive evaluation of LLM safety. DCCH is a valuable comparative metric, allowing for a more direct assessment of harmfulness.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Assumptions:** The assumption that the LLM's induced distribution and total number of topics remains unchanged throughout CoT iterations is a strong one.  While justified by the authors, it might not hold in all real-world scenarios, especially with more complex prompts or LLMs.
*   **Subjectivity in Harmfulness:** While DCCH attempts to address this, assessing harmfulness always contains an element of subjectivity. The LLM's used as judges can have their own biases, and there's always room for debate about the "harmfulness" of specific outputs.
*   **Limited Generalizability of FICDETAIL:** FICDETAIL is specific to a "fictional story" approach. It is unclear how well it can generalize to all types of jailbreak contexts, thus limiting the potential broader application.
*   **Evaluation Dataset Limitedness:** While AdvBench has a vast set of potentially harmful instructions, it is questionable whether this single, albeit substantial, dataset could fully describe the universe of jailbreaking.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper provides a significant contribution by:

*   **Identifying a Dual Effect of CoT:** Revealing that CoT has both a risk-reducing and a harmfulness-increasing effect is a novel and important finding.
*   **Proposing a new way to evaluate Harmfulness**: By introducing the importance of context detail, the paper sets an important precedent in further discussions of evaluating LLM jailbreaks.

**Overall Impression:**

The paper is well-written, logically sound, and supported by thorough experimental results. It makes a compelling case that simply relying on CoT reasoning for LLM safety is insufficient and potentially dangerous. While some assumptions and limitations exist, the paper provides valuable insights and lays the foundation for future research in this area.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

A score of 8 reflects the paper's significant contribution to understanding the complexities of LLM safety. The identification of the dual effect of CoT is a novel and important finding, and the FICDETAIL method provides a practical demonstration of its limitations.  While the paper has some limitations in terms of assumptions and generalizability, its theoretical framework, empirical validation, and new metrics make it a substantial contribution to the field. This paper is a solid foundation and a needed wake-up call in LLM safety research.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10
