### Rethinking the Sampling Criteria in Reinforcement Learning for LLM Reasoning: A Competence-Difficulty Alignment Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17652v1)
- **Authors**: Deyang Kong, Qi Guo, Xiangyu Xi, Wei Wang, Jingang Wang, Xunliang Cai, Shikun Zhang, Wei Ye
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning exhibits potential in enhancing the reasoning abilities of large language models, yet it is hard to scale for the low sample efficiency during the rollout phase. Existing methods attempt to improve efficiency by scheduling problems based on problem difficulties. However, these approaches suffer from unstable and biased estimations of problem difficulty and fail to capture the alignment between model competence and problem difficulty in RL training, leading to suboptimal results. To tackle these limitations, this paper introduces \textbf{C}ompetence-\textbf{D}ifficulty \textbf{A}lignment \textbf{S}ampling (\textbf{CDAS}), which enables accurate and stable estimation of problem difficulties by aggregating historical performance discrepancies of problems. Then the model competence is quantified to adaptively select problems whose difficulty is in alignment with the model's current competence using a fixed-point system. Experimental results across a range of challenging mathematical benchmarks show that CDAS achieves great improvements in both accuracy and efficiency. CDAS attains the highest average accuracy against baselines and exhibits significant speed advantages compared to Dynamic Sampling, a competitive strategy in DAPO, which is \textbf{2.33} times slower than CDAS.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Rethinking the Sampling Criteria in Reinforcement Learning for LLM Reasoning: A Competence-Difficulty Alignment Perspective" addresses the problem of sample inefficiency in Reinforcement Learning (RL) for training Large Language Models (LLMs) on reasoning tasks.  The authors argue that existing sampling strategies, often based on problem difficulty, suffer from unstable difficulty estimations and a failure to align the difficulty of the sampled problems with the model's current competence. To overcome these limitations, they propose Competence-Difficulty Alignment Sampling (CDAS). CDAS estimates problem difficulty by aggregating historical performance discrepancies and quantifies model competence to adaptively select problems whose difficulty is aligned with the model's current competence. This alignment is achieved through a fixed-point system. Experiments on mathematical benchmarks show that CDAS achieves higher accuracy and efficiency compared to baselines, including Dynamic Sampling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strength:** The core idea of explicitly aligning problem difficulty with model competence is a valuable contribution. Existing methods often focus solely on difficulty estimation without considering the model's ability to handle those difficulties at a particular stage of training. The use of historical performance discrepancy for difficulty estimation is also a good approach to provide more stable estimates than single-step pass rates.
    *   **Weakness:** The specific implementation, while effective, relies on a combination of existing techniques. The use of a fixed-point system, while providing theoretical guarantees, isn't necessarily revolutionary in itself. While the combination is novel and tailored to this problem, each individual building block has been explored to some extent.

*   **Significance and Impact:**

    *   **Strength:** The paper provides a practical solution to a significant bottleneck in scaling RL for LLM reasoning. Improving sample efficiency directly translates to reduced training costs and faster development cycles. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the improvements in both accuracy and efficiency, suggesting a real-world impact.
    *   **Weakness:** The experiments primarily focus on mathematical reasoning tasks using Qwen2.5 models. While the results are strong in this domain, it is important to note that this work is still limited to mathematical tasks, and its generalizability to other LLM tasks, particularly those with open-ended outputs or requiring complex natural language understanding, remains an open question. Moreover, the reliance on a rule-based reward function might also limit its application in more complex settings. The extent to which CDAS may be affected by reward hacking also remains unclear.

*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed approach and experimental setup. The ablation studies and analysis provide valuable insights into the method's behavior and contribute to the overall understanding.

*   **Rigor:** The experimental methodology appears sound, with clear comparisons to relevant baselines.  The inclusion of ablation studies strengthens the argument for the effectiveness of the individual components of CDAS. The fixed-point system has theoretical guarantees of convergence.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by addressing a critical challenge in scaling RL for LLM reasoning: sample inefficiency.  The Competence-Difficulty Alignment Sampling (CDAS) approach is well-motivated, and the experimental results convincingly demonstrate its effectiveness in mathematical reasoning tasks. While the implementation relies on a combination of existing techniques and the experiments are limited to a specific model and domain, the paper introduces a novel perspective and provides a practical solution with real-world implications.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** A score of 7 reflects a solid and valuable contribution. The paper addresses a relevant problem, proposes a novel approach, and presents strong experimental results within a limited domain. The novelty is present in the combination of ideas and the specific tailoring to the RL-for-LLM problem, but not entirely groundbreaking. The potential impact on the field is tangible, but wider generalizability needs to be confirmed through further research.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### GeoGramBench: Benchmarking the Geometric Program Reasoning in Modern LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17653v1)
- **Authors**: Shixian Luo, Zezhou Zhu, Yu Yuan, Yuncheng Yang, Lianlei Shan, Yong Wu
- **Abstract**: Geometric spatial reasoning forms the foundation of many applications in artificial intelligence, yet the ability of large language models (LLMs) to operate over geometric spatial information expressed in procedural code remains underexplored. In this paper, we address this gap by formalizing the Program-to-Geometry task, which challenges models to translate programmatic drawing code into accurate and abstract geometric reasoning. To evaluate this capability, we present GeoGramBench, a benchmark of 500 carefully refined problems organized by a tailored three-level taxonomy that considers geometric complexity rather than traditional mathematical reasoning complexity. Our comprehensive evaluation of 17 frontier LLMs reveals consistent and pronounced deficiencies: even the most advanced models achieve less than 50% accuracy at the highest abstraction level. These results highlight the unique challenges posed by program-driven spatial reasoning and establish GeoGramBench as a valuable resource for advancing research in symbolic-to-spatial geometric reasoning. Project page: https://github.com/LiAuto-DSR/GeoGramBench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GeoGramBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform geometric reasoning based on procedural code.  The authors formalize the "Program-to-Geometry" task, where models must interpret programmatic drawing code (specifically Asymptote) and reason about the resulting geometric shapes and relationships to solve problems. GeoGramBench consists of 500 problems organized into a three-level taxonomy: Primitive Recognition, Local Relation Composition, and Global Abstract Integration, categorized by geometric complexity rather than traditional mathematical reasoning complexity.  The paper evaluates 17 different LLMs on the benchmark, finding significant performance deficiencies, even in the most advanced models, particularly at higher abstraction levels. The authors also analyze model behaviors, propose a multi-stage internal reasoning process hypothesis, and discuss limitations and future directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the *explicit* formalization and benchmarking of the Program-to-Geometry task using procedural code. While previous benchmarks touched upon geometric reasoning and some included snippets of drawing code, GeoGramBench is specifically designed to isolate and assess this ability. The taxonomy is also a novel attempt to classify problems based on geometric complexity *within* the code interpretation context, rather than mathematical reasoning difficulty.
* **Significance:** The paper highlights a significant gap in LLMs' capabilities.  The fact that even the best models struggle on more complex geometric problems presented through procedural code suggests a fundamental limitation in their ability to translate symbolic representations into spatial understanding. The benchmark provides a valuable tool for researchers to focus on this specific weakness and develop models that can more effectively bridge the gap between code and geometric reasoning.  The analysis of model behaviors and the hypothesized internal reasoning process are also significant contributions, offering insights into where models are failing.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined task and benchmark:** The paper clearly defines the Program-to-Geometry task and provides a comprehensive benchmark with careful problem selection and annotation. The benchmark is publicly available to promote further research in the area.
    *   **Detailed analysis:** The paper goes beyond simply reporting accuracy scores and provides a more in-depth analysis of model behaviors, including examples of model reasoning processes and common failure modes.
    *   **Relevant Taxonomy:** The taxonomy considers geometric complexity with code understanding, providing valuable means to dissect LLM code to geometry reasoning abilities.
    *   **Addressing Leakage:** The authors specifically address and mitigate a crucial problem inherent in this domain: answer leakage within the code.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Code Domain:** While Asymptote is appropriate, the benchmark is primarily reliant on it. Generalizing to other procedural description languages will solidify the observations.
    *   **Lack of Error Analysis on a Per-Problem Basis:** While the global performance is presented for each category, a deeper, granular error analysis of specific problem instances across different models is lacking.  Identifying *which* types of geometry problems in the code-driven setting are the most challenging *across* models would provide even more targeted directions for future research.

* **Potential Influence:**  GeoGramBench has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating and improving LLMs' ability to reason about geometry expressed through code. It could stimulate research into new model architectures, training techniques, and reasoning strategies specifically designed to address the challenges highlighted in the paper.

* **Rigorous Rationale of Score:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM evaluation by introducing a new benchmark focused on program-driven geometric reasoning. The comprehensive analysis and insightful findings demonstrate the challenges LLMs face in this domain.  While the benchmark's scope could be expanded to include other procedural drawing languages and the analysis could be made more granular by exploring specific problem instances, the paper provides a solid foundation for future research. It identifies a critical gap in current LLM capabilities, offers a valuable tool for addressing it, and provides a useful framework for understanding model behavior.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### EVADE: Multimodal Benchmark for Evasive Content Detection in E-Commerce Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17654v1)
- **Authors**: Ancheng Xu, Zhihao Yang, Jingpeng Li, Guanghu Yuan, Longze Chen, Liang Yan, Jiehui Zhou, Zhen Qin, Hengyun Chang, Hamid Alinejad-Rokny, Bo Zheng, Min Yang
- **Abstract**: E-commerce platforms increasingly rely on Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to detect illicit or misleading product content. However, these models remain vulnerable to evasive content: inputs (text or images) that superficially comply with platform policies while covertly conveying prohibited claims. Unlike traditional adversarial attacks that induce overt failures, evasive content exploits ambiguity and context, making it far harder to detect. Existing robustness benchmarks provide little guidance for this demanding, real-world challenge. We introduce EVADE, the first expert-curated, Chinese, multimodal benchmark specifically designed to evaluate foundation models on evasive content detection in e-commerce. The dataset contains 2,833 annotated text samples and 13,961 images spanning six demanding product categories, including body shaping, height growth, and health supplements. Two complementary tasks assess distinct capabilities: Single-Violation, which probes fine-grained reasoning under short prompts, and All-in-One, which tests long-context reasoning by merging overlapping policy rules into unified instructions. Notably, the All-in-One setting significantly narrows the performance gap between partial and full-match accuracy, suggesting that clearer rule definitions improve alignment between human and model judgment. We benchmark 26 mainstream LLMs and VLMs and observe substantial performance gaps: even state-of-the-art models frequently misclassify evasive samples. By releasing EVADE and strong baselines, we provide the first rigorous standard for evaluating evasive-content detection, expose fundamental limitations in current multimodal reasoning, and lay the groundwork for safer and more transparent content moderation systems in e-commerce. The dataset is publicly available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/koenshen/EVADE-Bench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "EVADE: Multimodal Benchmark for Evasive Content Detection in E-Commerce Applications":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EVADE, a new Chinese multimodal benchmark dataset designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to detect evasive content in e-commerce applications. Evasive content refers to inputs (text and images) that appear to comply with platform policies superficially but covertly convey prohibited claims. The dataset contains 2,833 annotated text samples and 13,961 images across six product categories (body shaping, height growth, health supplements, etc.).  The paper defines two tasks, Single-Violation and All-in-One, that assess distinct capabilities in fine-grained reasoning and long-context understanding. The authors benchmark 26 LLMs and VLMs and demonstrate significant performance gaps, even in state-of-the-art models, highlighting the limitations of current models in detecting evasive content. The dataset and baselines are publicly released.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel and timely benchmark dataset. While research exists on adversarial attacks and robustness in various NLP and vision tasks, the specific problem of *evasive content detection* in e-commerce, particularly in Chinese, is relatively underexplored. The focus on multimodal content and realistic e-commerce scenarios enhances the practical relevance of the benchmark. The dataset aims to tackle the specific nuances of adversarial attacks on e-commerce platforms that take the form of subtle messaging and rule evasion rather than outright violations. This is a meaningful step forward.
*   **Significance:** The benchmark addresses a critical real-world problem with substantial economic and societal impact. The proliferation of misleading or deceptive product claims on e-commerce platforms can lead to consumer harm, regulatory fines, and reputational damage. The EVADE dataset and the accompanying evaluation provide a valuable resource for researchers to develop and evaluate more robust content moderation systems. The analysis of model performance highlights crucial limitations in current multimodal reasoning capabilities and provides insights into areas for improvement, particularly around contextual understanding, reasoning fidelity, and visual grounding. The finding that clearer category boundaries significantly improve model consistency is a valuable contribution to benchmark design.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Expert-curated and High-Quality Dataset:** The dataset is annotated by domain experts, ensuring accuracy and reliability. The iterative annotation-refinement process enhances label consistency.
    *   **Well-Defined Tasks:** The two tasks, Single-Violation and All-in-One, effectively probe distinct capabilities related to fine-grained reasoning and long-context understanding.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper benchmarks a wide range of LLMs and VLMs, providing a comprehensive baseline for future research.
    *   **Public Availability:** The dataset and baselines are publicly available, facilitating further research and development in this area.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The benchmark focuses on a specific set of product categories and a Chinese language context. While this specificity allows for in-depth analysis, it might limit the generalizability of the findings to other languages or product domains. The authors themselves acknowledge the limitation in focusing on only six categories. Expanding to other categories with different evasive techniques would increase the benchmark's utility.
    *   **Potential for Benchmark Saturation:** As with all benchmarks, there's the potential for models to overfit to EVADE in the future, reducing its effectiveness as a measure of generalization. This will necessitate ongoing updates and expansions to the dataset.

*   **Justification:** EVADE represents a significant step towards addressing a critical real-world problem. The focus on evasive content detection, expert curation, and multimodal challenges makes it a valuable contribution. The comprehensive evaluation of LLMs and VLMs and the identification of key limitations offer clear directions for future research. However, the limitations in scope and potential for saturation warrant a slight reduction in the score.

### Too Consistent to Detect: A Study of Self-Consistent Errors in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17656v1)
- **Authors**: Hexiang Tan, Fei Sun, Sha Liu, Du Su, Qi Cao, Xin Chen, Jingang Wang, Xunliang Cai, Yuanzhuo Wang, Huawei Shen, Xueqi Cheng
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) often generate plausible but incorrect content, error detection has become increasingly critical to ensure truthfulness. However, existing detection methods often overlook a critical problem we term as self-consistent error, where LLMs repeatly generate the same incorrect response across multiple stochastic samples. This work formally defines self-consistent errors and evaluates mainstream detection methods on them. Our investigation reveals two key findings: (1) Unlike inconsistent errors, whose frequency diminishes significantly as LLM scale increases, the frequency of self-consistent errors remains stable or even increases. (2) All four types of detection methshods significantly struggle to detect self-consistent errors. These findings reveal critical limitations in current detection methods and underscore the need for improved methods. Motivated by the observation that self-consistent errors often differ across LLMs, we propose a simple but effective cross-model probe method that fuses hidden state evidence from an external verifier LLM. Our method significantly enhances performance on self-consistent errors across three LLM families.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Too Consistent to Detect: A Study of Self-Consistent Errors in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper identifies and analyzes a critical problem in large language models (LLMs) called "self-consistent errors."  These errors occur when an LLM repeatedly produces the same *incorrect* answer across multiple stochastic samples for a given question. This is in contrast to "inconsistent errors," where the model's responses vary across samples.  The authors demonstrate that unlike inconsistent errors, self-consistent errors don't diminish with increasing model scale and remain resistant to detection by existing methods (probability-based, prompt-based, probe-based, and consistency-based). To address this, they propose a cross-model probe approach that leverages hidden states from an external "verifier" LLM to improve detection performance, exploiting the observation that self-consistent errors often vary across different LLMs.  Experiments on SciQ and TriviaQA datasets using models from the Qwen and Llama families show that the proposed cross-model probe significantly enhances the detection of self-consistent errors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in formally defining and characterizing the problem of "self-consistent errors." While the *existence* of such errors has been acknowledged indirectly, this paper is among the first to clearly define, measure their prevalence, and thoroughly evaluate existing error detection methods against them. The proposed cross-model probe isn't entirely new in concept (using external models for verification), but it's a well-motivated application in the context of self-consistent errors, leveraging their model-specific nature.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the critical insights it provides about the limitations of current LLM error detection techniques. The finding that existing methods, especially consistency-based ones, struggle with self-consistent errors raises serious concerns about their reliability in real-world applications. By demonstrating that these errors persist even as LLM scale increases, the work highlights a crucial area for future research. The proposed cross-model probe offers a practical and effective solution to this specific issue.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The concept of self-consistent errors is well-defined and easy to understand.
    *   **Comprehensive Empirical Analysis:** The paper thoroughly evaluates a variety of error detection methods on multiple datasets and LLM families.
    *   **Well-Motivated Solution:** The cross-model probe is grounded in a sound observation about the model-specific nature of self-consistent errors.
    *   **Effective Improvement:** The experimental results clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Solutions:** While the cross-model probe is effective, it focuses primarily on a specific type of error. The paper doesn't explore more general techniques to combat self-consistent errors at their root (e.g., improving training data, model architectures).
    *   **Dependency on a Verifier LLM:** The approach introduces a dependency on a verifier LLM, which adds computational overhead and raises questions about the verifier's own accuracy and biases. How to select the verifier model isn't entirely clear or rigorously justified other than empirical testing.
    *   **Somewhat Incremental:** While novel in its problem definition and characterization, the *technical solution* is relatively incremental, building on existing probing techniques. It's a clever *application* rather than a fundamentally new methodology.
    *   **Generalizability to other domains:** The empirical analysis focused on TriviaQA and SciQ datasets. While they are representative, generalizability to other domains (e.g., creative writing, coding, etc.) is uncertain.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM error detection by shifting the focus to more robust methods that can handle self-consistent errors. It also provides a practical solution that can be adopted by practitioners in the field. It opens up an interesting direction: using model diversity as a means of identifying problematic areas in the knowledge of LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by identifying and formally defining a previously underappreciated problem in LLM error detection (self-consistent errors). Its comprehensive empirical analysis reveals the limitations of existing methods, and the proposed cross-model probe provides a practical and effective solution. While the technical solution is somewhat incremental, the paper's critical insights and potential influence on the field warrant a high score. The weaknesses relating to scope and dependency on an external LLM prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Plan-R1: Safe and Feasible Trajectory Planning as Language Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17659v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaolong Tang, Meina Kan, Shiguang Shan, Xilin Chen
- **Abstract**: Safe and feasible trajectory planning is essential for real-world autonomous driving systems. However, existing learning-based planning methods often rely on expert demonstrations, which not only lack explicit safety awareness but also risk inheriting unsafe behaviors such as speeding from suboptimal human driving data. Inspired by the success of large language models, we propose Plan-R1, a novel two-stage trajectory planning framework that formulates trajectory planning as a sequential prediction task, guided by explicit planning principles such as safety, comfort, and traffic rule compliance. In the first stage, we train an autoregressive trajectory predictor via next motion token prediction on expert data. In the second stage, we design rule-based rewards (e.g., collision avoidance, speed limits) and fine-tune the model using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a reinforcement learning strategy, to align its predictions with these planning principles. Experiments on the nuPlan benchmark demonstrate that our Plan-R1 significantly improves planning safety and feasibility, achieving state-of-the-art performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Plan-R1: Safe and Feasible Trajectory Planning as Language Modeling":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Plan-R1, a two-stage trajectory planning framework for autonomous driving. Inspired by the success of large language models (LLMs), Plan-R1 first pre-trains an autoregressive model on expert driving data to learn general driving behaviors.  It then fine-tunes the model using reinforcement learning with carefully designed, rule-based reward functions that explicitly promote safety, comfort, and rule compliance. This separates behavior learning from principle alignment. The reward functions encompass aspects like on-road adherence, collision avoidance, speed limit compliance, and driving comfort. The fine-tuning process utilizes Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO).  Experiments on the nuPlan benchmark demonstrate that Plan-R1 improves planning safety and feasibility, achieving state-of-the-art performance, especially in reactive closed-loop simulations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using a two-stage approach for trajectory planning, inspired by LLMs, is interesting. Separating behavior learning from constraint alignment is a valuable contribution. The use of interpretable, rule-based rewards, instead of relying solely on expert preferences, is also a positive step. However, individually, each component (autoregressive modeling, reinforcement learning, rule-based rewards) is not entirely novel; the novelty lies in the specific combination and application to trajectory planning with an emphasis on safety and feasibility alignment. The reliance on the prior work of  trajectory as a sequence prediction task makes this incremental work.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical limitation of many learning-based planning approaches: the lack of explicit safety and feasibility guarantees, and the potential to inherit unsafe behaviors from imperfect expert data. By explicitly incorporating planning principles through rule-based rewards and reinforcement learning, Plan-R1 provides a more robust and reliable planning solution. The state-of-the-art performance on the nuPlan benchmark validates the effectiveness of the approach and demonstrates its potential impact. The decoupling methodology could inspire future research directions in more robust and reliable planning systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The two-stage framework is well-motivated and addresses a clear need in the field.
    *   The use of interpretable, rule-based rewards promotes transparency and allows for easier debugging and improvement.
    *   The experimental results on the nuPlan benchmark are convincing, demonstrating significant improvements in safety and feasibility, particularly in reactive scenarios.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of the fine-tuning stage and the choice of KL regularization weight.
    *   The qualitative results show that Plan-R1 can avoid undesirable behaviors, like speeding, that are present in expert data.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the nuPlan benchmark is extensive, it is still a simulated environment. The real-world performance of Plan-R1 might be different.
    *   The rule-based rewards, while interpretable, still require careful design and may not be exhaustive in capturing all aspects of safety and feasibility.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the computational cost of the approach, especially the fine-tuning stage with GRPO.
    * The work follows previous research by discretizing trajectories, which can incur information loss and introduce potential issues when transforming the trajectory back.
    * The reliance on expert trajectory pre-training, although alleviated via a second-stage refinement process, may also limit the possibility of exceeding expert performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** Plan-R1 has the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable learning-based planning systems. The two-stage framework and the use of rule-based rewards could become a standard practice in the field. The success of Plan-R1 could also inspire further research on combining imitation learning and reinforcement learning for various robotic tasks.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

Plan-R1 presents a valuable contribution by addressing a key challenge in learning-based trajectory planning: ensuring safety and feasibility. The two-stage framework is novel and effective, and the use of rule-based rewards is a positive step towards more transparent and controllable planning systems. The state-of-the-art results on the nuPlan benchmark demonstrate the potential impact of the approach. However, the paper is not groundbreaking, as it builds on existing techniques in autoregressive modeling and reinforcement learning. While each individual component is not fundamentally new, the integration is significant. The reliance of nuPlan simulation and rule-based reward designs limits the work, as well as the reliance on expert trajectory pre-training. Overall, Plan-R1 represents a significant and well-executed advancement in the field, meriting a score of 7.
- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### DAM-GT: Dual Positional Encoding-Based Attention Masking Graph Transformer for Node Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17660v1)
- **Authors**: Chenyang Li, Jinsong Chen, John E. Hopcroft, Kun He
- **Abstract**: Neighborhood-aware tokenized graph Transformers have recently shown great potential for node classification tasks. Despite their effectiveness, our in-depth analysis of neighborhood tokens reveals two critical limitations in the existing paradigm. First, current neighborhood token generation methods fail to adequately capture attribute correlations within a neighborhood. Second, the conventional self-attention mechanism suffers from attention diversion when processing neighborhood tokens, where high-hop neighborhoods receive disproportionate focus, severely disrupting information interactions between the target node and its neighborhood tokens. To address these challenges, we propose DAM-GT, Dual positional encoding-based Attention Masking graph Transformer. DAM-GT introduces a novel dual positional encoding scheme that incorporates attribute-aware encoding via an attribute clustering strategy, effectively preserving node correlations in both topological and attribute spaces. In addition, DAM-GT formulates a new attention mechanism with a simple yet effective masking strategy to guide interactions between target nodes and their neighborhood tokens, overcoming the issue of attention diversion. Extensive experiments on various graphs with different homophily levels as well as different scales demonstrate that DAM-GT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods in node classification tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DAM-GT: Dual Positional Encoding-Based Attention Masking Graph Transformer for Node Classification" introduces a new graph transformer architecture for node classification. It addresses two key limitations identified in existing neighborhood-aware tokenized graph transformers: (1) the failure to adequately capture attribute correlations within a neighborhood, and (2) the attention-diverting interference issue where high-hop neighborhoods receive disproportionate attention. The proposed DAM-GT model tackles these issues with a novel dual positional encoding scheme (incorporating both topology and attribute information) and a mask-aware self-attention mechanism to guide interactions between target nodes and their neighborhoods. Experimental results on various graph datasets demonstrate that DAM-GT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents two main novelties: the dual positional encoding and the mask-aware self-attention mechanism. The dual positional encoding, combining topology-aware and attribute-aware encoding, is a sound idea, especially for heterophilous graphs. The mask-aware self-attention is a simple yet effective method to focus the attention on the target node, addressing a crucial issue. While self-attention masking isn't entirely new in the broader context of transformers, its specific application and rationale within the *graph transformer* domain seem original.

*   **Significance:** Addressing the limitations of neighborhood-aware tokenized graph transformers is an important step forward. Tokenization is a key method for improving the scalability of transformers on graphs. DAM-GT represents a significant improvement by explicitly addressing the over-attention to high-hop neighborhoods and integrating node attribute relationships, particularly for heterophilous graphs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and analyzes the limitations of existing methods.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The proposed solutions (dual positional encoding and mask-aware self-attention) are well-motivated by the problem analysis.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments demonstrate that DAM-GT consistently outperforms existing methods on a variety of datasets.
    *   **Ablation studies:** the paper provides a thorough evaluation through ablation tests and variant analyses.
    *   **Scalability:** The paper demonstrates that DAM-GT exhibits competitive computing efficiency when handling large-scale graphs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity of Positional Encoding:** While attribute-aware positional encoding is a good idea, more details on the computational overhead of k-means clustering and feature engineering might be beneficial for implementation reproducibility.
    *   The justification that the method effectively mitigates over-smoothing could have been supported by a more formal analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in graph transformers. It addresses practical limitations of existing methods and provides a new direction for designing more effective and scalable graph transformer architectures for node classification.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7.5. The paper has clear strengths in problem definition, motivation, and experimental validation. The proposed dual positional encoding and the mask-aware self-attention mechanism represent significant advances in graph transformers. DAM-GT improves the scalability and efficiency of transformers in dealing with graph-structured data. While the individual components might draw from existing ideas, the specific combination and rationale in the context of graph transformers are novel and significant. The weaknesses are relatively minor and don't detract significantly from the overall contribution. The work offers a good step towards more powerful graph transformer models for node classification.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automating Versatile Time-Series Analysis with Tiny Transformers on Embedded FPGAs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17662v1)
- **Authors**: Tianheng Ling, Chao Qian, Lukas Johannes Haßler, Gregor Schiele
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based models have shown strong performance across diverse time-series tasks, but their deployment on resource-constrained devices remains challenging due to high memory and computational demand. While prior work targeting Microcontroller Units (MCUs) has explored hardware-specific optimizations, such approaches are often task-specific and limited to 8-bit fixed-point precision. Field-Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs) offer greater flexibility, enabling fine-grained control over data precision and architecture. However, existing FPGA-based deployments of Transformers for time-series analysis typically focus on high-density platforms with manual configuration. This paper presents a unified and fully automated deployment framework for Tiny Transformers on embedded FPGAs. Our framework supports a compact encoder-only Transformer architecture across three representative time-series tasks (forecasting, classification, and anomaly detection). It combines quantization-aware training (down to 4 bits), hardware-aware hyperparameter search using Optuna, and automatic VHDL generation for seamless deployment. We evaluate our framework on six public datasets across two embedded FPGA platforms. Results show that our framework produces integer-only, task-specific Transformer accelerators achieving as low as 0.033 mJ per inference with millisecond latency on AMD Spartan-7, while also providing insights into deployment feasibility on Lattice iCE40. All source code will be released in the GitHub repository (https://github.com/Edwina1030/TinyTransformer4TS).
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper presents a unified and automated framework for deploying Tiny Transformers on embedded FPGAs for time-series analysis.  It focuses on three representative tasks: forecasting, classification, and anomaly detection. The framework combines quantization-aware training (down to 4 bits), hardware-aware hyperparameter search using Optuna, and automatic VHDL generation. The authors evaluate their framework on six public datasets across two embedded FPGA platforms (AMD Spartan-7 and Lattice iCE40), achieving low latency and energy consumption. The results show the framework can generate task-specific Transformer accelerators achieving millisecond latency and low energy consumption on the AMD Spartan-7. It also highlights the challenges of deployment on the ultra-constrained Lattice iCE40.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Automation:** The primary strength of this paper lies in its automation.  The framework eliminates the need for manual design and configuration, a significant bottleneck in FPGA-based deployments.  Automating the process makes it easier for users with limited hardware expertise to deploy Tiny Transformers on FPGAs.
    *   **Unified Approach:** The paper offers a unified approach for three distinct time-series tasks (forecasting, classification, anomaly detection). This generality is valuable, as prior work often focuses on task-specific optimizations.
    *   **Hardware-Aware Optimization:** The integration of hardware-aware optimization through quantization-aware training and Optuna-based hyperparameter search is a strong point. This allows the framework to explore the design space efficiently and generate configurations that balance accuracy and hardware feasibility.
    *   **Practical Evaluation:** The extensive evaluation on multiple datasets and two different FPGA platforms (even showing limitations on the iCE40) demonstrates the practical applicability of the framework.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a clear description of the methodology, including the model architecture, quantization process, and VHDL generation process.
    *   **Source Code Release:** The commitment to releasing the source code enhances the reproducibility and usability of the work.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Model Architecture Simplicity:** The encoder-only Transformer, while efficient, might limit performance on more complex time-series tasks that benefit from a full encoder-decoder architecture. While the design choice is justified by resource constraints, it's a simplification that affects modeling capabilities.
    *   **Limited iCE40 Success:** While the negative result of the iCE40 deployment is informative, it also highlights a limitation of the current framework.  The framework in its current state doesn't seem capable of adapting to the severely constrained environments effectively, needing further optimizations.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The core components (QAT, hyperparameter search, VHDL generation) have been explored individually in prior work. The novelty lies in the integration of these components into a unified, automated framework for *Tiny* Transformers applied to *embedded* FPGAs for *time-series*. The degree of novelty needs to be justified with an analysis comparing it to other related works.
    *   **Comparison with related work:** While the comparison to MCUs is strong, a more thorough comparison to *other* FPGA-based approaches would better establish the framework's advantages.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the growing field of TinyML by providing a practical solution for deploying Transformer-based models on resource-constrained FPGAs.
    *   The automated framework has the potential to accelerate the adoption of Tiny Transformers in various IoT applications.
    *   The hardware-aware optimization techniques can be applied to other deep learning models and hardware platforms.
    *   The paper provides valuable insights into the trade-offs between accuracy, energy efficiency, and resource utilization in FPGA-based Transformer deployments.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

*   The automation and unification of the deployment process represent a significant step forward.
*   The hardware-aware optimization strategy is valuable for achieving energy-efficient deployments.
*   The paper provides a clear and well-executed methodology with extensive experimental results.
*   However, the incremental nature of the novelty (integration rather than a groundbreaking invention) and the limitations regarding the iCE40 prevent a higher score. A detailed novelty comparison with related works is missing and more comparison would be greatly benefit this paper.
*   While the experimental results are strong, the limited success with iCE40 requires further innovations. The simpler model architecture, while practical, limits the upper bound of performance.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Dynamic Theory of Mind: Evaluating LLM Adaptation to Temporal Evolution of Human States
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17663v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Xiao, Jiashuo Wang, Qiancheng Xu, Changhe Song, Chunpu Xu, Yi Cheng, Wenjie Li, Pengfei Liu
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly participate in human-AI interactions, evaluating their Theory of Mind (ToM) capabilities - particularly their ability to track dynamic mental states - becomes crucial. While existing benchmarks assess basic ToM abilities, they predominantly focus on static snapshots of mental states, overlooking the temporal evolution that characterizes real-world social interactions. We present \textsc{DynToM}, a novel benchmark specifically designed to evaluate LLMs' ability to understand and track the temporal progression of mental states across interconnected scenarios. Through a systematic four-step framework, we generate 1,100 social contexts encompassing 5,500 scenarios and 78,100 questions, each validated for realism and quality. Our comprehensive evaluation of ten state-of-the-art LLMs reveals that their average performance underperforms humans by 44.7\%, with performance degrading significantly when tracking and reasoning about the shift of mental states. This performance gap highlights fundamental limitations in current LLMs' ability to model the dynamic nature of human mental states.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DYNTOM, a novel benchmark for evaluating Large Language Models' (LLMs) ability to understand and track the temporal evolution of human mental states (Theory of Mind - ToM). Unlike existing ToM benchmarks that focus on static snapshots of mental states, DYNTOM assesses LLMs' capacity to reason about how beliefs, emotions, intentions, and actions change over a series of interconnected social scenarios. The benchmark is constructed using a systematic four-step framework: social context construction, mental state trajectory design, scenario generation, and question formulation, all undergoing rigorous human validation. Experiments on ten representative LLMs reveal that they significantly underperform humans in DYNTOM, particularly in tracking state changes across scenarios, highlighting their limitations in modeling the dynamic nature of human mental states. The paper analyzes specific failure modes, including difficulties in tracking mental states in the middle of long sequences and challenges with reasoning about belief changes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the benchmark's focus on the *temporal evolution* of mental states. This is a crucial aspect of ToM that has been largely overlooked by previous benchmarks, which primarily focus on static scenarios.  This emphasis on dynamics is a significant advancement. However, the construction framework, while systematic, largely relies on prompting LLMs (specifically GPT-4-Turbo) for scenario and question generation, albeit with human validation. The reliance on LLMs for data generation is a common technique, so the novelty in that aspect is limited.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from demonstrating the limitations of current LLMs in a more realistic and complex ToM task. The findings have important implications for human-AI interaction, where understanding and responding to changing user mental states is essential. The detailed analysis of failure modes provides valuable insights for future research aimed at improving LLMs' ToM capabilities. DYNTOM provides a valuable testing tool and allows for nuanced evaluation that goes beyond simple correctness. Furthermore, its publicly available framework allows easy expansion of the scenarios and questions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Focus on a crucial and previously under-addressed aspect of ToM: temporal dynamics.
    *   Systematic benchmark construction with human validation.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of multiple LLMs.
    *   Detailed analysis of failure modes, providing actionable insights.
    *   Publicly available dataset and framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on GPT-4-Turbo for data generation, raising potential concerns about bias or artifacts in the benchmark.
    *   The level of difficulty in the generated scenarios may not reflect human-level complexity in real-world social interactions. There may be other ways to generate the scenarios, especially from a human-in-the-loop perspective.
    *   Limited exploration of alternative prompting strategies to improve LLMs' performance. While CoT was tested, it was not always beneficial. More experimentation with specialized prompts for ToM is needed.
    *   The study does not offer any solutions or suggestions of how to remedy the problem. It only makes suggestions that LLMs are inherently limited.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the direction of ToM research in LLMs by shifting the focus towards dynamic reasoning. DYNTOM will likely become a standard benchmark for evaluating progress in this area, and the identified failure modes will inspire new research efforts aimed at addressing these limitations. This could also lead to more advanced techniques for designing LLMs that can better model human behavior.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**  The paper presents a novel and significant contribution by addressing the under-explored aspect of temporal dynamics in ToM evaluation for LLMs. The systematic benchmark construction and comprehensive evaluation are strengths.  However, the reliance on LLMs for data generation and the absence of concrete solutions to the identified limitations temper the overall assessment. While the paper makes a substantial advance, there are opportunities to further refine the methodology and explore more proactive solutions to the challenges highlighted. Its influence will likely be substantial.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### EMRA-proxy: Enhancing Multi-Class Region Semantic Segmentation in Remote Sensing Images with Attention Proxy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17665v1)
- **Authors**: Yichun Yu, Yuqing Lan, Zhihuan Xing, Xiaoyi Yang, Tingyue Tang, Dan Yu
- **Abstract**: High-resolution remote sensing (HRRS) image segmentation is challenging due to complex spatial layouts and diverse object appearances. While CNNs excel at capturing local features, they struggle with long-range dependencies, whereas Transformers can model global context but often neglect local details and are computationally expensive.We propose a novel approach, Region-Aware Proxy Network (RAPNet), which consists of two components: Contextual Region Attention (CRA) and Global Class Refinement (GCR). Unlike traditional methods that rely on grid-based layouts, RAPNet operates at the region level for more flexible segmentation. The CRA module uses a Transformer to capture region-level contextual dependencies, generating a Semantic Region Mask (SRM). The GCR module learns a global class attention map to refine multi-class information, combining the SRM and attention map for accurate segmentation.Experiments on three public datasets show that RAPNet outperforms state-of-the-art methods, achieving superior multi-class segmentation accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EMRA-proxy, a novel semantic segmentation model specifically designed for high-resolution remote sensing (HRRS) images. It tackles the challenges posed by complex spatial layouts and variations in object appearance within HRRS imagery. EMRA-proxy employs two key components: Homogeneous Regions Attention Proxy (HRA-proxy) and Multi-class Attention Proxy (MCA-proxy). HRA-proxy abandons pixel-wise prediction, operating at the region level to capture contextual information and learn homogeneous semantic masks. MCA-proxy complements this by learning a global class attention map, addressing limitations in ViT's multi-class information extraction. The integration of these proxies aims to achieve higher precision in multi-class remote sensing image segmentation. The paper demonstrates the efficacy of EMRA-proxy through experiments on several publicly available HRRS datasets, showing superiority over existing state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Approach:** The region-based approach, abandoning traditional pixel-wise classification, is a significant departure from the norm in semantic segmentation for HRRS images.
    *   **Addressing Specific HRRS Challenges:** The model explicitly targets the issues of intra-class variability, complex spatial arrangements, and the need for finer detail extraction characteristic of HRRS data.
    *   **Effective Combination of Techniques:** The integration of Transformer-based architecture for global context with CNN-inspired region processing offers a potentially powerful hybrid approach. The HRA and MCA proxy design is thoughtful.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experiments on multiple datasets, demonstrating a clear improvement over existing techniques. Ablation studies convincingly support the contribution of each module (HRA-proxy and MCA-proxy).
    *   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is generally well-structured, clearly explaining the proposed method and experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity and Computational Cost:** While performance is improved, the complexity of integrating Transformers, region processing, and attention mechanisms likely leads to higher computational demands, which might be a limiting factor for large-scale applications. This is partially addressed by experimenting with different ViT sizes, but a full cost-benefit analysis is needed.
    *   **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:** The reliance on ImageNet pre-trained weights, even for ViT, may not be optimal for HRRS imagery, which often has very different characteristics than natural images. More investigation into domain-specific pre-training or fine-tuning strategies would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Individual Components:** While the overall architecture is novel, the individual components (Transformer, attention mechanisms, CNN-like region processing) are well-established.  The core novelty lies in their specific combination and application to the HRRS semantic segmentation problem.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** The experimental section explores the effect of hyperparameters. However, careful fine-tuning of these parameters may be required in other datasets which restricts its applicability.

*   **Significance and Potential Influence:**

    *   The region-based approach could pave the way for more efficient and accurate semantic segmentation techniques tailored specifically for HRRS imagery.
    *   The hybrid CNN-Transformer approach provides a viable path for addressing the limitations of purely CNN-based or Transformer-based models in dealing with the unique challenges of HRRS data.
    *   The clear improvement in segmentation accuracy, as demonstrated by the experiments, has the potential to impact various applications in remote sensing, including land cover mapping, urban planning, and environmental monitoring.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    The paper presents a compelling combination of existing and novel techniques to address a relevant problem in remote sensing. It demonstrates significant performance gains through a well-designed architecture and extensive experimental validation. While there is a slight lack of groundbreaking innovation in individual components, the novelty of the overall approach, combined with the solid experimental results, earns a score in the upper-middle range. While certain limitations are highlighted, the benefits outweigh the weaknesses.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Tuning Language Models for Robust Prediction of Diverse User Behaviors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17682v1)
- **Authors**: Fanjin Meng, Jingtao Ding, Jiahui Gong, Chen Yang, Hong Chen, Zuojian Wang, Haisheng Lu, Yong Li
- **Abstract**: Predicting user behavior is essential for intelligent assistant services, yet deep learning models often struggle to capture long-tailed behaviors. Large language models (LLMs), with their pretraining on vast corpora containing rich behavioral knowledge, offer promise. However, existing fine-tuning approaches tend to overfit to frequent ``anchor'' behaviors, reducing their ability to predict less common ``tail'' behaviors. In this paper, we introduce BehaviorLM, a progressive fine-tuning approach that addresses this issue. In the first stage, LLMs are fine-tuned on anchor behaviors while preserving general behavioral knowledge. In the second stage, fine-tuning uses a balanced subset of all behaviors based on sample difficulty to improve tail behavior predictions without sacrificing anchor performance. Experimental results on two real-world datasets demonstrate that BehaviorLM robustly predicts both anchor and tail behaviors and effectively leverages LLM behavioral knowledge to master tail behavior prediction with few-shot examples.
- **Summary**: The paper "Tuning Language Models for Robust Prediction of Diverse User Behaviors" introduces BehaviorLM, a two-stage progressive fine-tuning approach to improve the ability of large language models (LLMs) to predict diverse user behaviors, particularly addressing the challenge of long-tailed behavior distributions. The method first fine-tunes the LLM on frequent "anchor" behaviors alongside a general conversational corpus to preserve general knowledge and prevent bias. The second stage involves fine-tuning on a balanced subset of all behaviors, selected based on sample difficulty, to enhance tail behavior prediction without sacrificing anchor performance. Experimental results on two real-world datasets demonstrate that BehaviorLM robustly predicts both anchor and tail behaviors, effectively leveraging LLM behavioral knowledge, even with few-shot examples.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and important problem in intelligent assistant services: accurately predicting diverse user behaviors, especially the less frequent ones (tail behaviors). The issue of class imbalance in user behavior data and the tendency of standard fine-tuning to overfit to frequent behaviors is well-articulated. The core idea of BehaviorLM – a two-stage fine-tuning process that initially focuses on anchor behaviors and then refines with a difficulty-based selection of samples – is conceptually sound and well-motivated by the empirical observations presented.

The paper's novelty lies primarily in the specific approach to fine-tuning LLMs for this task. While other works have explored fine-tuning or incorporating LLMs in behavior prediction, BehaviorLM's progressive fine-tuning with difficulty-based sampling is a distinguishing feature. The empirical evaluation on two real-world datasets provides evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method. The improvements achieved on tail behavior prediction compared to baselines, as well as the reported sample efficiency, are promising. The ablation studies further validate the contribution of each component of the BehaviorLM approach.

However, some aspects warrant further scrutiny.

*   **Limited Architectural Innovation:** The paper does not introduce significant architectural changes to the LLM itself. The innovation is primarily in the fine-tuning strategy.
*   **Generalizability:** While the datasets are real-world, their specific characteristics might influence the performance of BehaviorLM. It would be valuable to see results on a wider range of datasets or with different types of LLMs (beyond Llama3.1-8B and Qwen).  How does BehaviorLM scale to hundreds or thousands of user behaviors?
*   **Difficulty Metric**:  The 'difficulty' metric hinges on correct/incorrect prediction, and category membership (anchor or tail).  This seems simplistic and could be improved.  More sophisticated methods of difficulty estimation could be investigated, especially given that 'difficulty' in the context of an LLM encompasses more than just a correct/incorrect flag.
*   **Anchor Behavior Selection:**  The definition of "anchor behaviors" as those occurring > 1% of the time seems arbitrary.  A more principled method of selecting anchor behaviors might improve performance.
*   **Baseline comparisons:** The baselines include a range of traditional and LLM-based methods, but some baselines are from 2023 or 2024. The LLM landscape is rapidly evolving; comparing against the very latest state-of-the-art LLMs would be beneficial.

The significance of the paper lies in its practical contribution to improving user behavior prediction with LLMs, especially for less common behaviors. This could have important implications for personalized services and intelligent assistants. The improved sample efficiency is also a valuable contribution, potentially reducing the cost of adapting LLMs to specific user behavior datasets.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to address a practical problem. The empirical results and ablation studies provide solid evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method. The paper is clearly written and well-organized. However, the lack of significant architectural innovation, the limited evaluation across diverse datasets and the simple difficulty metric prevent a higher score. The significance of the work lies in its potential practical impact and improved sample efficiency. While the paper pushes the state-of-the-art in LLM fine-tuning strategies for the specific task of user behavior prediction, more in-depth baseline comparison and method generalizability is warranted to justify an exceptional score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FutureSightDrive: Thinking Visually with Spatio-Temporal CoT for Autonomous Driving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17685v1)
- **Authors**: Shuang Zeng, Xinyuan Chang, Mengwei Xie, Xinran Liu, Yifan Bai, Zheng Pan, Mu Xu, Xing Wei
- **Abstract**: Visual language models (VLMs) have attracted increasing interest in autonomous driving due to their powerful reasoning capabilities. However, existing VLMs typically utilize discrete text Chain-of-Thought (CoT) tailored to the current scenario, which essentially represents highly abstract and symbolic compression of visual information, potentially leading to spatio-temporal relationship ambiguity and fine-grained information loss. Is autonomous driving better modeled on real-world simulation and imagination than on pure symbolic logic? In this paper, we propose a spatio-temporal CoT reasoning method that enables models to think visually. First, VLM serves as a world model to generate unified image frame for predicting future world states: where perception results (e.g., lane divider and 3D detection) represent the future spatial relationships, and ordinary future frame represent the temporal evolution relationships. This spatio-temporal CoT then serves as intermediate reasoning steps, enabling the VLM to function as an inverse dynamics model for trajectory planning based on current observations and future predictions. To implement visual generation in VLMs, we propose a unified pretraining paradigm integrating visual generation and understanding, along with a progressive visual CoT enhancing autoregressive image generation. Extensive experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method, advancing autonomous driving towards visual reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "FutureSightDrive," a novel approach to autonomous driving that leverages visual reasoning within Visual Language Models (VLMs). The core idea is to move away from discrete text-based Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning and instead utilize a "spatio-temporal CoT." This involves having the VLM generate predicted future world states in the form of unified image frames, encompassing perception results (lane dividers, 3D detections) and ordinary future frames. This spatio-temporal CoT serves as intermediate reasoning, enabling the VLM to act as an inverse dynamics model for trajectory planning.  To facilitate this, the authors propose a unified pre-training paradigm integrating visual generation and understanding and a progressive generation method. Experiments across trajectory planning, future frame generation, and scene understanding tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach by advocating for visual reasoning instead of text-based CoT in autonomous driving. The spatio-temporal CoT is a decent idea, and using VLMs as world models to *generate* future states, rather than just analyzing current states, is a distinguishing factor. The unified pre-training paradigm and progressive generation method contribute to the implementation of this vision.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from potentially bridging the gap between perception and action in autonomous driving.  The emphasis on visual reasoning directly aligns with how humans operate and could lead to more robust and interpretable systems. Avoiding the textual bottleneck is a key benefit. The reported performance improvements on nuScenes, combined with the ablation studies, suggest practical value. However, the reliance on generative models can be a weakness, and real-time performance/reliability will be critical. The paper is reasonably well-written and clearly explains the concepts.

**Strengths:**

*   **Concept:**  The idea of spatio-temporal CoT for visual reasoning in autonomous driving is intuitive and promising.
*   **Architecture:** The paper proposes a feasible architecture for implementing the concept, using a unified pretraining strategy for visual generation and understanding.
*   **Experiments:**  The paper demonstrates results on relevant benchmarks (nuScenes) and provides ablation studies to support the effectiveness of different components.
*   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written, making the proposed method easy to understand.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Generative Model Reliability:** The core of the approach depends on generating accurate and physically plausible future states using VLMs. Generative models are known to be prone to hallucinations, inconsistencies, and biases. The paper doesn't fully address this critical challenge. Specifically, it is unclear whether and how future frame generation constraints are explicitly implemented in the model.
*   **Real-Time Performance:**  Generating high-quality future frames is computationally expensive. The paper does not adequately discuss or demonstrate real-time performance, which is crucial for autonomous driving.
*   **Dataset limitations:** The results are based on nuScenes, which is a relatively constrained driving environment. The generalization of the approach to more complex and diverse scenarios is not fully explored.
*   **Baselines:** The experimental section compares with some other methods, but there isn't strong and comprehensive comparison with strong vision-language models for trajectory planning.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper can influence future research in autonomous driving by promoting the use of visual reasoning and exploring the potential of VLMs as world models. The concept of spatio-temporal CoT could be adopted and further refined by other researchers. However, the limitations related to generative model reliability and real-time performance need to be addressed to make a significant impact.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

I assign a score of 7.  The paper has a strong core concept (spatio-temporal CoT), a solid architecture, and convincing experimental results. The unified pre-training paradigm is also a useful practical contribution. However, it is not groundbreaking enough to warrant a score higher than 7, mostly due to the heavy reliance on generative models (which have associated reliability concerns, which the paper does not adequately address) and the lack of evidence that the algorithm is deployable in the real world in terms of computation. The paper is not transformative, but is a good quality increment and good inspiration for future lines of work. The concerns over real-time performance also detract from a higher valuation.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ELSPR: Evaluator LLM Training Data Self-Purification on Non-Transitive Preferences via Tournament Graph Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17691v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Yu, Yilun Liu, Minggui He, Shimin Tao, Weibin Meng, Xinhua Yang, Li Zhang, Hongxia Ma, Chang Su, Hao Yang, Fuliang Li
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are widely used as evaluators for open-ended tasks, while previous research has emphasized biases in LLM evaluations, the issue of non-transitivity in pairwise comparisons remains unresolved: non-transitive preferences for pairwise comparisons, where evaluators prefer A over B, B over C, but C over A. Our results suggest that low-quality training data may reduce the transitivity of preferences generated by the Evaluator LLM. To address this, We propose a graph-theoretic framework to analyze and mitigate this problem by modeling pairwise preferences as tournament graphs. We quantify non-transitivity and introduce directed graph structural entropy to measure the overall clarity of preferences. Our analysis reveals significant non-transitivity in advanced Evaluator LLMs (with Qwen2.5-Max exhibiting 67.96%), as well as high entropy values (0.8095 for Qwen2.5-Max), reflecting low overall clarity of preferences. To address this issue, we designed a filtering strategy, ELSPR, to eliminate preference data that induces non-transitivity, retaining only consistent and transitive preference data for model fine-tuning. Experiments demonstrate that models fine-tuned with filtered data reduce non-transitivity by 13.78% (from 64.28% to 50.50%), decrease structural entropy by 0.0879 (from 0.8113 to 0.7234), and align more closely with human evaluators (human agreement rate improves by 0.6% and Spearman correlation increases by 0.01).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ELSPR: Evaluator LLM Training Data Self-Purification on Non-Transitive Preferences via Tournament Graph Reconstruction" addresses the issue of non-transitivity in pairwise comparisons performed by LLMs used as evaluators. Non-transitivity occurs when LLMs exhibit preferences like A > B, B > C, but C > A, which undermines the reliability of their evaluations. The authors hypothesize that low-quality training data contributes to this problem. They propose a graph-theoretic framework, ELSPR, which models pairwise preferences as tournament graphs, quantifies non-transitivity, and introduces directed graph structural entropy to measure overall preference clarity.  ELSPR filters out preference data that induces non-transitivity, retaining only consistent and transitive data for fine-tuning.  Experiments demonstrate that fine-tuning with filtered data reduces non-transitivity, decreases structural entropy, and improves alignment with human evaluators.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic graph-theoretic approach to analyzing and mitigating non-transitivity in LLM evaluators. While the concept of non-transitivity has been identified previously, the authors provide a concrete methodology for quantifying and addressing it. The use of directed graph structural entropy is a novel metric for assessing the clarity of LLM preferences.  The proposed data filtering method, ELSPR, is also a practical contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper has significant implications for improving the reliability of LLM-based evaluation systems. As LLMs are increasingly used to evaluate other LLMs (and even potentially human outputs), ensuring the accuracy and consistency of these evaluations is crucial.  By reducing non-transitivity and improving alignment with human judgements, the authors contribute to more robust and trustworthy evaluation frameworks. The framework has practical importance since it enables researchers to leverage existing large-scale LLM-based preferences for fine-tuning better evaluator models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem of non-transitivity is well-defined and its importance is convincingly argued.
    *   The graph-theoretic framework provides a solid foundation for analysis and mitigation.
    *   The introduction of structural entropy offers a useful metric for quantifying preference clarity.
    *   The ELSPR filtering method is simple, effective, and readily implementable.
    *   The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the benefits of the proposed approach.
    *   The investigation of edge cases or the scenarios under which non-transitive preference is more likely is insightful and adds value.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper provides a significant reduction in non-transitivity, the issue is not completely eliminated. Further research is needed to address the remaining inconsistencies.
    *   The evaluation is primarily conducted on the AlpacaEval dataset.  Validation on a wider range of evaluation tasks and datasets would further strengthen the findings.
    *   The study mainly focuses on pairwise comparison tasks in single-turn dialogues. The extension to multi-turn dialogue tasks is left as future work.
    *   The improvement in human agreement rate is marginal.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on LLM evaluation and benchmarking.  The graph-theoretic framework and the ELSPR filtering method provide valuable tools for improving the reliability of LLM-based evaluations.  The insights into the causes of non-transitivity can also inform the design of more robust evaluation protocols. The work highlights the importance of data quality in training LLM evaluators, shifting the focus from solely scaling model size to improving data selection.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM evaluation. It provides a concrete methodology for addressing the issue of non-transitivity in LLM evaluators, which is a critical problem for ensuring the reliability of automated evaluations. The proposed ELSPR method is effective and readily implementable. While the improvements in human agreement rate are marginal, the substantial reduction in non-transitivity and the clear demonstration of improved preference clarity are compelling. The paper lays a solid foundation for future research in this area and is likely to have a significant impact on the design of more robust and trustworthy evaluation frameworks. However, the lack of validation on a wider range of datasets and the marginal improvement in human agreement rate are factors that keep the score below 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Activation Control for Efficiently Eliciting Long Chain-of-thought Ability of Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17697v1)
- **Authors**: Zekai Zhao, Qi Liu, Kun Zhou, Zihan Liu, Yifei Shao, Zhiting Hu, Biwei Huang
- **Abstract**: Despite the remarkable reasoning performance, eliciting the long chain-of-thought (CoT) ability in large language models (LLMs) typically requires costly reinforcement learning or supervised fine-tuning on high-quality distilled data. We investigate the internal mechanisms behind this capability and show that a small set of high-impact activations in the last few layers largely governs long-form reasoning attributes, such as output length and self-reflection. By simply amplifying these activations and inserting "wait" tokens, we can invoke the long CoT ability without any training, resulting in significantly increased self-reflection rates and accuracy. Moreover, we find that the activation dynamics follow predictable trajectories, with a sharp rise after special tokens and a subsequent exponential decay. Building on these insights, we introduce a general training-free activation control technique. It leverages a few contrastive examples to identify key activations, and employs simple analytic functions to modulate their values at inference time to elicit long CoTs. Extensive experiments confirm the effectiveness of our method in efficiently eliciting long CoT reasoning in LLMs and improving their performance. Additionally, we propose a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that trains only a last-layer activation amplification module and a few LoRA layers, outperforming full LoRA fine-tuning on reasoning benchmarks with significantly fewer parameters. Our code and data are publicly released.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a method called EELo-CoT (Efficiently Eliciting Long Chain-of-Thought) to improve the reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) without extensive training. The core idea is that the potential for long chain-of-thought reasoning already exists in pre-trained models, and it can be "awakened" by identifying and manipulating specific, high-impact activations in the final layers of the LLM. The method first identifies these activations through contrastive analysis. It then uses an analytic function based on the observed activation dynamics around special tokens (like "wait") to control and amplify these activations at inference time. A forcing reflection strategy, which inserts "wait" tokens to encourage self-correction, is also employed. Furthermore, the paper proposes a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method, using LoRA and an activation amplification module, reducing the training parameter. The authors conduct experiments on math and science reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating improved accuracy and self-reflection rates compared to baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel approach to eliciting long chain-of-thought reasoning. The key contribution is the idea of manipulating specific activations at inference time without full fine-tuning.
