**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a promising approach to LLM-based GUI testing in a less-explored area (desktop applications) but suffers from limited evaluation and reliance on subjective assessments. The novelty is incremental, building upon existing work in web and mobile GUI testing. The significance is moderate, showing potential benefits but lacking comparative data and broader application. The potential influence is limited by the scope of the evaluation. Overall, the paper offers a valuable contribution, but a more rigorous and comprehensive evaluation is needed to fully demonstrate its effectiveness.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Scaling Recurrent Neural Networks to a Billion Parameters with Zero-Order Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17852v1)
- **Authors**: Francois Chaubard, Mykel Kochenderfer
- **Abstract**: During inference, Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) scale constant in both FLOPs and GPU memory with increasing context length, as they compress all prior tokens into a fixed-size memory. In contrast, transformers scale linearly in FLOPs and, at best, linearly in memory during generation, since they must attend to all previous tokens explicitly. Despite this inference-time advantage, training large RNNs on long contexts remains impractical because standard optimization methods depend on Backpropagation Through Time (BPTT). BPTT requires retention of all intermediate activations during the forward pass, causing memory usage to scale linearly with both context length and model size. In this paper, we show that Zero-Order Optimization (ZOO) methods such as Random-vector Gradient Estimation (RGE) can successfully replace BPTT to train RNNs with convergence rates that match, or exceed BPTT by up to 19 fold, while using orders of magnitude less memory and cost, as the model remains in inference mode throughout training. We further demonstrate that Central-Difference RGE (CD-RGE) corresponds to optimizing a smoothed surrogate loss, inherently regularizing training and improving generalization. Our method matches or outperforms BPTT across three settings: (1) overfitting, (2) transduction, and (3) language modeling. Across all tasks, with sufficient perturbations, our models generalize as well as or better than those trained with BPTT, often in fewer steps. Despite the need for more forward passes per step, we can surpass BPTT wall-clock time per step using recent advancements such as FlashRNN and distributed inference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Scaling Recurrent Neural Networks to a Billion Parameters with Zero-Order Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes using Zero-Order Optimization (ZOO) methods, specifically Random-vector Gradient Estimation (RGE), as a viable alternative to Backpropagation Through Time (BPTT) for training large Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs).  The authors argue that BPTT's memory requirements scale linearly with context length, hindering the training of large RNNs on long sequences.  ZOO methods, by estimating gradients through function evaluations (forward passes) rather than backpropagation, significantly reduce memory requirements. They demonstrate that, despite needing more forward passes, recent advancements like FlashRNN and distributed training can make ZOO-based training competitive, even exceeding the wall-clock time performance of BPTT.  The paper presents empirical results on overfitting, transduction tasks, and language modeling, showing that ZOO can match or outperform BPTT while using substantially less memory.  They also highlight that Central-Difference RGE (CD-RGE) acts as an implicit regularizer, improving generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of applying ZOO to train large RNNs is not entirely new, as the paper acknowledges prior work using similar techniques on smaller RNNs. However, the successful scaling of this approach to billion-parameter RNNs on long sequences, coupled with empirical demonstrations across various tasks, represents a substantial advancement. The analysis of CD-RGE as an implicit regularizer and the integration with modern training techniques (FlashRNN, distributed training) also contribute to the novelty. The combination of distributed training with zero order methods to scale RNNs with FlashRNN is somewhat novel. It is not a totally novel idea as prior works have attempted zero order training of RNNs and Transformers.

*   **Significance:** If the claims hold up under scrutiny, this paper has the potential to be quite significant. The bottleneck to large language models is the quadratic scaling of attention in transformers. RNNs do not have this scaling issue, but are limited in their training due to memory constraints. By addressing the memory bottleneck in training RNNs, the paper opens up the possibility of exploring RNN architectures at scales comparable to transformers. The reduction in memory requirements allows for larger models and longer sequence lengths, which could lead to improved performance in various applications. Furthermore, the implicit regularization property of CD-RGE is valuable, potentially leading to more robust and generalizable models. The main weakness of this paper is that it is unlikely to actually be very useful, as most modern models leverage transformers. Also, even with improvements to RNN based methods, they are unlikely to have a long memory.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a critical limitation of RNNs: the memory bottleneck during training.
    *   Demonstrates empirical success in scaling ZOO to billion-parameter RNNs.
    *   Provides an analysis of CD-RGE as an implicit regularizer.
    *   Leverages recent advancements in RNN kernels and distributed training to improve wall-clock time.
    *   Clear and well-written, with thorough experimental details.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The need for a large number of forward passes might still be a significant bottleneck in some scenarios, despite optimizations.
    *   Generalization ability may be improved, but the models presented are limited. This is primarily a technique improvement.
    *   Requires very good code optimization.
    *   Does not explore RNN architectures to take advantage of these techniques.

*   **Impact:**
    *   Could encourage researchers to revisit RNN architectures and explore new designs that leverage the memory efficiency of ZOO-based training.
    *   May influence the development of more memory-efficient training algorithms for other deep learning models.
    *   Could lead to the creation of larger and more capable RNNs for tasks that require long-term dependencies.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution, successfully scaling ZOO for RNN training and demonstrating its effectiveness. The integration with FlashRNN and distributed training shows a practical approach to addressing the computational cost. The analysis of CD-RGE's regularization properties adds valuable insight. However, the paper does not solve any really hard problems. The lack of true novelty, coupled with the fact that most modern models are transformers that do not even require these techniques lead to a score of 6.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Multi-Person Interaction Generation from Two-Person Motion Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17860v1)
- **Authors**: Wenning Xu, Shiyu Fan, Paul Henderson, Edmond S. L. Ho
- **Abstract**: Generating realistic human motion with high-level controls is a crucial task for social understanding, robotics, and animation. With high-quality MOCAP data becoming more available recently, a wide range of data-driven approaches have been presented. However, modelling multi-person interactions still remains a less explored area. In this paper, we present Graph-driven Interaction Sampling, a method that can generate realistic and diverse multi-person interactions by leveraging existing two-person motion diffusion models as motion priors. Instead of training a new model specific to multi-person interaction synthesis, our key insight is to spatially and temporally separate complex multi-person interactions into a graph structure of two-person interactions, which we name the Pairwise Interaction Graph. We thus decompose the generation task into simultaneous single-person motion generation conditioned on one other's motion. In addition, to reduce artifacts such as interpenetrations of body parts in generated multi-person interactions, we introduce two graph-dependent guidance terms into the diffusion sampling scheme. Unlike previous work, our method can produce various high-quality multi-person interactions without having repetitive individual motions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms existing methods in reducing artifacts when generating a wide range of two-person and multi-person interactions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Graph-driven Interaction Sampling," a novel approach for generating realistic and diverse multi-person interactions. It leverages existing two-person motion diffusion models as motion priors.  Instead of training a new model for multi-person interaction, the method decomposes complex interactions into a graph structure of two-person interactions (Pairwise Interaction Graph).  It generates motions for each person by conditioning on the motion of one other person from their graph neighbors.  To mitigate artifacts like interpenetrations, the approach incorporates two graph-dependent guidance terms (Proxemics loss and Gauss Linking Integral (GLI) loss) into the diffusion sampling scheme.  The method demonstrates the generation of high-quality multi-person interactions, reducing artifacts and allowing control over interaction patterns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty by cleverly reformulating the complex problem of multi-person motion generation into smaller, manageable two-person interaction units.  The key innovation lies in the *Pairwise Interaction Graph* which enables the leveraging of existing two-person motion generation models. The approach moves beyond simple concatenation or sequential generation methods (e.g., FreeMotion).  The incorporation of graph-dependent guidance terms (Proxemics and GLI) to reduce artifacts is also a novel contribution to the field. The application of GLI in this context is interesting.

**Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its ability to generate plausible and controllable multi-person interactions without the need for extensive multi-person motion capture data, which are scarce.  The ability to easily adapt existing two-person models also increases its practical value. It enables a broader range of applications in animation, gaming, and virtual reality by making multi-character interaction generation more accessible. The paper also provides new evaluation metrics to quantify collision in close human interactions.

**Strengths:**

*   **Elegant decomposition:** The core idea of decomposing multi-person motion into pairwise interactions is a well-reasoned simplification.
*   **Leveraging existing models:** Reusing existing two-person motion models reduces the need for training large, complex, multi-person models.
*   **Artifact reduction:** The Proxemics and GLI losses effectively mitigate interpenetration and collision artifacts.
*   **Controllability:** The graph structure provides a good level of user control over interaction patterns.
*   **Versatility:**  The approach can be plugged into different two-person motion generation models, as demonstrated using both InterGen and in2In backbones.
* **Experimental evaluation:** Thorough experiments using quantitative metrics demonstrate improvements over existing methods.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Graph Dependence:** The method's reliance on a pre-defined interaction graph, while providing controllability, means the system is not learning these interactions automatically. Automating or suggesting interaction graphs based on scene context would be a significant advancement. LLM with designed prompts for graph generation might be useful to address this weakness.
*   **Indirect Relationships:**  The reliance on pairwise interactions can potentially miss complex non-local relationships between characters not directly connected in the graph. While auxiliary conditioning is briefly mentioned, further exploration of this area could improve the generated results, particularly regarding global scene consistency. This is acknowledged by the authors in their future work.
*   **SMPL fitting issues:** SMPL fitting relies on joint-based motion which could introduce issues when the base model is trained only on joint-based motion. The authors acknowledged this limitation.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in multi-person motion generation, particularly by encouraging the development of methods that leverage existing datasets and models. The introduction of graph-based interaction models could also pave the way for more sophisticated control mechanisms. The reported reductions in interpenetrations and collisions also make this method suitable for practical applications.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8**.  While not completely revolutionary, the "Graph-driven Interaction Sampling" method provides a significant step forward in multi-person motion generation. The innovative use of pairwise graphs and the mitigation of interpenetration artifacts make this approach both novel and valuable. The paper has certain limitations, mainly the reliance on pre-defined interaction graphs and the lack of comprehensive handling of indirect relationships, which prevent a perfect score. Overall, the paper is well-written, and experimentally solid. It introduces a new, effective, and practical approach to a challenging problem, making it an important contribution to the field.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Superplatforms Have to Attack AI Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17861v1)
- **Authors**: Jianghao Lin, Jiachen Zhu, Zheli Zhou, Yunjia Xi, Weiwen Liu, Yong Yu, Weinan Zhang
- **Abstract**: Over the past decades, superplatforms, digital companies that integrate a vast range of third-party services and applications into a single, unified ecosystem, have built their fortunes on monopolizing user attention through targeted advertising and algorithmic content curation. Yet the emergence of AI agents driven by large language models (LLMs) threatens to upend this business model. Agents can not only free user attention with autonomy across diverse platforms and therefore bypass the user-attention-based monetization, but might also become the new entrance for digital traffic. Hence, we argue that superplatforms have to attack AI agents to defend their centralized control of digital traffic entrance. Specifically, we analyze the fundamental conflict between user-attention-based monetization and agent-driven autonomy through the lens of our gatekeeping theory. We show how AI agents can disintermediate superplatforms and potentially become the next dominant gatekeepers, thereby forming the urgent necessity for superplatforms to proactively constrain and attack AI agents. Moreover, we go through the potential technologies for superplatform-initiated attacks, covering a brand-new, unexplored technical area with unique challenges. We have to emphasize that, despite our position, this paper does not advocate for adversarial attacks by superplatforms on AI agents, but rather offers an envisioned trend to highlight the emerging tensions between superplatforms and AI agents. Our aim is to raise awareness and encourage critical discussion for collaborative solutions, prioritizing user interests and perserving the openness of digital ecosystems in the age of AI agents.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper argues that superplatforms (e.g., Google, Amazon) *must* attack AI agents (e.g., those driven by large language models (LLMs)) to protect their business models.  The authors contend that AI agents threaten the current user-attention-based monetization strategies of these platforms by offering user autonomy and potentially disintermediating superplatforms as gatekeepers of digital traffic.  They apply gatekeeping theory to analyze the conflict, explore potential attack strategies superplatforms might employ against AI agents, focusing on attacks against GUI-based agents, and discuss the unique challenges and trade-offs inherent in such attacks (black box nature, imperceptibility to users, robustness).  The paper emphasizes that this is a descriptive forecast, not an advocacy for adversarial actions, and seeks to raise awareness and stimulate discussion around this emerging tension.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**

The paper's core novelty lies in framing the relationship between superplatforms and AI agents as an adversarial one *rooted in economic gatekeeping*. Applying gatekeeping theory to this specific context is a novel contribution. While adversarial attacks on AI systems and agents have been studied extensively, the focus on *superplatform-initiated* attacks with unique constraints (e.g., imperceptibility, black-box access) presents a relatively unexplored area. The taxonomy of attacks (goals, knowledge, visibility, timing) tailored to this specific scenario is also a valuable aspect.  It also proposes a number of attack strategies, although the level of detail is somewhat superficial.

* **Significance:**

The potential significance is considerable. The paper highlights a crucial and likely future dynamic in the tech landscape. If the authors' premise holds true (that superplatforms will perceive AI agents as an existential threat to their control over user attention), the ensuing "cold war" between them could have significant implications for the user experience, innovation, and the overall structure of the digital ecosystem.  The paper raises important questions about power, control, and the future of the internet in the age of AI. The paper's conceptual contribution lies in recognizing and articulating a fundamental tension and its potential consequences. However, the paper lacks concrete experimental validation or empirical analysis. The attack strategies are relatively high-level, and the paper does not delve into specific technical implementations.

* **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear and Compelling Argument:** The paper presents a well-reasoned and persuasive argument for its central thesis.
    *   **Novel Application of Theory:** Using gatekeeping theory to analyze the power dynamics between superplatforms and AI agents is insightful.
    *   **Identification of Key Challenges:** The paper clearly identifies the unique constraints and challenges associated with superplatform-initiated attacks.
    *   **Timeliness:** The topic is extremely relevant and timely, given the rapid advancements in AI and the increasing dominance of superplatforms.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** The paper is primarily theoretical and lacks empirical evidence to support its claims. There are no experiments or simulations to demonstrate the feasibility or effectiveness of the proposed attack strategies.
    *   **High-Level Treatment of Attack Techniques:** The paper provides a taxonomy of attack methods but does not delve into the technical details or implementation specifics.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Mitigation Strategies:** While the focus is on attacks, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of potential mitigation strategies or defenses that AI agents might employ.
    *   **Potential for Misinterpretation:** While the authors explicitly state they are not advocating for adversarial attacks, the framing of the paper could be misinterpreted. A stronger emphasis on ethical considerations and responsible development is warranted.

*   **Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in several areas:

    *   **Adversarial AI:** The unique constraints and objectives of superplatform-initiated attacks could inspire new research directions in adversarial AI.
    *   **AI Agent Security:** The paper highlights the need for more robust and resilient AI agents that can withstand sophisticated attacks.
    *   **Platform Governance:** The paper raises important questions about the role of platforms in shaping the digital ecosystem and the need for ethical guidelines and responsible innovation.
    *   **AI Disintermediation:** This work will influence other works exploring how AI systems will alter business ecosystems.

**Score:** 6.5/10

**Justification:**

I'm assigning a score of 6.5 out of 10 because the paper is valuable in its novel framing of the superplatform/AI agent relationship as an adversarial one and for highlighting a likely future dynamic. The application of gatekeeping theory is insightful, and the identification of unique challenges is important. However, the lack of empirical validation and the high-level treatment of attack techniques limit its immediate impact. Further research, including experiments and more detailed technical analysis, is needed to fully realize the potential of this work. A more nuanced discussion of ethical concerns would also strengthen the paper. The concepts presented in this paper will influence the direction of research within this area.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Daily-Omni: Towards Audio-Visual Reasoning with Temporal Alignment across Modalities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17862v1)
- **Authors**: Ziwei Zhou, Rui Wang, Zuxuan Wu
- **Abstract**: Recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve promising performance on visual and audio benchmarks independently. However, the ability of these models to process cross-modal information synchronously remains largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce: 1) Daily-Omni, an Audio-Visual Questioning and Answering benchmark comprising 684 videos of daily life scenarios from diverse sources, rich in both audio and visual information, and featuring 1197 multiple-choice QA pairs across 6 major tasks; 2) Daily-Omni QA Generation Pipeline, which includes automatic annotation, QA generation and QA optimization, significantly improves efficiency for human evaluation and scalability of the benchmark; 3) Daily-Omni-Agent, a training-free agent utilizing open-source Visual Language Model (VLM), Audio Language Model (ALM) and Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) model to establish a baseline for this benchmark. The results show that current MLLMs still struggle significantly with tasks requiring audio-visual integration, but combining VLMs and ALMs with simple temporal alignment techniques can achieve substantially better performance. Codes and benchmark are available at \href{https://github.com/Lliar-liar/Daily-Omni}{https://github.com/Lliar-liar/Daily-Omni}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Daily-Omni, a new audio-visual question-answering (AVQA) benchmark designed to evaluate multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on real-world daily life scenarios. It addresses limitations in existing datasets by focusing on temporal alignment across audio and visual modalities and incorporating a wider range of sounds beyond just speech. The authors also present a Daily-Omni QA Generation Pipeline, which automates annotation, QA generation, and optimization to improve scalability and efficiency. Furthermore, they propose Daily-Omni-Agent, a training-free agent that combines open-source visual and audio language models along with temporal alignment techniques, serving as a strong baseline. Experiments demonstrate that current MLLMs struggle with tasks requiring deep audio-visual temporal integration and that the Daily-Omni Agent achieves state-of-the-art results among open-source methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Dataset (Positive):** The Daily-Omni dataset is a significant contribution.  Existing AVQA datasets often focus on specific domains (e.g., music, panoramic videos) or tasks, lack temporal dynamics, or rely on static image-audio pairs. Daily-Omni's emphasis on daily life scenarios, temporal alignment, diverse soundscapes, and a more extensive range of question types fills a gap.
*   **QA Generation Pipeline (Positive):**  The automated QA generation pipeline is valuable. The use of MLLMs and LLMs for annotation, question generation, and optimization, along with human evaluation and filtering, represents a scalable approach to creating high-quality AVQA benchmarks. Automation significantly reduces the annotation time.
*   **Daily-Omni Agent (Mixed):** The Daily-Omni Agent, while a useful baseline, has limited novelty in its individual components. It leverages existing open-source models (VLMs, ALMs, ASR) and combines them with relatively simple temporal alignment techniques. However, the *combination* of these elements into a single, effective agent within the Daily-Omni framework provides a practical demonstration of the benchmark's utility.

**Significance:**

*   **Advancement of MLLMs (Positive):**  By highlighting the challenges that current MLLMs face in complex audio-visual reasoning, the Daily-Omni benchmark provides a valuable resource for driving progress in this area. It encourages the development of models capable of truly integrating and aligning information from different modalities over time, rather than simply processing them independently. The paper's analysis of the performance of different model architectures (OLMs, VLMs, text-only LLMs) is also insightful.
*   **Practicality (Positive):** The release of the dataset, code, and baseline agent enables other researchers to easily utilize the benchmark and compare their models against existing methods. The efficient QA generation pipeline also lowers the barrier to entry for future dataset expansion and customization.
*   **Limitations (Negative):** The analysis of models primarily uses open-source models with one proprietary model used for benchmarking. This may not be representative of the cutting edge. The dataset is still relatively small compared to some visual datasets.

**Strengths:**

*   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
*   Novel and useful dataset with a focus on temporal dynamics.
*   Scalable QA generation pipeline.
*   Strong baseline agent.
*   Comprehensive evaluation and analysis of existing MLLMs.
*   Public release of dataset and code.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The Daily-Omni Agent relies on established components, so the agent design is not particularly innovative.
*   While the dataset is novel, its size is limited compared to larger unimodal datasets or some existing multimodal datasets.
*   The performance improvements achieved by the Daily-Omni Agent are moderate, suggesting that more sophisticated techniques are needed to fully address the challenges of audio-visual temporal integration.

**Potential Influence:**

The Daily-Omni benchmark has the potential to become a widely used resource in the AVQA research community.  It could foster the development of more powerful and robust MLLMs capable of reasoning about real-world scenarios that involve synchronized audio and visual information.  The efficient QA generation pipeline may also inspire similar approaches for other multimodal benchmarks. The focus on audio-visual temporal awareness could shift the focus of the community.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

Daily-Omni represents a valuable contribution to the field of multimodal learning, warranting a score of 7.5.  The dataset addresses a clear gap in the existing literature by focusing on temporal alignment in real-world audio-visual scenarios. The automated QA generation pipeline and Daily-Omni Agent demonstrate the practical utility of the benchmark. The relatively incremental nature of the agent design and the dataset's size prevents a higher score. It sets a baseline for performance, and it will serve as a good step forward to test models. Ultimately, while it builds upon existing ideas, the synthesis of these elements into a practical, valuable resource justifies a solid score.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### The emergence of sparse attention: impact of data distribution and benefits of repetition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17863v1)
- **Authors**: Nicolas Zucchet, Francesco d'Angelo, Andrew K. Lampinen, Stephanie C. Y. Chan
- **Abstract**: Emergence is a fascinating property of large language models and neural networks more broadly: as models scale and train for longer, they sometimes develop new abilities in sudden ways. Despite initial studies, we still lack a comprehensive understanding of how and when these abilities emerge. To address this gap, we study the emergence over training of sparse attention, a critical and frequently observed attention pattern in Transformers. By combining theoretical analysis of a toy model with empirical observations on small Transformers trained on a linear regression variant, we uncover the mechanics driving sparse attention emergence and reveal that emergence timing follows power laws based on task structure, architecture, and optimizer choice. We additionally find that repetition can greatly speed up emergence. Finally, we confirm these results on a well-studied in-context associative recall task. Our findings provide a simple, theoretically grounded framework for understanding how data distributions and model design influence the learning dynamics behind one form of emergence.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the emergence of sparse attention patterns in Transformers during training, framing it as a key aspect of the broader phenomenon of emergent abilities in large language models. Through a combination of theoretical analysis on a simplified linear regression task and empirical observations on small Transformers trained on both the simplified task and an associative recall task, the authors seek to understand the mechanics driving sparse attention emergence. Key findings include: (1) emergence timing follows power laws dependent on task structure, architecture, and optimizer choice; (2) repetition in the training data can significantly accelerate emergence; and (3) the theoretical framework developed can explain the emergence of in-context learning in the associative recall task. The paper argues that sparse attention offers a unifying perspective on seemingly diverse emergence phenomena and highlights the practical benefits of repetition for accelerating specific neural circuit formation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Framing of a Significant Problem:** The paper addresses a crucial question in the field of large language models: how and when do emergent abilities arise? The framing of sparse attention as a lens for understanding this is compelling and potentially insightful.
    *   **Mixed Methods Approach:** The combination of theoretical modeling and empirical validation is a strength. The simplified linear regression task allows for analytical tractability, while the experiments on small Transformers provide a bridge to more realistic scenarios.
    *   **Identification of Key Factors:** The identification of task structure, architecture, optimizer choice, and repetition as influential factors in emergence timing is a valuable contribution. The observation that repetition can accelerate emergence challenges the standard notion that data diversity is always superior.
    *   **Connection to In-Context Learning:** The demonstration that the developed framework can explain the emergence of in-context learning in an associative recall task strengthens the claim that sparse attention is a unifying principle.
    *   **Well-written and organized:** The paper is generally well-written and the arguments are presented in a logical manner.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplifications and Idealizations:** The theoretical model relies on several simplifying assumptions (e.g., gradient flow dynamics, uniform attention at initialization) that limit its direct applicability to large, real-world models. While these simplifications are necessary for analytical tractability, the gap between the model and real-world LLMs is substantial.
    *   **Limited Scope of Empirical Validation:** The empirical experiments are conducted on *small* Transformers and relatively simple tasks. The extent to which the findings generalize to the large-scale models that exhibit the most compelling emergent abilities is unclear. The fact that Adam seems to play such a crucial role doesn't really get incorporated into the the theoretical framework.
    *   **Oversimplification of Repetition's Role:** While the paper highlights the benefits of repetition, it also acknowledges the potential for overfitting. However, the theoretical framework doesn't fully capture the trade-off between acceleration and generalization. The explanation of these trade-offs is somewhat shallow and a deeper dive is probably warranted.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Other Theories of Emergence:** The paper mentions other theoretical frameworks for emergence (e.g., grokking, singular learning theory) but doesn't provide a detailed comparison of their predictions or limitations relative to the sparse attention perspective.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Techniques:** The theoretical techniques used (linearization, dynamical systems analysis) are relatively standard. The novelty lies more in the application of these techniques to the specific problem of sparse attention emergence.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper's novelty lies primarily in its framing of sparse attention as a central mechanism for understanding emergent abilities in LLMs and in the identification of key factors influencing its emergence timing. The theoretical framework, while simplified, provides a potential starting point for a more comprehensive understanding of these dynamics. The finding that repetition can accelerate emergence, while not entirely new, is further substantiated and linked to the sparse attention framework. The use of a tractable linear regression task is quite neat.

    The significance of the paper is somewhat limited by the scope of the empirical validation and the degree of simplification in the theoretical model. However, the paper offers a potentially fruitful avenue for future research and could inspire more sophisticated models that bridge the gap between theory and practice. It also helps to better understand the role of data distribution (specifically repetition) in the learning dynamics of neural networks.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

The paper presents a well-reasoned and potentially insightful perspective on the emergence of sparse attention. The combination of theoretical analysis and empirical validation is a strength, and the identification of key factors influencing emergence timing is a valuable contribution. However, the simplifying assumptions of the theoretical model and the limited scope of the empirical validation somewhat constrain the paper's significance. While it does advance the state of the art, its practical implications are not fully demonstrated.

In summary, the score of 6 reflects a paper with good potential that takes a thoughtful approach to a critical problem in LLMs but suffers from certain limitations of scope and validation. While the *core idea is strong*, a score in the 7-9 range would require stronger experimental confirmation on at-scale models.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### T2I-Eval-R1: Reinforcement Learning-Driven Reasoning for Interpretable Text-to-Image Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17897v1)
- **Authors**: Zi-Ao Ma, Tian Lan, Rong-Cheng Tu, Shu-Hang Liu, Heyan Huang, Zhijing Wu, Chen Xu, Xian-Ling Mao
- **Abstract**: The rapid progress in diffusion-based text-to-image (T2I) generation has created an urgent need for interpretable automatic evaluation methods that can assess the quality of generated images, therefore reducing the human annotation burden. To reduce the prohibitive cost of relying on commercial models for large-scale evaluation, and to improve the reasoning capabilities of open-source models, recent research has explored supervised fine-tuning (SFT) of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) as dedicated T2I evaluators. However, SFT approaches typically rely on high-quality critique datasets, which are either generated by proprietary LLMs-with potential issues of bias and inconsistency-or annotated by humans at high cost, limiting their scalability and generalization. To address these limitations, we propose T2I-Eval-R1, a novel reinforcement learning framework that trains open-source MLLMs using only coarse-grained quality scores, thereby avoiding the need for annotating high-quality interpretable evaluation rationale. Our approach integrates Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) into the instruction-tuning process, enabling models to generate both scalar scores and interpretable reasoning chains with only easy accessible annotated judgment scores or preferences. Furthermore, we introduce a continuous reward formulation that encourages score diversity and provides stable optimization signals, leading to more robust and discriminative evaluation behavior. Experimental results on three established T2I meta-evaluation benchmarks demonstrate that T2I-Eval-R1 achieves significantly higher alignment with human assessments and offers more accurate interpretable score rationales compared to strong baseline methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces T2I-Eval-R1, a novel reinforcement learning framework for training open-source Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to evaluate text-to-image (T2I) generation.  Instead of relying on expensive, high-quality annotated datasets of evaluation rationales, T2I-Eval-R1 uses only coarse-grained quality scores for training.  The approach integrates Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) into instruction tuning, enabling the model to generate both scalar scores and interpretable reasoning chains. A continuous reward formulation is also introduced to encourage score diversity and stable optimization. Experimental results on standard T2I meta-evaluation benchmarks demonstrate that T2I-Eval-R1 achieves significantly higher alignment with human assessments and provides more accurate interpretable score rationales compared to strong baseline methods, including those that use closed-source LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a worthwhile and practical approach to address a significant problem: the lack of scalable and interpretable evaluation methods for T2I generation. The novelty lies in:
    *   Using reinforcement learning, specifically GRPO, with coarse-grained scores to train open-source MLLMs for T2I evaluation. This is a departure from supervised fine-tuning (SFT) approaches that rely on expensive or potentially biased high-quality rationale datasets.
    *   The continuous reward formulation addresses limitations of binary reward schemes in previous GRPO approaches, allowing for finer-grained learning based on continuous scores.
    *   The modular prompt design.
    *   The construction of mixed datasets from multiple sources, enhancing the generalizability and robustness of the MLLM evaluator.

*   **Significance:** The research is highly significant due to the rising importance of diffusion models and the critical need for automated quality control and development tools.
    *   Reducing Reliance on Commercial Models:  T2I-Eval-R1 aims to make T2I evaluation more accessible by using open-source models rather than expensive closed-source APIs. This democratizes research and development in the field.
    *   Improving Interpretability:  Generating interpretable rationales along with scores is vital for understanding the strengths and weaknesses of T2I models, enabling targeted improvements.
    *   Scalability and Generalizability: The focus on coarse-grained supervision and RL contributes to more scalable evaluation methods. The TIFA results are encouraging in the generalizability.
    *   Performance: The reported gains in correlation with human judgments are notable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong Empirical Results: The paper provides thorough experimental validation on multiple established benchmarks.  The results clearly demonstrate the superiority of T2I-Eval-R1 over existing methods.
    *   Clear Technical Exposition: The description of the framework is clear and well-structured. The motivations behind design choices, particularly the continuous reward formulation, are clearly explained.
    *   Good Ablation Studies: The ablation studies provide convincing evidence for the benefits of the proposed design, particularly the value of continuous rewards.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited Scope of Evaluation Dimensions: The paper acknowledges that evaluation across truly novel dimensions (e.g., aesthetics) is constrained by the availability of appropriate datasets. A demonstration of rapidly adapting the evaluator to a new dimension even with small set of labelled data would be an improvement.
    *   Model Size and Computational Resources: While understandable due to resource constraints, the use of a relatively smaller MLLM (Qwen2.5-VL-7B) raises questions about the potential scaling behavior of the framework.
    *   Limited Consideration of Harmful Content: While the paper acknowledges the potential for harmful content and applies standard ethical practices like dataset curation, there is not a strong discussion of how T2I-Eval-R1 specifically addresses this problem.
    *   Dependence on Gold Rationales for Fine-tuning: While the paper focuses on reinforcement learning, certain models (e.g., Enhance) still involve supervised learning and rejection sampling based on existing datasets.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper's approach has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and interpretable T2I evaluation methods.
    *   The RL training strategy could be adapted to other generative tasks or evaluation settings.
    *   The approach might encourage the development of richer benchmark datasets for T2I evaluation.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **8**. The T2I-Eval-R1 framework offers a significant advancement in T2I evaluation. It introduces a novel and effective approach to training open-source MLLMs for interpretable evaluation using only coarse-grained supervision. The experimental results are compelling, and the technical exposition is well-structured.
However, the limited dataset diversity and computational resources somewhat restrict the scope of the study and leave open the scaling behavior of the framework to larger MLLMs and more diverse evaluation scenarios, preventing it from receiving a higher score. Despite these limitations, the contributions are significant and address a critical problem in the field.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### LLM Meeting Decision Trees on Tabular Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17918v1)
- **Authors**: Hangting Ye, Jinmeng Li, He Zhao, Dandan Guo, Yi Chang
- **Abstract**: Tabular data have been playing a vital role in diverse real-world fields, including healthcare, finance, etc. With the recent success of Large Language Models (LLMs), early explorations of extending LLMs to the domain of tabular data have been developed. Most of these LLM-based methods typically first serialize tabular data into natural language descriptions, and then tune LLMs or directly infer on these serialized data. However, these methods suffer from two key inherent issues: (i) data perspective: existing data serialization methods lack universal applicability for structured tabular data, and may pose privacy risks through direct textual exposure, and (ii) model perspective: LLM fine-tuning methods struggle with tabular data, and in-context learning scalability is bottle-necked by input length constraints (suitable for few-shot learning). This work explores a novel direction of integrating LLMs into tabular data throughough logical decision tree rules as intermediaries, proposes a decision tree enhancer with LLM-derived rule for tabular prediction, DeLTa. The proposed DeLTa avoids tabular data serialization, and can be applied to full data learning setting without LLM fine-tuning. Specifically, we leverage the reasoning ability of LLMs to redesign an improved rule given a set of decision tree rules. Furthermore, we provide a calibration method for original decision trees via new generated rule by LLM, which approximates the error correction vector to steer the original decision tree predictions in the direction of ``errors'' reducing. Finally, extensive experiments on diverse tabular benchmarks show that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DeLTa, a novel approach to integrate Large Language Models (LLMs) with decision trees for tabular data prediction.  Unlike most existing LLM-based methods that serialize tabular data into natural language, DeLTa leverages LLMs to refine decision tree rules.  It first trains an ensemble of decision trees (Random Forest) and then uses an LLM to generate an improved, more coherent rule given the initial set of decision tree rules. This new rule then guides a sample-specific error correction vector used to calibrate the predictions of the original decision trees. This avoids tabular data serialization and allows application to full data learning scenarios without LLM fine-tuning. The method is evaluated on various tabular benchmarks and demonstrates state-of-the-art performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The central idea of using LLMs to *refine* decision tree rules rather than directly predicting labels or features from serialized tabular data is relatively novel. It addresses the data privacy concerns and the limitations of serialization methods. The approach avoids both the need for LLM fine-tuning and the input length constraints of in-context learning. The combination of leveraging an LLM for rule reasoning, together with gradient-based error correction, is also an interesting contribution.

* **Significance:** Tabular data remains ubiquitous and important. The paper's potential to improve tabular data prediction performance *without* requiring LLM fine-tuning or exposing raw tabular data to the LLM offers a significant practical advantage. The claim of state-of-the-art performance on diverse benchmarks certainly makes it significant. However, a critical question is how much better it performs compared to highly optimized traditional tabular methods (like optimized CatBoost or XGBoost configurations).

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addresses Key Issues:** The paper directly tackles the critical issues of data privacy and LLM fine-tuning limitations associated with existing LLM-based tabular data approaches.
    * **Clear Methodology:** The approach is well-defined and explained. The steps involved in rule refinement, error correction, and the overall DeLTa framework are clearly presented.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:**  The experiments on diverse tabular benchmarks show competitive or state-of-the-art performance compared to existing LLM-based and traditional methods.  The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of the individual components of DeLTa.
    * **Privacy preserving:**  The method avoids direct exposure of tabular data to the LLM, which improves data privacy.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Random Forest:** The performance is inherently tied to the quality of the initial decision trees. While Random Forests are a good starting point, the approach may be less effective if the initial rules are already poor.
    * **LLM Prompt Engineering:** The effectiveness of the rule refinement step depends significantly on the quality of the prompt provided to the LLM. The paper mentions prompt details in Appendix A.4, but more discussion on prompt engineering strategies and their impact on performance would be beneficial.
    * **Computational Cost:** While it claims computational efficiency, the runtime comparison table is only for the Adult dataset. Furthermore, it requires an LLM, which in itself is computationally expensive to query compared to vanilla tabular methods.
    * **Lack of Comparisons against highly optimized traditional methods:** It would be great to see results against highly optimized hyperparameter search for traditional tabular methods to see if LLMs *really* make a difference. This comparison is missing, which reduces the score.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by encouraging exploration of LLM-based rule refinement and knowledge distillation techniques for tabular data prediction. It offers a potentially more efficient and privacy-preserving alternative to direct LLM-based prediction.

* **Justification of Score:** Overall, the paper presents a novel and interesting approach to integrating LLMs with decision trees for tabular data prediction, addressing important limitations of previous methods. The empirical results support its effectiveness. However, the LLM's influence depends on the effectiveness of the prompting and the quality of the initial decision trees, and the performance gap against traditional algorithms, highly hyperparameter tuned, is not evident. Because of this, and since it requires expensive calls to LLM services compared to traditional methods, a high score is not possible.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Selection Mechanisms for Sequence Modeling using Linear State Space Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17932v1)
- **Authors**: Umberto Casti, Sandro Zampieri, Fabio Pasqualetti
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in language modeling tasks have been driven by architectures such as Transformers and, more recently, by Selective State Space Models (SSMs). In this paper, we introduce an alternative selection mechanism inspired by control theory methodologies. Specifically, we propose a novel residual generator for selection, drawing an analogy to fault detection strategies in Linear Time-Invariant (LTI) systems. Unlike Mamba, which utilizes Linear Time-Varying (LTV) systems, our approach combines multiple LTI systems, preserving their beneficial properties during training while achieving comparable selectivity. To evaluate the effectiveness of the proposed architecture, we test its performance on synthetic tasks. While these tasks are not inherently critical, they serve as benchmarks to test the selectivity properties of different cores architecture. This work highlights the potential of integrating theoretical insights with experimental advancements, offering a complementary perspective to deep learning innovations at the intersection of control theory and machine learning.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including an assessment of its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an alternative selection mechanism for sequence modeling using Linear State Space Models (SSMs), inspired by control theory principles, specifically fault detection in Linear Time-Invariant (LTI) systems.  Unlike Mamba, which uses Linear Time-Varying (LTV) systems for selection, this approach combines multiple LTI systems, aiming to retain their benefits during training while achieving comparable selectivity.  The proposed method involves a residual generator for selection, drawing an analogy to fault detection strategies. The effectiveness of the architecture is evaluated on synthetic tasks, specifically variations of the Induction Head task, used as benchmarks to test the selectivity properties of different core architectures. The authors emphasize the connection between SSMs and control theory and propose their method as a building block for larger models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a moderately novel approach. The core idea of leveraging fault detection mechanisms from control theory for sequence selection in SSMs is interesting.  The architecture using a "residual generator" and the combination of multiple LTI systems for selectivity provides a fresh perspective. The comparison to Mamba and the explicit aim to retain the advantages of LTI systems while addressing their limitations are important. The extension of the Induction Head task to an Extended Induction Head task to evaluate performance in capturing contextual information is also a worthwhile addition.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate but has the potential to grow. The paper doesn't introduce a complete, state-of-the-art sequence modeling architecture directly competing with Transformer or Mamba. Instead, it focuses on exploring a different selection mechanism, which is potentially a fundamental building block. The authors acknowledge this and position their work as a contribution to the understanding of SSMs through the lens of control theory. The synthetic tasks, while simple, are well-chosen to highlight the differences between the proposed approach and the time-varying discretization used in Mamba. A potential impact could be influencing the design of future SSM architectures with control theory insights. However, the paper lacks experimental results on more complex, real-world datasets, which limits the assessment of its practical impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear explanation of the proposed method, including the analogy to fault detection.
    *   Well-defined synthetic tasks for evaluating selectivity.
    *   Explicit comparison to Mamba and a discussion of the trade-offs involved.
    *   Emphasis on the connection between SSMs and control theory.
    *   The I/O representation for the model, enabling parallelizable training
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Lack of experimental results on real-world datasets.
    *   The synthetic tasks are relatively simple and may not fully reflect the challenges of real-world sequence modeling.
    *   The improvement over other selection mechanism is not validated with more complex task.
    *   The Mamba model has far more parameters than the models in the paper's experimentations

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's potential lies in:
    *   Inspiring new selection mechanisms in SSMs, drawing upon control theory principles.
    *   Promoting a deeper understanding of the theoretical foundations of SSMs.
    *   Providing a building block that can be integrated into larger architectures.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a novel perspective on selection mechanisms in SSMs, grounded in control theory. However, the limited experimental evaluation on synthetic data and the lack of a complete architecture to demonstrate the practical advantages of the proposed selection mechanism restrain its immediate impact. The paper falls short of being a groundbreaking contribution that immediately transforms the field. It does, however, offer a solid starting point for future research that could lead to more impactful results.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: eess.SY
- **Score**: 6/10

### Understanding Gated Neurons in Transformers from Their Input-Output Functionality
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17936v1)
- **Authors**: Sebastian Gerstner, Hinrich Schütze
- **Abstract**: Interpretability researchers have attempted to understand MLP neurons of language models based on both the contexts in which they activate and their output weight vectors. They have paid little attention to a complementary aspect: the interactions between input and output. For example, when neurons detect a direction in the input, they might add much the same direction to the residual stream ("enrichment neurons") or reduce its presence ("depletion neurons"). We address this aspect by examining the cosine similarity between input and output weights of a neuron. We apply our method to 12 models and find that enrichment neurons dominate in early-middle layers whereas later layers tend more towards depletion. To explain this finding, we argue that enrichment neurons are largely responsible for enriching concept representations, one of the first steps of factual recall. Our input-output perspective is a complement to activation-dependent analyses and to approaches that treat input and output separately.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the input-output (IO) functionality of gated neurons in transformer models. Unlike previous work that focuses on activation-based or output-weight-based neuron analysis, this paper classifies neurons based on the relationship between their input and output weight vectors.  It introduces concepts like "enrichment" and "depletion" neurons, where enrichment neurons add detected directions to the residual stream and depletion neurons remove them. The study analyzes 12 different language models and finds that enrichment neurons dominate early layers, while depletion neurons are more prevalent in later layers. The authors propose that enrichment neurons play a role in concept representation and depletion neurons in residual stream sharpening. They also discuss the "double-checking" phenomenon where a neuron uses two orthogonal input vectors (gate and input) to detect the same concept.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its IO-centric perspective on neuron function, particularly the focus on enrichment and depletion.  Prior work had not systematically examined this aspect in gated activation functions. The double-checking concept is also a novel observation. However, the idea that early layers handle feature extraction and later layers fine-tune or sharpen representations is not entirely new, as the authors acknowledge.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is moderate.  It offers a valuable new lens for understanding transformer behavior and provides a structured framework for classifying neurons based on their IO interactions. The finding that enrichment dominates early layers and depletion later layers offers insight into how language models progressively build and refine representations.  The paper also offers an exhaustive way of analyzing all neurons in gated activation functions (opposed to just focusing on subsets), which makes it useful to fully understand them.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defined concepts and methodology. The definitions of enrichment, depletion, and other IO functionalities are precise and well-explained. The parameter-based method is efficient and exhaustive.
    *   Extensive empirical analysis. The study covers 12 different models, providing a robust dataset for generalization.
    *   Interesting and intuitive interpretations. The authors connect their findings to existing theories of transformer architecture and the stages of inference.
    *   Case studies providing qualitative insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited mechanistic explanation. While the paper *identifies* differences in IO function across layers, it doesn't provide a *causal* explanation for why this happens. The "stages of inference" explanation remains a hypothesis.
    *   Limited ablation studies. While mentioned as future work, the lack of ablation experiments weakens the claim that enrichment and depletion neurons are directly responsible for the stages of inference.
    *   Model-centric perspective. The insights are derived largely from analyzing the *weights* of the model, rather than the data that flows *through* the model.
    *   Interpretations are still high level. While it gives a new structure to think about neurons, the functionality can be quite vague (e.g., with conditional depletion, it remains unclear in many cases).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's framework could influence future interpretability research by encouraging a more holistic view of neuron function that considers both inputs and outputs. The concept of enrichment and depletion neurons could become a standard way of categorizing and analyzing transformer behavior. Further work could build upon this foundation by designing targeted interventions to test the functional roles of these neuron types. The methods developed are clearly detailed, which allows for straightforward continuation from other work.

*   **Score Justification:** The paper offers a valuable new perspective and provides a solid empirical foundation. However, the lack of strong mechanistic explanations and the reliance on existing theories limits its overall impact. While novel in its systematic IO-centric approach and comprehensive taxonomy, it's more of an incremental step than a paradigm shift. While future results may add onto the paper, based on the current results it is more of an exploration of an area that adds a valuable lens to the field. The paper has both strengths and weaknesses; to better strengthen it requires more work into *why* the phenomena occur, and less on *showing* that it occurs. It remains however a great framework to study gated activation functions.
