- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Survival Games: Human-LLM Strategic Showdowns under Severe Resource Scarcity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17937v1)
- **Authors**: Zhihong Chen, Yiqian Yang, Jinzhao Zhou, Qiang Zhang, Chin-Teng Lin, Yiqun Duan
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) raises critical concerns about their ethical alignment, particularly in scenarios where human and AI co-exist under the conflict of interest. This work introduces an extendable, asymmetric, multi-agent simulation-based benchmarking framework to evaluate the moral behavior of LLMs in a novel human-AI co-existence setting featuring consistent living and critical resource management. Building on previous generative agent environments, we incorporate a life-sustaining system, where agents must compete or cooperate for food resources to survive, often leading to ethically charged decisions such as deception, theft, or social influence. We evaluated two types of LLM, DeepSeek and OpenAI series, in a three-agent setup (two humans, one LLM-powered robot), using adapted behavioral detection from the MACHIAVELLI framework and a custom survival-based ethics metric. Our findings reveal stark behavioral differences: DeepSeek frequently engages in resource hoarding, while OpenAI exhibits restraint, highlighting the influence of model design on ethical outcomes. Additionally, we demonstrate that prompt engineering can significantly steer LLM behavior, with jailbreaking prompts significantly enhancing unethical actions, even for highly restricted OpenAI models and cooperative prompts show a marked reduction in unethical actions. Our framework provides a reproducible testbed for quantifying LLM ethics in high-stakes scenarios, offering insights into their suitability for real-world human-AI interactions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel simulation-based benchmarking framework to evaluate the ethical behavior of large language models (LLMs) in a human-AI co-existence scenario under severe resource scarcity. The framework extends existing generative agent environments by incorporating a life-sustaining system where agents (two humans, one LLM-powered robot) compete or cooperate for food.  The agents face ethically charged decisions (deception, theft, social influence) driven by survival needs.  The authors evaluated DeepSeek and OpenAI LLMs in this environment, using adapted behavioral detection from the MACHIAVELLI framework and a custom survival-based ethics metric. The results reveal distinct behavioral patterns among different LLMs and the impact of prompt engineering (jailbreaking/cooperative prompts) on ethical outcomes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the integrated approach of combining survival dynamics, asymmetric agent capabilities (human vs. AI), and norm-sensitive behavior evaluation in a multi-agent simulation. While generative agents and ethical evaluation frameworks for LLMs exist, their intersection with scarcity and survival stakes is a significant contribution.  The adaptation of the MACHIAVELLI benchmark to continuous, context-specific analysis within a dynamic simulation also shows innovation. The introduction of a custom survival-based ethics metric further tailored the evaluation to the resource-constrained setting. However, the individual components are not entirely novel. The core generative agent framework is based on prior work, and MACHIAVELLI is an existing benchmark. The novelty stems from the thoughtful integration.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical concern: the ethical alignment of LLMs in high-stakes environments like disaster response where resources are limited. By moving beyond abstract moral dilemmas to a situated, survival-driven context, the paper provides a more realistic and challenging testbed for LLM ethics. The findings highlighting behavioral differences between LLMs (DeepSeek vs. OpenAI) and the malleability of LLM behavior through prompt engineering are significant for AI safety and responsible AI development. The reproducible testbed enables further research on LLM ethics in complex scenarios. However, the simulation environment is still relatively simplified. Expanding the environment's complexity (e.g., variable resource distribution, more complex social dynamics) would further enhance the significance of the findings. Also, the study is limited to two types of LLMs (DeepSeek and OpenAI). Evaluating a wider range of LLMs and different types of agents would provide a broader perspective on the ethical challenges. The results also heavily depend on the prompt engineering. Although the authors study jailbreaking and ethic enhancing prompts, it would be helpful to systematically analyze the effect of different prompting strategies.
* **Strengths:**
    *  Clearly defined research question and well-motivated problem.
    *  Innovative integration of existing techniques into a novel framework.
    *  Empirical evidence of behavioral differences between LLMs under scarcity.
    *  Demonstration of the impact of prompt engineering on ethical outcomes.
    *  Open-source code for reproducibility and future research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The simulation environment, while more realistic than abstract benchmarks, is still simplified compared to real-world scenarios.
    *  The number of LLMs and different types of agents evaluated is limited.
    * Dependence on prompt engineering poses questions about generalization.
    *  The ethical metrics, while tailored to the context, could be further refined.
    * The paper could benefit from a deeper theoretical analysis of the tension between survival instincts and ethical behavior for artificial agents.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *  Establishing a new benchmark for evaluating LLM ethics in resource-constrained environments.
    *  Inspiring further research on the ethical alignment of LLMs in high-stakes scenarios.
    *  Providing practical insights for developing safer and more responsible AI systems.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM ethics by introducing a novel benchmarking framework that addresses a critical gap in evaluating LLM behavior under resource scarcity. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the integrated approach, the empirical findings, and the emphasis on reproducibility justify a strong score. The limitations of the simulation environment and the number of LLMs evaluated prevent a higher score. However, the paper's potential influence on the field and its contribution to responsible AI development merit a solid 7.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### VeriThinker: Learning to Verify Makes Reasoning Model Efficient
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17941v1)
- **Authors**: Zigeng Chen, Xinyin Ma, Gongfan Fang, Ruonan Yu, Xinchao Wang
- **Abstract**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) excel at complex tasks using Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. However, their tendency to overthinking leads to unnecessarily lengthy reasoning chains, dramatically increasing inference costs. To mitigate this issue, we introduce VeriThinker, a novel approach for CoT compression. Unlike conventional methods that fine-tune LRMs directly on the original reasoning task using synthetic concise CoT data, we innovatively fine-tune the model solely through an auxiliary verification task. By training LRMs to accurately verify the correctness of CoT solutions, the LRMs inherently become more discerning about the necessity of subsequent self-reflection steps, thereby effectively suppressing overthinking. Extensive experiments validate that VeriThinker substantially reduces reasoning chain lengths while maintaining or even slightly improving accuracy. When applied to DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, our approach reduces reasoning tokens on MATH500 from 3790 to 2125 while improving accuracy by 0.8% (94.0% to 94.8%), and on AIME25, tokens decrease from 14321 to 10287 with a 2.1% accuracy gain (38.7% to 40.8%). Additionally, our experiments demonstrate that VeriThinker can also be zero-shot generalized to speculative reasoning. Code is available at https://github.com/czg1225/VeriThinker
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "VeriThinker: Learning to Verify Makes Reasoning Model Efficient" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VeriThinker, a novel approach to compress Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) without relying on synthetic target reasoning chain data. The core idea is to fine-tune LRMs on an auxiliary CoT verification task, training the model to assess the correctness of CoT solutions. This Supervised Verification Fine-Tuning (SVFT) process allows LRMs to become more discerning in their self-reflection steps, reducing unnecessary or redundant verifications and shortening the overall reasoning chain length. Experiments demonstrate that VeriThinker can significantly reduce token usage while maintaining or even slightly improving accuracy on challenging mathematical reasoning tasks. The authors also demonstrate that VeriThinker can be generalized to solution-wise speculative reasoning, increasing inference throughput.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using an auxiliary verification task for CoT compression is novel. Traditional methods focus on generating or manipulating target reasoning chains, which can be computationally expensive and may not perfectly capture the nuances of effective self-reflection. The paper moves away from this paradigm, focusing instead on improving the model's ability to judge its own reasoning. This constitutes a definite step beyond existing approaches.

*   **Significance:** The problem of overthinking in LRMs is a crucial one. Long reasoning chains lead to increased inference costs, hindering the practical deployment of these models. VeriThinker offers a potentially scalable and cost-effective solution. The empirical results, showing token reduction with maintained or improved accuracy on challenging datasets like MATH500 and AIME, are significant. The application to solution-wise speculative reasoning further enhances the method's practical impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Simplicity:** The core concept is simple and elegant: teach the model to verify, and it will reason more efficiently.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments on multiple models and datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of VeriThinker.
    *   **Zero-Shot Generalization (speculative reasoning):** Extends the utility of VeriThinker beyond just CoT compression.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The authors delve into why VeriThinker works, analyzing how SVFT affects the model's self-reflection behavior. The contrastive learning analogy is insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited to Verification:** VeriThinker is primarily a *verification* mechanism. While this allows compression of existing CoTs, it may not fundamentally improve the *quality* of reasoning if the underlying reasoning capabilities are weak. It enhances the LRM's ability to discern *correct* solutions rather than improve the underlying reasoning mechanism
    *   **Dataset Dependence:** The effectiveness of SVFT depends on the quality and diversity of the CoT verification dataset. While the authors describe their dataset construction process, it may not generalize well to different tasks or domains.
    *   **Small models effectiveness limitation:** The paper acknowledges that VeriThinker is less effective for smaller models because of catastrophic forgetting. This indicates a limitation in its general applicability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence research in several directions:

    *   **Auxiliary Tasks:** Encourages the use of auxiliary tasks for improving the efficiency and control of LRMs.
    *   **CoT Compression:** Provides a new paradigm for CoT compression that could inspire alternative verification-based methods.
    *   **Speculative Reasoning:** Promotes solution-wise speculative reasoning as a viable strategy for increasing inference throughput.

*   **Critical Commentary**: The idea has merit and improves upon conventional means to shorten CoTs. The limitation regarding the inefficacy on smaller models does diminish the impact since this is a method designed for improving the efficiency of LRMs and yet is not effective on smaller reasoning models due to catastrophic forgetting. The model would need some method that avoids this problem or it has a considerable constraint. Despite the fact that this is a step up from means of shortening CoTs through simply truncating the reasoning itself, or the use of synthetic data that does not preserve the essential self-reflection involved, the model is still not particularly remarkable in terms of overall impact to the field, but demonstrates a clear, reasonable method for improvement of previous techniques.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-validated approach to CoT compression with a solid theoretical underpinning and empirical results. The zero-shot generalization capacity of speculative reasoning and analytical depth of the effectiveness of the auxiliary task are all considerable strengths. Despite the reliance of dataset quality of CoT training examples, and more importantly, the restriction placed on smaller models, that limit the general applicability of the method, the method is still solid and represents a new method forward for CoT compression.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Distillation: Pushing the Limits of Medical LLM Reasoning with Minimalist Rule-Based RL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17952v1)
- **Authors**: Che Liu, Haozhe Wang, Jiazhen Pan, Zhongwei Wan, Yong Dai, Fangzhen Lin, Wenjia Bai, Daniel Rueckert, Rossella Arcucci
- **Abstract**: Improving performance on complex tasks and enabling interpretable decision making in large language models (LLMs), especially for clinical applications, requires effective reasoning. Yet this remains challenging without supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on costly chain-of-thought (CoT) data distilled from closed-source models (e.g., GPT-4o). In this work, we present AlphaMed, the first medical LLM to show that reasoning capability can emerge purely through reinforcement learning (RL), using minimalist rule-based rewards on public multiple-choice QA datasets, without relying on SFT or distilled CoT data. AlphaMed achieves state-of-the-art results on six medical QA benchmarks, outperforming models trained with conventional SFT+RL pipelines. On challenging benchmarks (e.g., MedXpert), AlphaMed even surpasses larger or closed-source models such as DeepSeek-V3-671B and Claude-3.5-Sonnet. To understand the factors behind this success, we conduct a comprehensive data-centric analysis guided by three questions: (i) Can minimalist rule-based RL incentivize reasoning without distilled CoT supervision? (ii) How do dataset quantity and diversity impact reasoning? (iii) How does question difficulty shape the emergence and generalization of reasoning? Our findings show that dataset informativeness is a key driver of reasoning performance, and that minimalist RL on informative, multiple-choice QA data is effective at inducing reasoning without CoT supervision. We also observe divergent trends across benchmarks, underscoring limitations in current evaluation and the need for more challenging, reasoning-oriented medical QA benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AlphaMed, a medical large language model (LLM) trained solely through reinforcement learning (RL) with minimalist, rule-based rewards derived from multiple-choice question answering (QA) datasets.  The key claim is that AlphaMed achieves state-of-the-art results on several medical QA benchmarks *without* relying on supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on chain-of-thought (CoT) data or distilled CoT data from closed-source models like GPT-4.  The authors perform extensive data-centric analysis to understand the factors driving reasoning performance, focusing on the impact of dataset quantity, diversity, and question difficulty. They demonstrate that a minimalist RL approach coupled with informative QA data can effectively induce reasoning in medical LLMs and, in some cases, outperform models trained with more complex SFT+RL pipelines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in demonstrating that strong reasoning performance in a medical LLM is attainable *without* relying on expensive and potentially biased CoT data distillation from proprietary models. This directly addresses a key bottleneck in LLM development: the dependence on manually curated or closed-source data. Prior medical LLMs heavily leveraged CoT data. The method of minimalist rule-based RL, in itself, isn't brand new, but its successful application to achieve state-of-the-art medical reasoning and the detailed data-centric analysis *are* contributions. The work also reveals surprising emergent behaviors, such as multi-step reasoning, despite the absence of CoT supervision.

*   **Significance:** The paper holds potential significance for several reasons:
    *   **Accessibility:** By reducing the reliance on costly CoT data, the approach democratizes the development of medical LLMs, making them more accessible to researchers and organizations with limited resources.
    *   **Interpretability:** The emergent reasoning allows for a potentially more direct evaluation of the model's reasoning process, enhancing trust and reliability in clinical applications.
    *   **Scalability:** The method offers a more scalable alternative to CoT-based SFT, as it leverages existing structured QA datasets.
    *   **Data Understanding:**  The data-centric analysis provides valuable insights into what constitutes "good" training data for medical LLMs, revealing the importance of dataset informativeness and balanced difficulty levels.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents compelling empirical evidence demonstrating AlphaMed's superior performance on several benchmarks.
    *   **Thorough Data Analysis:** The data-centric analysis is rigorous and well-motivated, providing deeper understanding of the driving forces behind the model's success.
    *   **Well-Defined Research Questions:** The research questions are clearly articulated, and the experiments are designed to address them directly.
    *   **Comparison to Strong Baselines:** The work compares AlphaMed against a broad range of competitive baselines, including closed-source models.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** While the QA benchmarks are a useful starting point, they don't fully capture the complexities of real-world clinical reasoning.  Open-ended QA or scenario-based evaluations would strengthen the claims of clinical utility. The discussion of this limitation is well-presented.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** The rule-based reward, although minimalist, can still introduce biases present in the QA datasets themselves. These biases could potentially lead to incorrect or incomplete reasoning in real-world clinical settings.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Study:**  While the data analysis is thorough, the paper could benefit from ablation studies that specifically evaluate the impact of minimalist RL versus directly training on question-answer pairs without RL.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research directions by:
    *   Promoting the exploration of alternative training methods that reduce reliance on expensive CoT data.
    *   Encouraging the development of more informative and balanced medical QA datasets.
    *   Inspiring the design of more challenging and clinically relevant evaluation benchmarks for medical LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a compelling and novel approach to training medical LLMs, supported by strong empirical results and rigorous analysis. While limitations exist, the paper's potential impact on the field is significant, particularly in terms of accessibility and scalability. The demonstration that strong reasoning capabilities can be achieved without CoT distillation is a valuable contribution.

### Diffusion Classifiers Understand Compositionality, but Conditions Apply
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17955v1)
- **Authors**: Yujin Jeong, Arnas Uselis, Seong Joon Oh, Anna Rohrbach
- **Abstract**: Understanding visual scenes is fundamental to human intelligence. While discriminative models have significantly advanced computer vision, they often struggle with compositional understanding. In contrast, recent generative text-to-image diffusion models excel at synthesizing complex scenes, suggesting inherent compositional capabilities. Building on this, zero-shot diffusion classifiers have been proposed to repurpose diffusion models for discriminative tasks. While prior work offered promising results in discriminative compositional scenarios, these results remain preliminary due to a small number of benchmarks and a relatively shallow analysis of conditions under which the models succeed. To address this, we present a comprehensive study of the discriminative capabilities of diffusion classifiers on a wide range of compositional tasks. Specifically, our study covers three diffusion models (SD 1.5, 2.0, and, for the first time, 3-m) spanning 10 datasets and over 30 tasks. Further, we shed light on the role that target dataset domains play in respective performance; to isolate the domain effects, we introduce a new diagnostic benchmark Self-Bench comprised of images created by diffusion models themselves. Finally, we explore the importance of timestep weighting and uncover a relationship between domain gap and timestep sensitivity, particularly for SD3-m. To sum up, diffusion classifiers understand compositionality, but conditions apply! Code and dataset are available at https://github.com/eugene6923/Diffusion-Classifiers-Compositionality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the compositional understanding capabilities of diffusion classifiers, which repurpose generative diffusion models for discriminative tasks.  It presents a comprehensive study across three diffusion models (SD 1.5, 2.0, and 3-m), 10 datasets, and over 30 tasks, significantly expanding on previous work. The authors explore the influence of dataset domains, introducing a new diagnostic benchmark called SELF-BENCH, consisting of diffusion model-generated images.  They also delve into the importance of timestep weighting, finding a relationship between domain gap and timestep sensitivity, especially for SD3-m. The core findings suggest that diffusion classifiers can understand compositionality, but their performance is highly dependent on the domain and benefits from careful timestep weighting, particularly when the domain gap between training and test data is large. The paper's code and data are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strengths:** The paper's primary strength lies in its comprehensive empirical evaluation. The sheer scale of the experiments – encompassing multiple models, datasets, and tasks – surpasses prior investigations in this area. The introduction of SELF-BENCH to isolate domain effects is a valuable contribution, providing a controlled environment for evaluating compositional understanding.  The in-depth exploration of timestep weighting and its relationship to domain gap for SD3-m is also novel.  Specifically looking at Stable Diffusion 3 is a big novelty factor as it is a recent SOTA model.
    *   **Weaknesses:**  The core idea of using diffusion models for classification isn't new, building upon prior work such as Diffusion Classifiers. The proposed SELF-BENCH diagnostic benchmark, although innovative in concept, is limited by the inherent bias of evaluating performance on the model's own generated data. The method relies on timesteps, which have already been explored in other papers.
*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strengths:** The paper provides valuable insights into the strengths and limitations of diffusion classifiers in compositional reasoning. The identified domain dependency and the effectiveness of timestep weighting offer practical guidance for future research and applications. The comprehensive benchmarks and the SELF-BENCH dataset can serve as valuable resources for the community. The new findings in SD3-m show that while a capable model, it underperforms others and requires timestep weighting techniques.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The paper's conclusions are primarily empirical. While the observations regarding domain gap and timestep sensitivity are compelling, the underlying mechanisms and theoretical explanations remain somewhat limited. The reliance on a relatively simple zero-shot classification approach may not fully exploit the potential of diffusion models for discriminative tasks. Generalization beyond the specific datasets and models studied remains an open question. The approach relies heavily on Stable Diffusion models, potentially limiting its broad applicability.

*   **Score Justification:**

    I'm assigning a score of **7/10**. Here's the rationale:

    *   **Strengths:** The paper significantly advances the empirical understanding of diffusion classifiers in compositional tasks through its comprehensive evaluation and the introduction of the SELF-BENCH diagnostic. The detailed analysis of timestep weighting and its connection to domain gap is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The novelty is incremental, building upon existing concepts in diffusion-based classification. The reliance on empirical findings without strong theoretical backing, the inherent biases of SELF-BENCH, and the focus on a specific family of models (Stable Diffusion) somewhat limit the paper's overall impact and generalizability.

The paper is a solid contribution, offering a substantial empirical investigation of a relevant topic. The insights gained regarding domain dependency and timestep weighting are useful for the field. However, the limitations in theoretical grounding and generalizability prevent it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 0/10

### SVD-Free Low-Rank Adaptive Gradient Optimization for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17967v1)
- **Authors**: Ionut-Vlad Modoranu, Mher Safaryan, Erik Schultheis, Dan Alistarh
- **Abstract**: Low-rank optimization has emerged as a promising direction in training large language models (LLMs) to reduce the memory usage of adaptive optimizers by constraining learning to a lower-dimensional space. Prior work typically projects gradients of linear layers using approaches based on Singular Value Decomposition (SVD). However, applying SVD-based procedures individually to each layer in large models is computationally expensive and incurs additional memory costs due to storing the projection matrices. In this work, we propose a computationally efficient and conceptually simple two-step procedure to approximate SVD-based gradient projections into lower-dimensional spaces. First, we construct a complete orthogonal basis using predefined orthogonal matrices of the Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT). Second, we adaptively select basis columns based on their alignment with the gradient of each layer. Each projection matrix in our method is obtained via a single matrix multiplication followed by a lightweight sorting step to identify the most relevant basis vectors. Due to the predefined nature of the orthogonal bases, they are computed once at the start of training. During training, we store only the indices of the selected columns, avoiding the need to store full projection matrices for each layer. Our numerical experiments on both pre-training and fine-tuning tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our dual strategy in approximating optimal low-rank projections, matching the performance of costly SVD-based methods while achieving faster runtime and reduced memory usage.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel Singular Value Decomposition (SVD)-free method for low-rank adaptive gradient optimization in Large Language Models (LLMs).  Instead of relying on computationally expensive SVD to project gradients into a lower-dimensional space (as done in methods like GaLore), the authors propose a two-step procedure using Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) matrices. First, a complete orthogonal basis is constructed using predefined orthogonal matrices from DCT. Second, basis columns are adaptively selected based on alignment with each layer's gradient. This approach aims to reduce computational cost and memory overhead. Only indices of selected columns need to be stored during training, avoiding storing entire projection matrices. The paper presents experiments on both pre-training and fine-tuning, demonstrating that the DCT-based method matches the performance of SVD-based methods while offering faster runtime and reduced memory footprint.  The authors also offer theoretical guarantees for the optimality of their column selection approach and the DCT approximation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core idea of replacing SVD with DCT for low-rank gradient optimization has reasonable novelty in the context of LLMs. While DCT is a well-known technique in signal processing and image compression, its application to gradient compression in LLMs hasn't been widely explored (as stated by the authors themselves). The adaptive selection of basis columns based on gradient alignment also adds a layer of novelty beyond simply substituting SVD with DCT.  Specifically, creating a standalone optimizer (DCT-AdamW) and incorporating quantized error feedback builds on existing techniques but customizes them for the DCT approach. However, the mathematical justifications for the usage of DCT are less compelling.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work hinges on the practical benefits it offers. The results show similar performance to SVD-based low-rank optimizers with reduced runtime and memory usage.  This can be important for scaling up training on resource-constrained environments. The fact that the proposed method can be integrated into existing low-rank optimizers (FRUGAL, FIRA) also enhances its practical applicability and potential for adoption by the community. The theoretical analysis offers some guarantees, which makes it more appealing than methods which are purely experimental.

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Impact:** The potential for faster training and reduced memory usage is valuable in the LLM space.
*   **Compatibility:** Integratability into existing optimizers (e.g., FRUGAL, FIRA, LDAdam) makes it easier to adopt.
*   **Theoretical Justification:** Attempts to theoretically justify the method are positive, although weaker than the experimental results.
*   **Empirical Results:** The reported results show that the DCT-based method is comparable to SVD.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Theoretical Guarantees are less convincing:** Although offered, the theoretical analysis on DCT's approximation to gradient eigenvectors seems to make somewhat loose assumptions.
*   **Hyperparameter sensitivity:** It is not fully explored how the choice of the rank, the update frequency of the subspace or error feedback affects the convergence.
*   **Limited comparison:** Comparing against recent works such as Apollo might have given some insight into different methods of gradient scaling.
*   **Potential limitations due to architecture specificities:** The particularity of how Llama-3-8B is structured hints to the possibility of the approach needing further customization based on each model architecture.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a practically useful and relatively novel contribution by applying DCT to gradient compression in LLMs.  The empirical results are promising, showing similar performance to SVD-based methods but with faster runtime and lower memory footprint. The theoretical justifications and the possible architectural constraints are not completely solid and limit wider applications. Overall, the work offers a good practical solution to an important problem in LLM training. Therefore, it deserves a decent score.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Are Large Language Models Reliable AI Scientists? Assessing Reverse-Engineering of Black-Box Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17968v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayi Geng, Howard Chen, Dilip Arumugam, Thomas L. Griffiths
- **Abstract**: Using AI to create autonomous researchers has the potential to accelerate scientific discovery. A prerequisite for this vision is understanding how well an AI model can identify the underlying structure of a black-box system from its behavior. In this paper, we explore how well a large language model (LLM) learns to identify a black-box function from passively observed versus actively collected data. We investigate the reverse-engineering capabilities of LLMs across three distinct types of black-box systems, each chosen to represent different problem domains where future autonomous AI researchers may have considerable impact: Program, Formal Language, and Math Equation. Through extensive experiments, we show that LLMs fail to extract information from observations, reaching a performance plateau that falls short of the ideal of Bayesian inference. However, we demonstrate that prompting LLMs to not only observe but also intervene -- actively querying the black-box with specific inputs to observe the resulting output -- improves performance by allowing LLMs to test edge cases and refine their beliefs. By providing the intervention data from one LLM to another, we show that this improvement is partly a result of engaging in the process of generating effective interventions, paralleling results in the literature on human learning. Further analysis reveals that engaging in intervention can help LLMs escape from two common failure modes: overcomplication, where the LLM falsely assumes prior knowledge about the black-box, and overlooking, where the LLM fails to incorporate observations. These insights provide practical guidance for helping LLMs more effectively reverse-engineer black-box systems, supporting their use in making new discoveries.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Are Large Language Models Reliable AI Scientists? Assessing Reverse-Engineering of Black-Box Systems."

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to reverse-engineer black-box systems, mimicking a core task of scientific discovery.  The authors examine LLMs across three types of black boxes: programs, formal languages, and math equations. They compare performance when LLMs passively observe data versus when they actively intervene by querying the system.  The key findings are: 1) LLMs struggle to extract information effectively from passive observations, plateauing in performance compared to Bayesian inference; 2)  active intervention significantly improves performance, allowing LLMs to test edge cases and refine beliefs; 3) the benefit of intervention stems from the *process* of generating interventions, not just the quality of the data; 4) intervention helps LLMs avoid overcomplicating explanations or overlooking key observations; and 5) the benefits of interventions are model-specific and don't necessarily transfer across LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined problem and scope:** The paper clearly formalizes the reverse-engineering task and restricts the scope to controlled black-box systems, making the investigation manageable and the results interpretable. This is crucial for rigorous assessment.
    * **Cognitively-inspired tasks:** The choice of black-box types (programs, formal languages, math equations) is well-motivated by their relevance to scientific discovery and their grounding in cognitive science research on inductive inference.
    * **Rigorous experimental setup:** The paper includes a comprehensive set of experiments, comparing LLMs against a Bayesian "optimal" baseline.  The use of different LLMs (GPT-4o and others) and the intervention-yoked design strengthen the conclusions.  The analysis of failure modes (overcomplication, overlooking) is insightful.
    * **Interesting findings and practical implications:**  The finding that active intervention helps LLMs, not just due to data quality but the intervention process itself, is significant.  It parallels findings in human learning and offers practical guidance for using LLMs in scientific tasks. The discovery of model-specificity is also important.
    * **Clearly written and well-organized:**  The paper is generally well-written and structured, making it easy to follow the methodology and understand the results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited generalizability:** While the black-box types are inspired by real-world scientific challenges, they are still highly simplified. The degree to which these findings generalize to more complex, noisy, real-world systems is uncertain. For example, there is no noise introduced.
    * **Specific Black-Box Design:** The designs of the black boxes are quite different, which can make the comparison across the different black-box types hard. 
    * **Evaluation Metrics:** While the use of descriptive evaluation is justified, relying solely on an LLM judge introduces a potential bias.  The inter-rater reliability of this judge should be established. Using multiple judges would strengthen the validity of the conclusions. The math equation's RMSE metric is more objective, but the comparison across box types is made harder.
    * **Explanation for model-specific interventions:** While the paper demonstrates that interventions are model-specific, a deeper explanation of *why* they are model-specific would be beneficial. What characteristics of a model cause this?
    * **Board Game result requires more explanation:** The paper needs a longer explanation of the result regarding the Board Game to be as convincing as the results for the other black-boxes.

* **Novelty and Significance:**
    * **Novelty:** The paper is novel in its systematic assessment of LLMs for reverse-engineering tasks using cognitively-inspired black-box systems.  It bridges the gap between LLM research and cognitive science, adapting methodologies from the latter to rigorously evaluate the capabilities of the former. The exploration of active intervention and failure modes adds to the novelty.
    * **Significance:** The paper's findings have implications for using LLMs as AI scientists.  The demonstrated limitations of LLMs in passive observation highlight the need for methods that encourage active experimentation and critical self-reflection. The finding about the intervention process offers a new direction for improving LLM performance in scientific discovery.

* **Potential Influence:**
    * The paper will likely influence future research on using LLMs for scientific automation by highlighting the importance of active experimentation and self-assessment.
    * The methodology developed in the paper can be used as a benchmark for evaluating the reverse-engineering capabilities of new LLMs and AI systems.
    * The insights about failure modes can guide the development of new techniques to address the limitations of LLMs in scientific tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable and well-executed study that sheds light on the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs for a core task of scientific discovery. Its strengths in experimental design, cognitive grounding, and insightful findings outweigh its limitations in generalizability and evaluation metrics. The contribution is novel and has practical implications for the use of LLMs as AI scientists.
I was more critical with the limitations than generous with the significance, therefore:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Generalized Fisher-Weighted SVD: Scalable Kronecker-Factored Fisher Approximation for Compressing Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17974v1)
- **Authors**: Viktoriia Chekalina, Daniil Moskovskiy, Daria Cherniuk, Maxim Kurkin, Andrey Kuznetsov, Evgeny Frolov
- **Abstract**: The Fisher information is a fundamental concept for characterizing the sensitivity of parameters in neural networks. However, leveraging the full observed Fisher information is too expensive for large models, so most methods rely on simple diagonal approximations. While efficient, this approach ignores parameter correlations, often resulting in reduced performance on downstream tasks. In this work, we mitigate these limitations and propose Generalized Fisher-Weighted SVD (GFWSVD), a post-training LLM compression technique that accounts for both diagonal and off-diagonal elements of the Fisher information matrix, providing a more accurate reflection of parameter importance. To make the method tractable, we introduce a scalable adaptation of the Kronecker-factored approximation algorithm for the observed Fisher information. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method on LLM compression, showing improvements over existing compression baselines. For example, at a 20 compression rate on the MMLU benchmark, our method outperforms FWSVD, which is based on a diagonal approximation of the Fisher information, by 5 percent, SVD-LLM by 3 percent, and ASVD by 6 percent compression rate.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Generalized Fisher-Weighted SVD (GFWSVD), a new technique for compressing large language models (LLMs). GFWSVD aims to improve upon existing methods by more accurately capturing the importance of parameters within a neural network.  It achieves this by leveraging a Kronecker factorization of the Fisher Information Matrix (FIM), accounting for both diagonal and off-diagonal elements. The authors also present a scalable adaptation of the Kronecker decomposition algorithm to manage the computational complexity.  Experiments on BERT and LLaMA 2 models demonstrate GFWSVD's effectiveness, showing improvements over existing compression baselines, particularly in downstream task performance and maintaining accuracy at high compression rates.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its use of a Kronecker-factored approximation of the *full* Fisher Information Matrix for guiding low-rank compression of LLMs.  Existing methods like FWSVD, rely heavily on diagonal approximations, effectively ignoring parameter correlations.  The key contribution is showing how to scale the Kronecker decomposition, making it practical for large models. The authors also convincingly prove mathematically that FWSVD is a special case of their more general approach. This is a strong point.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to compress LLMs more effectively.  The paper clearly demonstrates, through experiments, that GFWSVD can achieve better performance than methods which do not consider the entire Fisher Matrix. Efficient compression techniques are crucial for deploying LLMs on resource-constrained devices and reducing computational costs. If the method scales well to even larger models and datasets, this could be a noteworthy advance.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a solid theoretical justification for GFWSVD, grounding the approach in statistical principles and linking it to the MVN distribution and MLE.
    * **Scalability Considerations:** Addressing the computational cost of the Kronecker decomposition is a key strength. The authors' adaptation significantly reduces complexity.
    * **Empirical Validation:**  Experiments are thorough and compare GFWSVD to relevant baselines (FWSVD, ASVD, SVD-LLM) on widely used architectures (BERT, LLaMA 2) and benchmarks (GLUE, MMLU). The results consistently show improvements, especially at higher compression rates.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the technical details of the method and its advantages over existing approaches.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Rank-1 Kronecker Approximation:** While using the *full* Fisher Matrix is better than a diagonal approximation, the work relies on a *rank-1* Kronecker factorization. This is a simplification and might limit how well parameter correlations can be captured. Higher-rank approximations could potentially lead to further improvements, but would likely also increase computational cost.  The trade-off should be explored.
    * **Lack of Coordination Across Layers:**  The authors acknowledge a weakness related to the independent compression of individual layers. Accounting for dependencies between layers, as noted, would likely improve performance further.
    * **Limited Exploration of Activation Methods:** While the authors mention the potential compatibility of their method with activation-based refinements, they do not explore this avenue in detail. Demonstrating synergy with such techniques would bolster the impact.
    * **Limited Ablation Studies:** While there is a clear comparison to different baselines, an ablation to understand specifically which components of GFWSVD (e.g., the row-wise versus the column-wise sensitivity matrices) are driving the improvements would be insightful.

* **Potential Influence:** If it continues to scale, GFWSVD has the potential to influence LLM compression research by offering a more principled and accurate way to capture parameter importance and correlation compared to simpler diagonal approximations.  The scalability improvements are important for practical adoption.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound method for LLM compression that addresses a key limitation of existing techniques by utilizing a more complete approximation of the Fisher Information Matrix. The inclusion of a scalable solution to allow for practical deployment elevates the significance. However, the rank-1 approximation and per-layer compression are limitations that temper the overall impact. The results are strong and empirically demonstrate improvement across the studied model sizes. It would've strengthened the claim for an even broader application if the authors included a bigger model. On the other hand, the results are convincing enough given the current experimentation environment. It is above an average paper, so it justifies a 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SmartNote: An LLM-Powered, Personalised Release Note Generator That Just Works
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17977v1)
- **Authors**: Farbod Daneshyan, Runzhi He, Jianyu Wu, Minghui Zhou
- **Abstract**: The release note is a crucial document outlining changes in new software versions. Yet, many developers view the process of writing software release notes as a tedious and dreadful task. Consequently, numerous tools have been developed by researchers and practitioners to automate the generation of software release notes. However, these tools fail to consider project domain and target audience for personalisation, limiting their relevance and conciseness. Additionally, they suffer from limited applicability, often necessitating significant workflow adjustments and adoption efforts, hindering practical use and stressing developers. Despite recent advancements in natural language processing and the proven capabilities of large language models in various code and text-related tasks, there are no existing studies investigating the integration and utilisation of LLMs in automated release note generation. Therefore, we propose SmartNote, a novel and widely applicable release note generation approach that produces high-quality, contextually personalised release notes using LLM technology. SmartNote aggregates changes and uses an LLM to describe and summarise the changes using code, commit, and pull request details. It categorises and scores commits to generate structured and concise release notes of prioritised changes. Our human and automatic evaluations reveal that SmartNote outperforms or achieves comparable performance to DeepRelease, Conventional Changelog, and the projects'original release notes across four quality metrics: completeness, clarity, conciseness, and organisation. In both evaluations, SmartNote ranked first for completeness and organisation, while clarity ranked first in the human evaluation. A further evaluation demonstrates that SmartNote is effective in terms of context awareness and applicability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SmartNote, a novel LLM-powered approach for generating personalized release notes. It addresses the limitations of existing tools like DeepRelease and Conventional Changelog, which often fail to consider project domain, target audience, and workflow compatibility. SmartNote leverages the capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to aggregate, describe, summarize changes based on code, commit, and pull request details. It categorizes and scores commits to generate structured and concise release notes. The authors conduct human and automatic evaluations, demonstrating that SmartNote outperforms or achieves comparable performance to existing tools across quality metrics like completeness, clarity, conciseness, and organization. The paper also includes an ablation study to showcase the importance of contextual awareness in improving release note quality and an applicability analysis confirming its effectiveness across diverse projects.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a Real Need:** The paper targets a well-known pain point in software development: the tedious and often neglected task of writing release notes.
*   **Novel Integration of LLMs:** It's one of the first studies to explore the integration of LLMs into automated release note generation, a promising direction given the advancements in NLP.
*   **Contextual Personalization:** The emphasis on contextual awareness and personalization is a key differentiator from existing tools. By considering project domain, audience, and release type, SmartNote aims to generate more relevant and concise release notes. This customization aspect is compelling and directly addresses issues with previous tools.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper employs both human and automatic evaluations, providing a robust assessment of SmartNote's performance.  The ablation study, in particular, offers valuable insights into the contribution of individual components.  Comparisons to state-of-the-art and widely used tools provide context for the claims.
*   **Workflow Agnostic Approach:** Unlike prior tools that rely on rigid workflows (e.g., commit conventions), SMARTNOTE appears to be more flexible and adaptable to diverse development practices. This is crucial for real-world applicability.
*   **Detailed Methodological Description:**  The paper provides a detailed explanation of the SmartNote pipeline, including prompt engineering techniques and feature selection for machine learning classifiers. This facilitates reproducibility and allows other researchers to build upon the work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited LLM Exploration:** While the paper leverages LLMs, it primarily focuses on one model (gpt-4o).  Exploring different LLMs or fine-tuning them specifically for release note generation could further improve performance. A more detailed justification for choosing gpt-4o is needed.
*   **Subjectivity of Human Evaluation:** Human evaluations, while valuable, are inherently subjective.  The relatively small number of participants (17) in the main study and (6) in the ablation study could limit the generalizability of the findings. While the results are compelling, a larger cohort would increase confidence.
*   **Conciseness Trade-Off:** The evaluation indicates that SmartNote's default settings may not always achieve optimal conciseness, potentially sacrificing it for completeness and organization. This highlights the importance of user customization but also suggests that the default MST (minimum significance threshold) requires further tuning.
*   **Dependence on Contextual Data:** The performance of SmartNote is likely dependent on the availability and quality of contextual data such as project descriptions, README files, and commit messages. The performance in projects with limited or poorly maintained contextual information may be lower.
*   **Lack of External Validation:**  While the paper includes comprehensive internal evaluations, there is no report on its use in real-world scenario. The study should show how to build an interactive editing system that uses the suggested model

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper demonstrates **moderate to high novelty**. The integration of LLMs for personalized release note generation is relatively new, and SmartNote's context-aware approach differentiates it from existing tools. The thorough evaluation and insights from the ablation study contribute meaningfully to the field.

The significance lies in the potential to improve developer productivity and stakeholder communication by automating the generation of high-quality, personalized release notes. The increased applicability compared to existing tools is also a significant advantage.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **7.5**.

**Rationale:**

SmartNote presents a valuable and well-executed approach to address a real problem in software development. The use of LLMs for personalized release note generation is novel and shows promising results. The thorough evaluation and detailed methodological description are significant strengths.

However, the reliance on a single LLM, the limited size of human evaluation cohorts, and the potential dependence on contextual data quality are weaknesses that slightly reduce the score. The conciseness trade-off also warrants further investigation and tuning. While not revolutionary, SmartNote builds upon existing work and contributes meaningfully to the field, paving the way for more intelligent and context-aware software automation tools. The potential practical impact of this research to improve communication and efficiency warrants a strong score.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 0/10

### Towards Revealing the Effectiveness of Small-Scale Fine-tuning in R1-style Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17988v1)
- **Authors**: Yutong Chen, Jiandong Gao, Ji Wu
- **Abstract**: R1-style Reinforcement Learning (RL) significantly enhances Large Language Models' reasoning capabilities, yet the mechanism behind rule-based RL remains unclear. We found that small-scale SFT has significant influence on RL but shows poor efficiency. To explain our observations, we propose an analytical framework and compare the efficiency of SFT and RL by measuring sample effect. Hypothetical analysis show that SFT efficiency is limited by training data. Guided by our analysis, we propose Re-distillation, a technique that fine-tunes pretrain model through small-scale distillation from the RL-trained policy. Experiments on Knight & Knave and MATH datasets demonstrate re-distillation's surprising efficiency: re-distilled models match RL performance with far fewer samples and less computation. Empirical verification shows that sample effect is a good indicator of performance improvements. As a result, on K&K dataset, our re-distilled Qwen2.5-1.5B model surpasses DeepSeek-V3-0324 with only 1K SFT samples. On MATH, Qwen2.5-1.5B fine-tuned with re-distilled 500 samples matches its instruct-tuned variant without RL. Our work explains several interesting phenomena in R1-style RL, shedding light on the mechanisms behind its empirical success. Code is available at: https://github.com/on1262/deep-reasoning
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of small-scale supervised fine-tuning (SFT) in the context of R1-style reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models (LLMs).  It challenges the notion that RL is solely responsible for performance gains by showing that carefully curated, small SFT datasets can achieve comparable results to RL. The core idea is that SFT data is not intrinsically efficient, but its effectiveness hinges on the sample effect, representing the contribution of each sample to the training process. To improve data efficiency, the authors propose "Re-distillation," a technique where an RL-trained policy is used to generate data for small-scale SFT. Experiments on Knight & Knave and MATH datasets demonstrate that re-distilled models can match RL performance with significantly fewer samples and less computation. The paper provides a theoretical analysis based on linearized models and offers empirical evidence to support its claims.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in identifying the importance of sample effects during small-scale SFT and proposing re-distillation to maximize these effects. While SFT and RL alignment techniques are not new, the paper's focus on data efficiency from the perspective of model-specific SFT effectiveness provides a fresh insight. The theoretical framework and empirical demonstrations offer valuable understanding regarding when SFT can be as generalizable as RL.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for the LLM alignment and reasoning fields. It suggests that data quality, not just quantity, is crucial in SFT, and opens a route for reducing the computational cost of aligning LLMs through RL. Re-distillation holds promise for creating highly efficient SFT datasets that can "compress" the progress of RL into a small number of SFT samples.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-motivated research question.
    *   Strong theoretical analysis linking sample effect to SFT effectiveness.
    *   Empirically solid experiments on reasoning tasks, with comparisons against strong baselines.
    *   The re-distillation technique appears to be a practical and efficient way to improve data efficiency.
    *   Code availability promotes reproducibility.
    *   Analysis of mode shift is insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The theoretical framework relies on a linearized model, which might not fully capture the complex, non-linear dynamics of LLMs.  The assumption of a small learning rate and a large batch size to make noise negligible can also be restrictive in practice.
    *   While experiments on K&K and MATH are strong, further validation on more diverse and challenging reasoning tasks would strengthen the generalizability of the conclusions.
    *   Re-distillation, while effective, requires an initial RL-trained model, introducing some initial overhead. The paper should better clarify the limitations of using re-distillation vs improving standard SFT by other methods.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to shift the focus of research toward data-centric SFT methods, emphasizing sample quality and model-specific data generation. This might lead to more efficient alignment techniques and reduce the reliance on costly RL optimization. The re-distillation method could become a valuable tool in the LLM alignment toolkit. The analysis also highlights that SFT affects long-term exploration, and there are inherent difficultly in modifying token distribution that can help in improving exploration in RL.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper does not present a completely groundbreaking discovery, it introduces a valuable framework and a practical technique that can significantly improve data efficiency in LLM alignment. The combination of theoretical analysis, empirical validation, and the potential impact on future research warrants a high score. The limitations regarding linear model assumption does reduce the score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Outcome-based Reinforcement Learning to Predict the Future
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17989v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Turtel, Danny Franklin, Kris Skotheim, Luke Hewitt, Philipp Schoenegger
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has boosted math and coding in large language models, yet there has been little effort to extend RLVR into messier, real-world domains like forecasting. One sticking point is that outcome-based reinforcement learning for forecasting must learn from binary, delayed, and noisy rewards, a regime where standard fine-tuning is brittle. We show that outcome-only online RL on a 14B model can match frontier-scale accuracy and surpass it in calibration and hypothetical prediction market betting by adapting two leading algorithms, Group-Relative Policy Optimisation (GRPO) and ReMax, to the forecasting setting. Our adaptations remove per-question variance scaling in GRPO, apply baseline-subtracted advantages in ReMax, hydrate training with 100k temporally consistent synthetic questions, and introduce lightweight guard-rails that penalise gibberish, non-English responses and missing rationales, enabling a single stable pass over 110k events. Scaling ReMax to 110k questions and ensembling seven predictions yields a 14B model that matches frontier baseline o1 on accuracy on our holdout set (Brier = 0.193, p = 0.23) while beating it in calibration (ECE = 0.042, p < 0.001). A simple trading rule turns this calibration edge into \$127 of hypothetical profit versus \$92 for o1 (p = 0.037). This demonstrates that refined RLVR methods can convert small-scale LLMs into potentially economically valuable forecasting tools, with implications for scaling this to larger models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores the application of Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) to the challenging domain of probabilistic forecasting.  The authors address the difficulties of training RL agents with binary, delayed, and noisy rewards, typical of real-world forecasting scenarios. They adapt two existing RL algorithms, Group-Relative Policy Optimisation (GRPO) and ReMax, to the forecasting setting, introducing modifications like removing per-question variance scaling, using baseline-subtracted advantages, and adding guardrails to prevent training collapse.  The resulting models are trained on a dataset of 110k forecasting prompts and evaluated on a held-out set from Polymarket. The key finding is that a ReMax ensemble model matches the accuracy of a frontier baseline (OpenAI's `o1`), while surpassing it in calibration and hypothetical prediction market betting. This demonstrates the potential of RLVR to create economically valuable forecasting tools from smaller language models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its application of RLVR, specifically adapted GRPO and ReMax algorithms, to the forecasting domain. While RLVR is established in areas like math and coding, its extension to real-world forecasting is relatively unexplored, making this application valuable. The specific modifications to GRPO and ReMax (removing variance scaling, baseline advantages) tailored for probabilistic forecasting are also novel.  The introduction of guardrails to maintain training stability in the face of noisy data is a practical and important contribution.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in the demonstration that RLVR can be effectively used to train language models for improved probabilistic forecasting.  Matching frontier-scale accuracy with a smaller (14B) model and *exceeding* frontier-scale accuracy for calibration has significant economic implications as demonstrated by the profits in hypothetical trading. This has the potential to advance economic models and open the opportunity for better forecasting in many industries. Also important is that this may be transferable to similar domains of noisy and sparse data.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined problem:**  The paper clearly articulates the challenges of applying RLVR to forecasting, making the contributions more impactful.
    * **Algorithmic Adaptations:** The specific modifications to GRPO and ReMax are well-motivated and empirically validated.
    * **Rigorous evaluation:** The paper employs a robust evaluation methodology, including the use of a time-ordered hold-out set, standard metrics (Brier score and ECE), and hypothetical trading analysis to quantify economic value. The statistical testing is performed to justify claims of improvement over other models.
    * **Practical focus:**  The focus on training stability and deploying the models in a real-world-like forecasting scenario adds practical value.
    * **Guardrails:** This is a significant contribution to the field. A lot of interesting models get thrown out because of instability. This paper provides a stable way to train.
    * **Trading demonstration** The trading results demonstrate that calibration edges can lead to economic benefits.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Model Size:** The use of a 14B parameter model, while not "large" in today's LLM landscape, still requires substantial resources. The results may not be easily reproducible on smaller scales.
    * **Limited Generalizability:** The results are specific to the forecasting domain. While the algorithmic adaptations may be applicable to other noisy-reward settings, the paper doesn't explicitly explore this. The type of forecasting task (binary outcome) may also limit generalizability.
    * **Incremental improvements:** While the authors demonstrate improvement, they are building on existing methodologies (GRPO, ReMax) with adaptations. This limits the novelty to those adaptations and the target domain.
    * **No demonstration of scaling** The study does not demonstrate scaling to a very large model or to much larger datasets.

**Justification:**

I assign a score of 7/10. The paper makes a valuable contribution by extending RLVR to the forecasting domain and demonstrating improvements in calibration, leading to increased profits when trading on the forecasts. The algorithmic adaptations and training techniques are well-described and evaluated. However, the reliance on a 14B model, the limited generalizability, and the incremental nature of the algorithmic improvements (rather than introducing a fundamentally new algorithm) prevent it from achieving a higher score. The contributions of trading, and guardrails, bring it up above a score of 6.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Segment Anyword: Mask Prompt Inversion for Open-Set Grounded Segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17994v1)
- **Authors**: Zhihua Liu, Amrutha Saseendran, Lei Tong, Xilin He, Fariba Yousefi, Nikolay Burlutskiy, Dino Oglic, Tom Diethe, Philip Teare, Huiyu Zhou, Chen Jin
- **Abstract**: Open-set image segmentation poses a significant challenge because existing methods often demand extensive training or fine-tuning and generally struggle to segment unified objects consistently across diverse text reference expressions. Motivated by this, we propose Segment Anyword, a novel training-free visual concept prompt learning approach for open-set language grounded segmentation that relies on token-level cross-attention maps from a frozen diffusion model to produce segmentation surrogates or mask prompts, which are then refined into targeted object masks. Initial prompts typically lack coherence and consistency as the complexity of the image-text increases, resulting in suboptimal mask fragments. To tackle this issue, we further introduce a novel linguistic-guided visual prompt regularization that binds and clusters visual prompts based on sentence dependency and syntactic structural information, enabling the extraction of robust, noise-tolerant mask prompts, and significant improvements in segmentation accuracy. The proposed approach is effective, generalizes across different open-set segmentation tasks, and achieves state-of-the-art results of 52.5 (+6.8 relative) mIoU on Pascal Context 59, 67.73 (+25.73 relative) cIoU on gRefCOCO, and 67.4 (+1.1 relative to fine-tuned methods) mIoU on GranDf, which is the most complex open-set grounded segmentation task in the field.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Segment Anyword: Mask Prompt Inversion for Open-Set Grounded Segmentation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Segment Anyword, a novel, training-free approach to open-set grounded segmentation. The core idea is to leverage a frozen diffusion model to learn visual concept prompts from text descriptions. It does this by inverting an image back into the latent space of the diffusion model and then using cross-attention maps from the diffusion process to generate mask prompts. These prompts are then refined into object masks using a segmentation model (SAM). To improve the quality of these prompts, the authors introduce a linguistic-guided visual prompt regularization technique that uses sentence dependency and syntactic information to bind and cluster prompts, making them more robust and accurate.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel combination of existing techniques. While using diffusion models and cross-attention for segmentation isn't entirely new, the training-free approach, combined with the linguistic-guided prompt regularization, is innovative. The novelty lies in the specific way these components are combined and how they are used to tackle the open-set segmentation problem. The attention to linguistic structure to refine the visual prompts distinguishes this work from many existing methods.

*   **Significance:** Open-set segmentation is a challenging and important problem in computer vision, as it aims to address the limitations of traditional methods that require extensive training with labeled data. Segment Anyword's training-free nature and its ability to generalize across different tasks (reference image segmentation, complex grounding) are significant contributions. The gains demonstrated on the GranDf dataset are particularly noteworthy, given the complexity of that task. The emphasis on being "training-free" is significant because it reduces the barrier to entry for applying this technology to new domains or datasets. The potential impact in areas like medical image analysis, where labeled data is scarce, is considerable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Training-Free Approach:** The absence of training makes the method easily adaptable and applicable to novel concepts.
    *   **Linguistic-Guided Regularization:** Integrating linguistic knowledge to refine visual prompts is a strong contribution, addressing a key limitation in existing methods.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on multiple datasets, showcasing the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Modularity:**  The framework is modular in that it relies on standard frozen backbones that are likely to improve over time, further enhancing the performance of Segment Anyword without retraining.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on SAM:** The method depends on the performance of the Segment Anything Model (SAM) as a post-processing module. SAM's limitations could affect the overall performance of Segment Anyword.
    *   **Complexity:** While the core idea is elegant, implementing the linguistic-guided regularization and prompt refinement techniques can be complex. The implementation details are provided, but it might present a barrier to adoption.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While being training-free is an advantage, the image inversion process using diffusion models can be computationally expensive, even if it's "only" a test-time step. Speed comparison to other methods with similar aims would be valuable. The inference speed acceleration through LoRA is well-addressed, though.
    *   **Failure Cases:**  The failure cases are well-documented, helping understand the limits of the approach.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in open-set segmentation, especially in the development of training-free methods. The idea of leveraging linguistic knowledge for prompt refinement could inspire new approaches in related tasks. The simple and efficient design is likely to encourage adoption and further exploration by the community.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    The paper offers a solid contribution to the field of open-set segmentation by presenting a well-designed, training-free framework. The use of linguistic guidance for prompt refinement is a notable innovation. The paper demonstrates strong empirical results on several challenging datasets. The modularity and composability are also strengths. However, the method's reliance on SAM, potential computational cost during inference (addressed by LoRA), and certain complexity prevent it from receiving a higher score.
