### Knot So Simple: A Minimalistic Environment for Spatial Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18028v1)
- **Authors**: Zizhao Chen, Yoav Artzi
- **Abstract**: We propose KnotGym, an interactive environment for complex, spatial reasoning and manipulation. KnotGym includes goal-oriented rope manipulation tasks with varying levels of complexity, all requiring acting from pure image observations. Tasks are defined along a clear and quantifiable axis of complexity based on the number of knot crossings, creating a natural generalization test. KnotGym has a simple observation space, allowing for scalable development, yet it highlights core challenges in integrating acute perception, spatial reasoning, and grounded manipulation. We evaluate methods of different classes, including model-based RL, model-predictive control, and chain-of-thought reasoning, and illustrate the challenges KnotGym presents. KnotGym is available at https://github.com/lil-lab/knotgym.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Knot So Simple: A Minimalistic Environment for Spatial Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KNOTGYM, a novel interactive environment for evaluating spatial reasoning and manipulation capabilities of AI agents. The environment focuses on knot manipulation tasks, where an agent must transform a rope into a desired topological configuration (knot) based on visual observation and applying forces. KNOTGYM allows for controlling task complexity based on the number of knot crossings, creating a measurable generalization ladder.  The authors evaluate several RL methods and prompting approaches on the environment, highlighting the challenges it poses to current AI techniques, particularly in tasks like tying or converting knots where precise spatial reasoning is required.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of an environment specifically designed to test *spatial reasoning* with deformable objects is novel. While environments exist for deformable object manipulation, KNOTGYM's focus on *topological goals* (Gauss codes) based on *visual input*, as opposed to coordinate-wise distance, adds a layer of abstraction and complexity. The use of knot theory to define complexity and a generalization ladder is also a strong point. This is a significant departure from typical robotics environments that focus on precise control towards coordinates which are very easy to optimize for.
*   **Significance:**  KNOTGYM offers a valuable benchmark for evaluating spatial reasoning capabilities. The challenge posed by KNOTGYM arises from the need to: (1) have an acute perception of the crossings of the rope; (2) develop a continuous space reasoning (3D); and (3) deal with a large search space due to the knot equivalence problem. The paper effectively demonstrates that contemporary RL and prompting methods struggle with the more complex tasks. The existence of this benchmark can therefore encourage development of new techniques for solving tasks of spatial reasoning, especially where abstract reasoning is required.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear and well-defined environment:** KNOTGYM is clearly presented with detailed specifications.
    *   **Measurable complexity:** The use of knot crossings as a complexity measure provides a clear benchmark.
    *   **Focus on abstract spatial reasoning:** The environment moves beyond simple coordinate manipulation, requiring agents to understand and reason about topological properties.
    *   **Accessible:** The authors provide open-source implementation which is important to further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simulator fidelity:** As mentioned in the paper, the use of MuJoCo may limit the fidelity of the simulation and the potential for scaling to very complex knots.
    *   **Limited exploration of other methods:** While the evaluation includes a decent range of approaches, exploring other methods, especially those incorporating pre-trained world models, could strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Lack of causal generalization evaluation:** The paper mentions this direction for future work, but including some preliminary results on causal generalization would enhance the paper's impact.

*   **Potential Influence:** KNOTGYM has the potential to become a standard benchmark in the field of spatial reasoning and manipulation. Its focus on abstraction and measurable complexity should drive the development of new AI techniques that go beyond simple imitation or optimization.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**  The paper provides a valuable novel environment and initial benchmark. While there is some room for expanding the set of evaluated methods and future work direction, the combination of topological reasoning, visual perception, and measurable complexity makes KNOTGYM a significant contribution to the field and should encourage new research. This makes it rate high among others in the field. The weaknesses mentioned limit the score, and the paper could reach a higher score if the future work is considered and the limitations addressed.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Contrastive Distillation of Emotion Knowledge from LLMs for Zero-Shot Emotion Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18040v1)
- **Authors**: Minxue Niu, Emily Mower Provost
- **Abstract**: The ability to handle various emotion labels without dedicated training is crucial for building adaptable Emotion Recognition (ER) systems. Conventional ER models rely on training using fixed label sets and struggle to generalize beyond them. On the other hand, Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong zero-shot ER performance across diverse label spaces, but their scale limits their use on edge devices. In this work, we propose a contrastive distillation framework that transfers rich emotional knowledge from LLMs into a compact model without the use of human annotations. We use GPT-4 to generate descriptive emotion annotations, offering rich supervision beyond fixed label sets. By aligning text samples with emotion descriptors in a shared embedding space, our method enables zero-shot prediction on different emotion classes, granularity, and label schema. The distilled model is effective across multiple datasets and label spaces, outperforming strong baselines of similar size and approaching GPT-4's zero-shot performance, while being over 10,000 times smaller.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a contrastive distillation framework to transfer emotion recognition knowledge from large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4 to smaller, more compact models.  The core idea is to use GPT-4 to generate rich, descriptive emotion annotations for text samples, effectively providing a more nuanced and expressive form of supervision than traditional fixed emotion label sets. This data is then used to train a BERT-based model using a contrastive learning objective, aligning text samples and their generated emotion descriptions in a shared embedding space.  The resulting distilled model can then perform zero-shot emotion recognition across various datasets, label spaces (including previously unseen labels), granularity levels, and tasks (classification and regression).  The authors demonstrate that their distilled model performs competitively with similar-sized baselines and approaches the zero-shot performance of the much larger GPT-4, while being significantly smaller.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates incremental novelty, but leverages existing techniques. The primary novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques: using LLM-generated descriptive labels for emotion distillation in a contrastive framework.  Prior work has explored zero-shot ER, LLMs for ER, contrastive learning, and descriptive emotion labels individually. However, the holistic approach and its application to flexible zero-shot learning across varied label spaces are a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practically relevant problem: adapting ER models to diverse application-specific needs without requiring task-specific data collection and retraining. The proposed method offers a potential path toward building more flexible and generalizable ER systems. Furthermore, the ability to create compact ER models from LLM supervision is important, as it offers a way to leverage the knowledge encoded in LLMs in resource-constrained settings.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical and Scalable:** The distillation approach is practically appealing because it leverages readily available LLMs without extensive human annotation effort.
    *   **Flexibility:** The distilled model's ability to generalize across different label spaces and granularities is a significant advantage.
    *   **Performance:** The experiments demonstrate competitive performance compared to baselines. The fact that a relatively small model can approach GPT-4's zero-shot ER performance is encouraging.
    *   **Interpretability:** The emotion space probing provides some insight into the model's learned representations and contributes to the interpretability of the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on LLM Quality:** The quality of the distilled model depends heavily on the quality and biases of the LLM used for generating annotations.  This introduces a potential source of bias and limits the generalizability of the distilled knowledge to the biases inherent in the source LLM.
    *   **Evaluation Limitations:** While the zero-shot evaluation is comprehensive, it's important to note that some baselines may have access to external resources (label definitions) or may have been pre-trained on datasets with emotion related data. Also, the threshold calibration is somewhat less than a truly zero-shot setting.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** A deeper analysis of the types of errors made by the distilled model would provide more insight into its limitations and guide future improvements.
    *   **Comparison in one dataset is skewed:** The performance comparisons on GoEmotions is not fully controlled, as models differ in the additional information available during training.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more adaptable and deployable ER systems. The approach of distilling knowledge from LLMs using descriptive annotations could be applied to other NLP tasks. Furthermore, the findings encourage further research into building dedicated emotion representation spaces.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper combines existing techniques in a novel way and demonstrates promising results, there are some limitations that need to be considered. The reliance on LLM-generated labels raises concerns about potential biases and limits the model's generalizability. In addition, further analysis into failure modes will greatly improve understanding the model. The limitations in evaluation settings with the baseline also limits the score. Overall, the approach shows strong potential, and it does offer a valuable contribution to the field of emotion recognition, while there still remains significant room for future development in the areas discussed.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### RestoreVAR: Visual Autoregressive Generation for All-in-One Image Restoration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18047v1)
- **Authors**: Sudarshan Rajagopalan, Kartik Narayan, Vishal M. Patel
- **Abstract**: The use of latent diffusion models (LDMs) such as Stable Diffusion has significantly improved the perceptual quality of All-in-One image Restoration (AiOR) methods, while also enhancing their generalization capabilities. However, these LDM-based frameworks suffer from slow inference due to their iterative denoising process, rendering them impractical for time-sensitive applications. To address this, we propose RestoreVAR, a novel generative approach for AiOR that significantly outperforms LDM-based models in restoration performance while achieving over $\mathbf{10\times}$ faster inference. RestoreVAR leverages visual autoregressive modeling (VAR), a recently introduced approach which performs scale-space autoregression for image generation. VAR achieves comparable performance to that of state-of-the-art diffusion transformers with drastically reduced computational costs. To optimally exploit these advantages of VAR for AiOR, we propose architectural modifications and improvements, including intricately designed cross-attention mechanisms and a latent-space refinement module, tailored for the AiOR task. Extensive experiments show that RestoreVAR achieves state-of-the-art performance among generative AiOR methods, while also exhibiting strong generalization capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the RestoreVAR paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RestoreVAR, a novel generative approach for All-in-One Image Restoration (AiOR). RestoreVAR leverages visual autoregressive modeling (VAR) to achieve state-of-the-art restoration performance with significantly faster inference speeds compared to latent diffusion model (LDM)-based methods. The key contributions include architectural modifications and improvements to the VAR framework, specifically, a cross-attention mechanism for degraded image conditioning and a latent-space refinement module to address the loss of fine details during vector quantization and VAE decoding. Experimental results demonstrate that RestoreVAR achieves superior performance compared to existing generative AIOR methods and strong generalization capabilities on real-world degradations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the adaptation of the VAR architecture for the AIOR task. While VAR itself is a recent development, its application to AIOR, particularly with the specific architectural modifications described (cross-attention for degraded image conditioning, and latent refinement), constitutes a significant innovation. Other AIOR works used VAR in a limited scope, for example for Super Resolution only, or as a feature guide in a non-generative AiOR architecture, which differentiates this work.  The proposed latent refinement module, designed to overcome the limitations of VQVAE in preserving fine details, also represents a novel contribution tailored specifically to the AIOR domain.

**Significance:**  The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in LDM-based AIOR methods: slow inference speed. The reported 10x speedup of RestoreVAR, while maintaining or improving restoration performance, makes generative AIOR more practical for real-time applications. This represents a substantial advance. Further, achieving state-of-the-art generative AiOR performance on top of the speedup makes this a viable alternative to LDMs for the task. The demonstration of strong generalization capabilities enhances the potential practical impact of the method.

**Strengths:**
*   **Speed and Performance Trade-off:** RestoreVAR effectively balances restoration quality with inference speed, addressing a key limitation of LDM-based approaches.
*   **Architectural Innovations:** The cross-attention mechanism and latent refinement module are well-designed to mitigate the specific challenges of applying VAR to the AIOR task.
*   **Extensive Evaluation:** The paper includes comprehensive quantitative and qualitative comparisons against existing AIOR methods, demonstrating the superiority of RestoreVAR.
*   **Generalization:** The model exhibits good generalization capabilities over real-world degradations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on VAE:** The performance of RestoreVAR is inherently tied to the capabilities of the underlying VAE architecture.  While the latent refiner and VAE fine-tuning mitigate this, the dependence is not completely eliminated. It is possible that a more advanced VAE (or alternative compression strategy) could unlock even greater improvements.
*   **Limited Negative Results:** The ablation studies mainly focus on demonstrating the effectiveness of individual components. Including more analysis of architectures and approaches that *didn't* work well could provide further insights and strengthen the paper.
*   **Overly smooth images**: The results in Figure 3 (c) HART exhibit overly textured output, while restoreVAR (d) has less details and might be overly smooth compared to ground truth.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of future AIOR methods by demonstrating the viability of VAR as an alternative to LDMs.  It could encourage further research into optimizing VAR-based architectures for low-level vision tasks and exploring new techniques to improve detail preservation in latent generative models. Furthermore, the novel approaches in addressing the specific limitations of VAE and VQ can be applied to other domains with similar challenges.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 8. The paper presents a novel application of VAR to AIOR, addressing a key performance bottleneck (inference speed) while achieving state-of-the-art generative performance and good generalization. The architectural innovations (cross-attention, latent refiner) are well-motivated and effectively contribute to the overall performance. The paper's weaknesses (VAE dependence, limited negative results) are relatively minor and do not detract significantly from its overall contribution. It has a high potential to influence future research and development in the field.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### LookWhere? Efficient Visual Recognition by Learning Where to Look and What to See from Self-Supervision
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18051v1)
- **Authors**: Anthony Fuller, Yousef Yassin, Junfeng Wen, Daniel G. Kyrollos, Tarek Ibrahim, James R. Green, Evan Shelhamer
- **Abstract**: Vision transformers are ever larger, more accurate, and more expensive to compute. The expense is even more extreme at high resolution as the number of tokens grows quadratically with the image size. We turn to adaptive computation to cope with this cost by learning to predict where to compute. Our LookWhere method divides the computation between a low-resolution selector and a high-resolution extractor without ever processing the full high-resolution input. We jointly pretrain the selector and extractor without task supervision by distillation from a self-supervised teacher, in effect, learning where and what to compute simultaneously. Unlike prior token reduction methods, which pay to save by pruning already-computed tokens, and prior token selection methods, which require complex and expensive per-task optimization, LookWhere economically and accurately selects and extracts transferrable representations of images. We show that LookWhere excels at sparse recognition on high-resolution inputs (Traffic Signs), maintaining accuracy while reducing FLOPs by up to 34x and time by 6x. It also excels at standard recognition tasks that are global (ImageNet classification) or local (ADE20K segmentation), improving accuracy while reducing time by 1.36x.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the LookWhere paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LookWhere, a novel method for efficient visual recognition, especially at high resolutions.  It achieves efficiency by decoupling computation into two parts: a low-resolution "selector" that predicts *where* to look in the high-resolution image, and a high-resolution "extractor" that processes only the selected patches to determine *what* is being seen. The selector and extractor are jointly pretrained using a self-supervised distillation approach from a powerful teacher model (DINOv2) *without* task-specific labels. This "what-where" distillation process trains the selector to predict areas of high visual interest (based on teacher attention maps) and the extractor to produce accurate representations from only the selected patches. The method demonstrates strong performance on standard benchmarks like ImageNet and ADE20K, as well as on high-resolution tasks like traffic sign and bird recognition, significantly reducing FLOPs and inference time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The key novelty lies in the combination of: (1) the *explicit factorization* of visual recognition into "where" and "what" streams with dedicated selector and extractor models; (2) the *joint pretraining* of these components using self-supervised distillation to transfer knowledge about salient regions and accurate representations; (3) the efficiency gained *without* fully processing the high-resolution input. This approach is different from token reduction methods, which prune after computation, or token selection methods, which often require per-task tuning. The what-where distillation is the core novel contribution.

*   **Weaknesses:** While the overall architecture is novel, the individual components (ViTs, attention distillation) are built upon existing techniques. Also, while the paper claims the method is efficient and straightforward to pretrain and finetune, this is based on only 30 epochs of finetuning in their set ups.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** The significance stems from addressing the critical challenge of scaling visual recognition to high-resolution imagery, which is increasingly relevant in many applications (e.g., remote sensing, medical imaging, autonomous driving). By achieving state-of-the-art accuracy-efficiency trade-offs, the paper offers a practical and potentially impactful solution. The transferability of the pretrained selector and extractor to different tasks is a significant advantage. The work also helps bridge the gap between large, expensive self-supervised models and resource-constrained deployment scenarios.

*   **Weaknesses:** The performance gains might be highly dependent on the nature of the task and the spatial sparsity of the information within the image. Also, a more in-depth comparison with a wider range of adaptive computation methods (particularly those that have also explored self-supervision) would strengthen the claims. The reliance on DINOv2 as a teacher also means the approach depends on the continued availability and performance of such large pretrained models, which requires very high compute for the distillation. The ablation studies are also limited in scope. While random selection in Traffic Sign causes large drops in accuracy, this isn't the case on Imagenet. This inconsistency highlights the potential sensitivity to dataset properties that needs to be further explored. A weakness here is that the selector isn't updated in finetuning, and this makes the pre-training setup extremely important to the paper's results.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a compelling approach to efficient visual recognition with clear novelty and practical significance. The "what-where" distillation framework is well-motivated and demonstrated through thorough experimentation. While the individual components are not entirely novel, their combination and the overall system design are original and effective. The paper has the potential to influence future research on adaptive computation and self-supervised learning for computer vision. It shows a practical way to leverage the strengths of large, pretrained models in resource-constrained settings. Although the reliance on pre-training as a core component is a potential weakness, the overall design has merit and can be influential.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### MathEDU: Towards Adaptive Feedback for Student Mathematical Problem-Solving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18056v1)
- **Authors**: Wei-Ling Hsu, Yu-Chien Tang, An-Zi Yen
- **Abstract**: Online learning enhances educational accessibility, offering students the flexibility to learn anytime, anywhere. However, a key limitation is the lack of immediate, personalized feedback, particularly in helping students correct errors in math problem-solving. Several studies have investigated the applications of large language models (LLMs) in educational contexts. In this paper, we explore the capabilities of LLMs to assess students' math problem-solving processes and provide adaptive feedback. The MathEDU dataset is introduced, comprising authentic student solutions annotated with teacher feedback. We evaluate the model's ability to support personalized learning in two scenarios: one where the model has access to students' prior answer histories, and another simulating a cold-start context. Experimental results show that the fine-tuned model performs well in identifying correctness. However, the model still faces challenges in generating detailed feedback for pedagogical purposes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "MathEDU: Towards Adaptive Feedback for Student Mathematical Problem-Solving" explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for assessing student mathematical problem-solving processes and providing adaptive feedback. The authors introduce a new dataset, MathEDU, which extends the existing MathQA dataset by including detailed annotations of student solutions and corresponding teacher feedback. They evaluate the performance of LLMs (specifically Llama3 and GPT-3.5) in three subtasks: answer accuracy assessment, error identification, and feedback generation, using both direct prompting and fine-tuning (LoRA) approaches. The experiments are conducted in two scenarios: one simulating access to a student's prior answer history and another simulating a cold-start context. The results indicate that fine-tuning improves accuracy in identifying correctness but remains limited in generating adaptive feedback.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by creating and releasing the MathEDU dataset.  Existing datasets either lack detailed student problem-solving steps or teacher feedback.  Combining both allows for more nuanced training and evaluation of LLMs for educational applications. The exploration of fine-tuning techniques on Llama3 for these tasks is a reasonable extension of prior work, but not revolutionary in itself. The multi-task and end-to-end fine tuning strategies, while relatively standard, are thoughtfully explored.

*   **Significance:** Automating feedback in math education has the potential to significantly improve online learning. LLMs can provide personalized and immediate assistance, addressing a key limitation of current platforms. The study's findings, while showing that challenges remain, help to understand the limitations of current LLMs and highlight areas for future research. The analysis of the different student problem-solving styles and how they affect the model’s performance is also a significant contribution.  The paper clearly shows the gap between current LLM capabilities and true adaptive tutoring.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The MathEDU dataset is a valuable resource for the community.
    *   The evaluation is thorough, covering multiple LLMs, fine-tuning approaches, and scenarios.
    *   The paper analyzes the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs in each subtask.
    *   The analysis of how student problem-solving styles affects model performance is insightful.
    *   The comparison to the 01-mini model provides valuable insights regarding the capabilities of more sophisticated LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dataset size (4,048 entries) is relatively small, which can limit the effectiveness of fine-tuning smaller models like Llama3 8B. The study does not sufficiently motivate the limited size beyond effort required for annotation.
    *   The paper primarily focuses on evaluating existing LLMs and methods, with limited exploration of new architectures or techniques specifically tailored for educational applications.
    *   The limited analysis of more powerful LLMs such as gpt-4 (only used as an evaluator), Phi-3 or Gemma limits the scope of the evaluation.
    *   The feedback generation performance is still not satisfactory, and the paper lacks a deep dive into why certain feedback patterns work or don’t work.
    *   The study would benefit from inclusion of ablation studies regarding various parts of the dataset, such as the exclusion of the rationale.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence future research in the application of LLMs to math education. The MathEDU dataset will be a valuable resource for researchers.  The analysis provides clear directions for future work, emphasizing the need for better LLMs for adaptive feedback generation. However, the limited novelty in model architectures/training approaches limits the paper's potential for widespread impact.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:** The paper is a solid contribution to the field of AI in education, primarily due to the creation and release of the MathEDU dataset, a valuable resource that fills a gap in existing datasets. The thorough evaluation of LLMs is also a strength. However, the limited dataset size, reliance on standard methods, and the lack of exploration of more advanced LLMs somewhat limit the paper's overall impact and novelty.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Reward Model Generalization for Compute-Aware Test-Time Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18065v1)
- **Authors**: Zeen Song, Wenwen Qiang, Siyu Zhao, Changwen Zheng, Gang Hua
- **Abstract**: External test-time reasoning enhances large language models (LLMs) by decoupling generation and selection. At inference time, the model generates multiple reasoning paths, and an auxiliary process reward model (PRM) is used to score and select the best one. A central challenge in this setting is test-time compute optimality (TCO), i.e., how to maximize answer accuracy under a fixed inference budget. In this work, we establish a theoretical framework to analyze how the generalization error of the PRM affects compute efficiency and reasoning performance. Leveraging PAC-Bayes theory, we derive generalization bounds and show that a lower generalization error of PRM leads to fewer samples required to find correct answers. Motivated by this analysis, we propose Compute-Aware Tree Search (CATS), an actor-critic framework that dynamically controls search behavior. The actor outputs sampling hyperparameters based on reward distributions and sparsity statistics, while the critic estimates their utility to guide budget allocation. Experiments on the MATH and AIME benchmarks with various LLMs and PRMs demonstrate that CATS consistently outperforms other external TTS methods, validating our theoretical predictions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of test-time compute optimality (TCO) in large language models (LLMs) using external test-time reasoning strategies. It focuses on the role of the process reward model (PRM), which scores and selects the best reasoning path from multiple generated paths.  The authors develop a theoretical framework based on PAC-Bayes to analyze how the generalization error of the PRM impacts compute efficiency and reasoning performance.  They show that lower generalization error of the PRM reduces the number of samples needed for correct answers. Motivated by the theoretical analysis, the paper proposes Compute-Aware Tree Search (CATS), an actor-critic framework that dynamically controls search behavior based on reward distributions and sparsity statistics (used as a proxy for PRM generalization error).  Experiments on MATH and AIME benchmarks demonstrate that CATS consistently outperforms other external TTS methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has decent novelty, mainly for formulating an end-to-end reinforcement learning approach to improve TCO with provable guarantees. It goes beyond existing empirical studies by providing a theoretical framework. The use of parameter sparsity as a proxy for generalization error is a reasonably practical, although not revolutionary, idea.  The CATS architecture, while based on A2C, is tailored to the specific problem of dynamic compute allocation in external TTS.  The formulation of the inference process as an MDP and the use of the actor-critic network to control search hyperparameters seem like a novel contribution. Prior works relied on empirically hand-engineered methods.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in several aspects:
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The PAC-Bayes analysis offers a rigorous way to analyze and understand the impact of PRM generalization error, which has been previously underexplored. Providing such theoretical bounds is important.
    *   **Practical Algorithm (CATS):**  CATS provides a practical method to dynamically adapt the search process, potentially leading to more efficient and accurate reasoning. The idea of adapting compute based on proxy signals for reward model accuracy is practically valuable.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The experiments on MATH and AIME provide evidence that CATS is effective across different LLMs and PRMs. This provides empirical support for the theoretical predictions, strengthening the paper's claims.
    *   **Addresses a relevant problem:** As models grow in size, TCO (test-time compute optimality) becomes a relevant problem for deployment, making it a significant challenge in the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical grounding for the proposed method.
    *   A practical and principled method (CATS) for dynamic compute allocation.
    *   Comprehensive experiments on standard benchmarks with different LLMs and PRMs.
    *   Addresses an important and practical problem (TCO).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The sparsity proxy for generalization error, while practical, might not be the most accurate representation of the PRM's generalization capabilities. Other more sophisticated metrics could be explored.
    *   The theoretical analysis makes certain assumptions that may not hold in practice (e.g., independence of errors). It is unclear how sensitive the conclusions are to these assumptions.
    *   While the performance gain from CATS is evident, there is scope for further analysis of the learned actor network: what actions are being chosen under what conditions? What are the failure cases?
    *   The specific design choices within CATS (e.g., the reward function, the exact network architectures) could be further justified or explored with ablation studies in the appendix.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research on external TTS and dynamic compute allocation strategies. The theoretical framework could be extended to other types of reward models or settings. CATS could serve as a baseline for future work on adaptive reasoning control. The use of parameter sparsity as a proxy for generalization error could be adopted in other contexts.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper demonstrates a solid contribution to the field. The theoretical framework, the CATS algorithm, and the experimental results are all well-executed and provide a significant advance over existing methods. The weaknesses, like the reliance on sparsity as a proxy for generalization, and the limited explanation of the actor, and limited ablations, prevent it from achieving a higher score. Overall, the paper offers a valuable combination of theoretical insights and practical solutions for a relevant and pressing problem.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Emergence of Hebbian Dynamics in Regularized Non-Local Learners
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18069v1)
- **Authors**: David Koplow, Tomaso Poggio, Liu Ziyin
- **Abstract**: Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) has emerged as a remarkably effective learning algorithm, underpinning nearly all state-of-the-art machine learning models, from large language models to autonomous vehicles. Despite its practical success, SGD appears fundamentally distinct from biological learning mechanisms. It is widely believed that the biological brain can not implement gradient descent because it is nonlocal, and we have found little (if any) experimental evidence for it. In contrast, the brain is widely thought to learn via local Hebbian learning principles, which have been seen as incompatible with gradient descent. In this paper, we establish a theoretical and empirical connection between the learning signals of neural networks trained using SGD with weight decay and those trained with Hebbian learning near convergence. We show that SGD with regularization can appear to learn according to a Hebbian rule, and SGD with injected noise according to an anti-Hebbian rule. We also provide empirical evidence that Hebbian learning properties can emerge in a network with weight decay from virtually any learning rule--even random ones. These results may bridge a long-standing gap between artificial and biological learning, revealing Hebbian properties as an epiphenomenon of deeper optimization principles and cautioning against interpreting their presence in neural data as evidence against more complex hetero-synaptic mechanisms.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the relationship between Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) with regularization (weight decay and noise injection) and Hebbian/anti-Hebbian learning, traditionally seen as distinct from gradient-based methods. The core claim is that under certain conditions (near convergence, with weight decay), SGD can give rise to learning dynamics that *resemble* Hebbian learning. Conversely, injecting noise into the SGD process makes it behave more like anti-Hebbian learning. This suggests that Hebbian-like properties observed in neural data might be epiphenomena of deeper optimization principles, rather than definitive evidence against more complex, non-local mechanisms.  The paper supports these claims with theoretical arguments and empirical results from simulations on CIFAR-10 and synthetic datasets, using MLPs and transformers. The paper analyzes transient phases and the effects of varying regularization techniques and initialization scales.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea that Hebbian-like behavior might emerge from more complex optimization dynamics is interesting and potentially significant.  While previous work has explored connections between gradient descent and Hebbian learning (e.g., through contrastive Hebbian learning), this paper's focus on the role of regularization (weight decay and noise) in shaping learning dynamics and its claim that almost *any* learning rule can resemble Hebbian learning near stationarity adds novelty.  However, the exact nature of the "emergence" could be better defined. Is it merely a correlation of update directions, or something deeper?

*   **Significance:** If the paper's claims hold, it has significant implications for how we interpret neural data and model biological learning.  It suggests that the presence of Hebbian-like plasticity should not be considered prima facie evidence against gradient-based or other non-local optimization methods in the brain. Instead, Hebbian behavior could be a convenient, approximate description of a more complex, underlying process. This shifts the focus towards understanding the *optimization objectives* and constraints that shape learning in the brain, rather than solely focusing on the synaptic update rules. This could lead to new interpretations of neural activity, and new avenues for research in both neuroscience and machine learning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem statement:** The paper addresses a long-standing debate about the relationship between artificial and biological learning.
    *   **Theoretical justification:** The paper provides a relatively simple, yet insightful, theoretical argument based on the balance between expansive and contractive forces during learning.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The simulations provide evidence supporting the theoretical claims across a range of architectures, datasets, and learning rules. The exploration of the impact of hyperparameters (weight decay, learning rate, batch size) helps solidify the message.
    *   **Exploration of transient dynamics:** Consideration of transient phases is a nice touch, adds richness to the analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Overly Broad Claims:** The claim that *any* learning rule will look Hebbian/anti-Hebbian with regularization is a strong statement that might be difficult to prove in full generality.
    *   **Limited Model Complexity:**  The models used in the simulations, while diverse, are still relatively small and simple compared to the complexity of the brain. The scalability of these findings to much larger and more complex models needs further investigation.
    *   **Interpretation of "Emergence":** The paper shows a correlation between update directions. Does the "emergent Hebbian" behavior truly contribute to representation learning or is it simply a byproduct? More analysis showing the impact on downstream tasks would be beneficial.
    *   **Specificity of Biological Plausibility:** While the paper addresses biological learning, the connections to specific neurobiological findings could be strengthened. Linking the theoretical findings more explicitly to observed neural phenomena (e.g., specific plasticity mechanisms, specific brain regions) would increase the impact. The paper acknowledges this, but more explicit connections and discussion would be valuable.

*   **Overall:** The paper presents a compelling argument that has the potential to reshape thinking about the relationship between artificial and biological learning. The novelty lies in emphasizing the role of regularization in shaping learning dynamics and questioning the interpretation of Hebbian behavior as evidence against gradient-based optimization. While the paper has some weaknesses, the insights are interesting and well-supported with initial findings.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper introduces a novel perspective on a significant problem. The theoretical arguments are sound, and the empirical evidence is supportive. However, the generality of the claims, the relative simplicity of the models, and the need for more concrete links to neurobiological observations limit the paper's immediate impact and justifies the 7 rather than a higher score. While important, the paper seems to be proposing a "re-evaluation" of existing findings, rather than discovering something entirely novel in and of itself. More work needs to be done to establish the specific implications for understanding and modeling biological learning.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Extended Inductive Reasoning for Personalized Preference Inference from Behavioral Signals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18071v1)
- **Authors**: Jia-Nan Li, Jian Guan, Wei Wu, Rui Yan
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated significant success in complex reasoning tasks such as math and coding. In contrast to these tasks where deductive reasoning predominates, inductive reasoning\textemdash the ability to derive general rules from incomplete evidence, remains underexplored. This paper investigates extended inductive reasoning in LLMs through the lens of personalized preference inference, a critical challenge in LLM alignment where current approaches struggle to capture diverse user preferences. The task demands strong inductive reasoning capabilities as user preferences are typically embedded implicitly across various interaction forms, requiring models to synthesize consistent preference patterns from scattered signals. We propose \textsc{AlignXplore}, a model that leverages extended reasoning chains to enable systematic preference inference from behavioral signals in users' interaction histories. We develop \textsc{AlignXplore} by combining cold-start training based on synthetic data with subsequent online reinforcement learning. Through extensive experiments, we demonstrate that \textsc{AlignXplore} achieves substantial improvements over the backbone model by an average of 11.05\% on in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks, while maintaining strong generalization ability across different input formats and downstream models. Further analyses establish best practices for preference inference learning through systematic comparison of reward modeling strategies, while revealing the emergence of human-like inductive reasoning patterns during training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Extended Inductive Reasoning for Personalized Preference Inference from Behavioral Signals" addresses the challenge of personalized preference inference in Large Language Models (LLMs). It argues that current LLM alignment methods struggle to capture diverse user preferences and proposes ALIGNXPLORE, a model that uses extended reasoning chains to infer user preferences from their interaction histories. ALIGNXPLORE is trained in two stages: first with synthetic data generated by a teacher model to bootstrap reasoning capabilities, and then fine-tuned using reinforcement learning to refine the model's preference inference and reasoning coherence. The paper evaluates ALIGNXPLORE on both in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks, demonstrating improvements over existing methods, strong generalization, and robustness. The authors also analyze different reward modeling strategies and highlight the progressive development of inductive reasoning capabilities during training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to personalized preference inference by explicitly focusing on extended inductive reasoning. While the individual components (synthetic data generation, reinforcement learning) are not entirely new, their combination and application to the preference inference task, especially with the emphasis on reasoning chains, is a significant contribution. The analysis of different reward functions is also a valuable addition. The work provides the first systematic investigation of extended inductive reasoning within the context of LLM personalization.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses a critical limitation of current LLM alignment methods, which tend to focus on general helpfulness and harmlessness rather than individual user preferences. By improving personalized preference inference, this work has the potential to make LLMs more useful and engaging for individual users. Also, the focus on explicitly inferring *preferences* rather than treating behavioral signals as black-box inputs provides a more interpretable and controllable approach to LLM personalization. The focus on interpretability and systematic reasoning processes constitutes a notable advance. The analyses provide insights into the dynamics of reward modeling and the progressive development of inductive reasoning skills, paving the way for more effective alignment strategies.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed experimental setup with comprehensive evaluation metrics.
    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating the effectiveness of ALIGNXPLORE.
    *   Valuable insights into reward modeling strategies and the development of inductive reasoning.
    *   Thorough analysis of generalization ability and robustness.
    *   Addresses ethical considerations of user privacy.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The synthetic data generation process relies on another (teacher) LLM, potentially introducing biases. More detailed analysis of the synthetic data quality and diversity would be beneficial.
    *   Evaluation relies heavily on offline metrics, although online evaluations were performed. A more comprehensive real-world user study would further validate the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   While the paper emphasizes the importance of extended reasoning chains, the analysis of these chains (e.g., visualizing or quantifying the complexity of the reasoning process) is limited.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field of LLM alignment and personalization. The focus on extended inductive reasoning provides a promising direction for future research. The analysis of reward modeling strategies and the insights into the development of inductive reasoning will be valuable for practitioners. The open-source model and training framework will facilitate further research in this area. The method will also be applicable to other tasks involving inductive reasoning.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper's score is based on the combination of its novelty, significance, and strengths. The paper is not entirely groundbreaking in its individual techniques, synthetic data and RL have been utilized extensively. However, the novel combination of these methods to tackle preference inference and the explicit emphasis on interpretable reasoning chains give it a degree of originality. The significance of the work lies in its potential to improve LLM personalization and overcome limitations of existing alignment methods. The strengths related to experimental design, results, and analysis support the significance.

The identified weaknesses (reliance on synthetic data and offline evaluation) are significant but don't negate the overall contribution. Addressing these points in future research would further strengthen the approach. The impact statement is appreciated and reflects the ethical considerations.

### Deep Video Discovery: Agentic Search with Tool Use for Long-form Video Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18079v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoyi Zhang, Zhaoyang Jia, Zongyu Guo, Jiahao Li, Bin Li, Houqiang Li, Yan Lu
- **Abstract**: Long-form video understanding presents significant challenges due to extensive temporal-spatial complexity and the difficulty of question answering under such extended contexts. While Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated considerable advancements in video analysis capabilities and long context handling, they continue to exhibit limitations when processing information-dense hour-long videos. To overcome such limitations, we propose the Deep Video Discovery agent to leverage an agentic search strategy over segmented video clips. Different from previous video agents manually designing a rigid workflow, our approach emphasizes the autonomous nature of agents. By providing a set of search-centric tools on multi-granular video database, our DVD agent leverages the advanced reasoning capability of LLM to plan on its current observation state, strategically selects tools, formulates appropriate parameters for actions, and iteratively refines its internal reasoning in light of the gathered information. We perform comprehensive evaluation on multiple long video understanding benchmarks that demonstrates the advantage of the entire system design. Our DVD agent achieves SOTA performance, significantly surpassing prior works by a large margin on the challenging LVBench dataset. Comprehensive ablation studies and in-depth tool analyses are also provided, yielding insights to further advance intelligent agents tailored for long-form video understanding tasks. The code will be released later.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Deep Video Discovery (DVD), an agentic system designed for long-form video understanding.  DVD addresses the challenges of processing information-dense, hour-long videos by segmenting them into shorter clips and employing an agent with a set of search-centric tools.  Unlike previous video agent approaches that rely on manually designed workflows, DVD emphasizes agent autonomy.  The agent leverages a Large Language Model (LLM) to reason about its current observation state, strategically selecting tools (Global Browse, Clip Search, Frame Inspect) and their parameters to gather relevant information.  The system is evaluated on several long-video benchmarks, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance on LVBench, particularly with the aid of transcripts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to long-form video understanding through an autonomous agentic framework. The key novelty lies in the *agent's ability to dynamically plan and execute search strategies* using a diverse set of tools across multiple granularities. This contrasts with previous approaches that often rely on pre-defined, rigid workflows, limiting their adaptability to complex queries. The design of the search-centric toolset (Global Browse, Clip Search, Frame Inspect) is also a notable contribution.
    *   _Limitations to novelty:_ Agentic search and tool use are *not* entirely new concepts in the broader field of AI or even video processing (as the related works section acknowledges). However, the specific application of these techniques, the autonomous nature of the agent, and the design of the toolset tailored for long-form videos demonstrate incremental novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge: effectively understanding hour-long videos. The demonstrated improvements on the challenging LVBench dataset, with substantial gains over existing methods, highlight the practical significance of the work.  The use of transcripts to further boost performance is also noteworthy, suggesting the potential for multimodal information fusion. The detailed ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component of the proposed framework.
    *   _Limitations to significance:_ While the improvements on LVBench are significant, the gains on other datasets, such as Video MME and LongVideoBench, are more modest. This suggests that the DVD agent might be particularly well-suited to the characteristics of LVBench, and its generalizability to all long-form video understanding tasks requires further investigation. Also, the use of GPT-4.1 raises concerns about reproducibility, as access to and the behavior of proprietary LLMs can change over time.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed agentic framework with autonomous tool selection.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple benchmarks.
    *   Detailed ablation studies and insightful analysis of agent behavior.
    *   The improvements over the previous SOTA are substantial, especially on the LVBench dataset.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on powerful, proprietary LLMs (GPT-4.1), raising concerns about reproducibility and accessibility.
    *   Modest gains on some benchmarks, suggesting limited generalizability to all types of long-form video understanding tasks.
    *   The use of audio transcripts requires additional pre-processing and may not be applicable in all scenarios.
    *   Computational overhead due to the iterative reasoning and search processes.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging further research into autonomous agentic systems for video understanding. The design of the search-centric toolset and the analysis of agent behavior can provide valuable insights for developing more efficient and effective video agents.  The work also highlights the importance of multimodal information fusion (e.g., audio and visual) for long-form video understanding.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I would assign this paper a score of **7.5**. While the use of agentic search and tool use is not entirely novel in itself, the *specific implementation for long-form video and the emphasis on agent autonomy* represents a valuable contribution. The significant performance improvements on LVBench and the detailed analysis of agent behavior are compelling. However, the *reliance on proprietary LLMs, the modest gains on some benchmarks, and the computational overhead* limit the overall impact and generalizability of the work. It’s a strong, well-executed paper that addresses a crucial problem and makes substantial progress, but the practical limitations and dependence on closed-source technology prevent a higher score.

### Stable Reinforcement Learning for Efficient Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18086v1)
- **Authors**: Muzhi Dai, Shixuan Liu, Qingyi Si
- **Abstract**: The success of Deepseek-R1 has drawn the LLM community's attention to reinforcement learning (RL) methods like GRPO. However, such rule-based 0/1 outcome reward methods lack the capability to regulate the intermediate reasoning processes during chain-of-thought (CoT) generation, leading to severe overthinking phenomena. In response, recent studies have designed reward functions to reinforce models' behaviors in producing shorter yet correct completions. Nevertheless, we observe that these length-penalty reward functions exacerbate RL training instability: as the completion length decreases, model accuracy abruptly collapses, often occurring early in training. To address this issue, we propose a simple yet effective solution GRPO-$\lambda$, an efficient and stabilized variant of GRPO, which dynamically adjusts the reward strategy by monitoring the correctness ratio among completions within each query-sampled group. A low correctness ratio indicates the need to avoid length penalty that compromises CoT quality, triggering a switch to length-agnostic 0/1 rewards that prioritize reasoning capability. A high ratio maintains length penalties to boost efficiency. Experimental results show that our approach avoids training instability caused by length penalty while maintaining the optimal accuracy-efficiency trade-off. On the GSM8K, GPQA, MATH-500, AMC 2023, and AIME 2024 benchmarks, it improves average accuracy by 1.48% while reducing CoT sequence length by 47.3%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of overthinking in large language models (LLMs) trained with reinforcement learning (RL), specifically when using the GRPO method.  GRPO, while effective, tends to encourage excessively long chains of thought (CoTs). Recent attempts to address this with length penalties during RL training can lead to instability and premature collapse of training.  The paper proposes GRPO-λ, a modification to GRPO that dynamically adjusts the reward strategy.  It monitors the correctness ratio within groups of query-sampled completions and switches between length-penalized rewards (when correctness is high) and standard 0/1 outcome rewards (when correctness is low, prioritizing reasoning ability).  The experimental results on various benchmarks demonstrate improved training stability, a better trade-off between accuracy and CoT length, and significant reduction in sequence length.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of dynamically adjusting the reward strategy based on the group-wise correctness ratio *is* novel. While length penalties are not new, the adaptive mechanism addresses a crucial shortcoming of indiscriminate length penalization, which is the potential for premature training collapse. The paper clearly identifies and tackles a specific problem with existing approaches.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a practical issue in RL-based training of LLMs for reasoning. Stabilizing the training process allows for more effective and efficient reasoning without sacrificing accuracy. LLM training is computationally intensive, so improving the stability and convergence properties is very valuable. The results demonstrate a meaningful improvement in sequence length reduction without a decrease in accuracy and potentially an accuracy boost. This represents a step toward more efficient LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of instability associated with length penalties in RL for LLMs.
    *   **Simple and Effective Solution:** GRPO-λ is a relatively simple modification to GRPO, making it easy to implement and integrate into existing training pipelines.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The method is rigorously evaluated on a range of challenging benchmarks (GSM8K, GPQA, MATH-500, AMC 2023, and AIME 2024), providing strong evidence for its effectiveness.
    *   **Analysis:** The paper presents a thoughtful analysis of the behavior of GRPO-λ compared to other approaches, highlighting the trade-offs between accuracy and efficiency. The inclusion of examples in Figure 4 illustrating differences between approaches on GSM8k is beneficial.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the empirical results are compelling, a more detailed theoretical analysis of why GRPO-λ stabilizes training would strengthen the paper. A mathematical explanation of how this dynamic reward adjustment prevents the degradation of reasoning capacity when penalizing length in early stages may provide greater clarity to the reader.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** The paper mentions setting λ equal to 20%. It would be beneficial to explore and discuss the sensitivity of GRPO-λ to the choice of λ. A small parameter study showing performance with different values of λ is needed to assess the robustness of the method. This information may be useful to readers in selecting appropriate hyperparameters for their own use cases.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** More ablation studies could be performed. It is important to measure the individual contributions of different components. For instance, what is the performance impact if only the efficiency priority optimization or accuracy priority optimization are used? It would also be useful to measure the contribution of the soft-clip sigmoid function, which is claimed to standardize and smooth length deviations.

*   **Potential Impact:**  The paper can influence the design of RL training methods for LLMs. GRPO-λ is a practical and readily adoptable technique that can improve the efficiency and stability of training.  It could potentially lead to further research into adaptive reward strategies for LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and empirically validated method (GRPO-λ) to address a key challenge in RL training for LLMs. The results convincingly demonstrate improved training stability and a better trade-off between accuracy and CoT length. It's a well-written and easy-to-understand paper that tackles a relevant problem. The experimental results are thorough and reproducible. Despite some lack of deeper analysis and limited parameter studies, it makes a valuable contribution to the field, and its core idea is both simple and effective.

### Data Mixing Can Induce Phase Transitions in Knowledge Acquisition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18091v1)
- **Authors**: Xinran Gu, Kaifeng Lyu, Jiazheng Li, Jingzhao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are typically trained on data mixtures: most data come from web scrapes, while a small portion is curated from high-quality sources with dense domain-specific knowledge. In this paper, we show that when training LLMs on such data mixtures, knowledge acquisition from knowledge-dense datasets, unlike training exclusively on knowledge-dense data (arXiv:2404.05405), does not always follow a smooth scaling law but can exhibit phase transitions with respect to the mixing ratio and model size. Through controlled experiments on a synthetic biography dataset mixed with web-scraped data, we demonstrate that: (1) as we increase the model size to a critical value, the model suddenly transitions from memorizing very few to most of the biographies; (2) below a critical mixing ratio, the model memorizes almost nothing even with extensive training, but beyond this threshold, it rapidly memorizes more biographies. We attribute these phase transitions to a capacity allocation phenomenon: a model with bounded capacity must act like a knapsack problem solver to minimize the overall test loss, and the optimal allocation across datasets can change discontinuously as the model size or mixing ratio varies. We formalize this intuition in an information-theoretic framework and reveal that these phase transitions are predictable, with the critical mixing ratio following a power-law relationship with the model size. Our findings highlight a concrete case where a good mixing recipe for large models may not be optimal for small models, and vice versa.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the knowledge acquisition dynamics of Large Language Models (LLMs) when trained on data mixtures containing both web-scraped data and knowledge-dense datasets. The core finding is that knowledge acquisition from knowledge-dense sources doesn't always scale smoothly with model size and mixing ratio but can exhibit phase transitions. Through controlled experiments using synthetic biographies mixed with web data, the authors demonstrate that: 1) above a certain model size, the model abruptly memorizes biographies; and 2) exceeding a critical mixing ratio leads to rapid biography memorization. The authors attribute these phase transitions to a capacity allocation problem, framing it as a knapsack problem where models must optimally allocate capacity across different datasets to minimize loss. They further formalize this intuition using an information-theoretic framework, revealing a power-law relationship between the critical mixing ratio and model size, and propose strategies to enhance knowledge acquisition under low mixing ratios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic identification and analysis of phase transitions in knowledge acquisition within LLMs *specifically concerning data mixtures*.  While the concept of capacity limitations in neural networks is well-established, the paper provides a concrete, quantifiable example in the context of LLM pre-training. It goes beyond simply observing that mixing ratios matter by showing *how* and *why* these transitions occur and showing the critical mixing ratio is related to model size following a power law, rather than a simple linear relation.The information-theoretic framing adds a valuable theoretical foundation.

*   **Significance:**  The paper's findings have significant practical implications for pre-training LLMs.  The observation that good mixing recipes for small models may not be optimal for larger models challenges the common practice of using smaller "proxy models" to inform data curation decisions. The strategies proposed for enhancing knowledge acquisition at low mixing ratios offer potential cost-effective ways to incorporate knowledge-dense data. The predictability of the critical mixing ratios with power laws provide a theoretically grounded guideline in how we mix LLM datasets.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-Controlled Experiments:**  The use of synthetic biographies allows for precise control and quantification of knowledge acquisition, sidestepping the difficulty of evaluating knowledge in unstructured text. This adds rigor to the empirical findings.
    *   **Strong Theoretical Framing:**  The information-theoretic analysis provides a compelling explanation for the observed phase transitions. The knapsack analogy is useful and intuitive.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The study identifies clear practical guidelines for data mixing and highlights a potential pitfall in using small models for data curation.
    *   **Validation Across Datasets:** The paper tests these insights over Wikipedia biographies and synthetic datasets, strengthening its arguments.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Synthetic Data Limitation:**  While the synthetic biography dataset offers control, it is inherently simplified compared to real-world knowledge domains. It's possible the sharpness of the phase transitions is exaggerated by the synthetic data.The generalization to extremely complex knowledge is to be rigorously demonstrated, such as more complex knowledge like mathematics or codes.
    *   **Simplifying Assumptions:** The theoretical analysis relies on assumptions, such as the optimal test loss on web-scraped data following a power law. The sensitivity of the results to these assumptions could be further explored.The relationship between the hyperparameters and the mixing strategy are not fully understood.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence data curation strategies for LLM pre-training, encouraging more careful consideration of mixing ratios and model size. It may also spur further research into understanding capacity allocation and phase transitions in neural networks. It provides a quantifiable insight to how LLMs should mix web and knowledge datasets.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper delivers a novel and well-supported analysis of data mixing strategies in LLM training. Its use of rigorous experimentation, combined with a strong information-theoretic framework, adds valuable new knowledge to the field. The practical implications, particularly the critique of small-model proxies, are significant. While the reliance on synthetic data introduces some limitations, the insights are validated on a real-world task, and the simplifying theoretical assumptions are clearly stated.

### QwenLong-CPRS: Towards $\infty$-LLMs with Dynamic Context Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18092v1)
- **Authors**: Weizhou Shen, Chenliang Li, Fanqi Wan, Shengyi Liao, Shaopeng Lai, Bo Zhang, Yingcheng Shi, Yuning Wu, Gang Fu, Zhansheng Li, Bin Yang, Ji Zhang, Fei Huang, Jingren Zhou, Ming Yan
- **Abstract**: This technical report presents QwenLong-CPRS, a context compression framework designed for explicit long-context optimization, addressing prohibitive computation overhead during the prefill stage and the "lost in the middle" performance degradation of large language models (LLMs) during long sequence processing. Implemented through a novel dynamic context optimization mechanism, QwenLong-CPRS enables multi-granularity context compression guided by natural language instructions, achieving both efficiency gains and improved performance. Evolved from the Qwen architecture series, QwenLong-CPRS introduces four key innovations: (1) Natural language-guided dynamic optimization, (2) Bidirectional reasoning layers for enhanced boundary awareness, (3) Token critic mechanisms with language modeling heads, and (4) Window-parallel inference. Comprehensive evaluations across five benchmarks (4K-2M word contexts) demonstrate QwenLong-CPRS's threefold effectiveness: (1) Consistent superiority over other context management methods like RAG and sparse attention in both accuracy and efficiency. (2) Architecture-agnostic integration with all flagship LLMs, including GPT-4o, Gemini2.0-pro, Claude3.7-sonnet, DeepSeek-v3, and Qwen2.5-max, achieves 21.59$\times$ context compression alongside 19.15-point average performance gains; (3) Deployed with Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct, QwenLong-CPRS surpasses leading proprietary LLMs by 4.85 and 10.88 points on Ruler-128K and InfiniteBench, establishing new SOTA performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "QWENLONG-CPRS: Towards ∞-LLMs with Dynamic Context Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces QWENLONG-CPRS, a context compression framework aimed at enhancing the long-context processing capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). It tackles the challenges of computational overhead during the prefill stage and the "lost in the middle" phenomenon that degrades performance when processing long sequences. The core of QWENLONG-CPRS is a dynamic context optimization mechanism, which enables multi-granularity context compression guided by natural language instructions.  The paper highlights four key innovations: (1) natural language-guided dynamic optimization, (2) bidirectional reasoning layers, (3) a token critic mechanism, and (4) window-parallel inference. Extensive evaluations demonstrate significant context compression, improved performance compared to methods like RAG and sparse attention, and competitive results against proprietary LLMs on long-context benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to long-context management. The dynamic context optimization paradigm, guided by natural language instructions, is a clever way to address both efficiency and the "lost in the middle" problem. The combination of bidirectional reasoning, token critic mechanisms, and window-parallel inference offers a holistic approach. However, similar ideas such as prompt compression, sparse attention and other optimization techniques exist, lowering the distinctiveness of the system.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of QWENLONG-CPRS is significant.  Efficient long-context processing is a crucial area for making LLMs more practical for real-world applications. The reported performance improvements and compression rates are compelling, suggesting that the framework can substantially reduce computational costs and improve the quality of LLM outputs for tasks involving long documents or conversations. The plug-and-play nature is a definite advantage. Claiming state-of-the-art performance can be improved by more in-depth comparison against existing methods, for instance, on other benchmarks like LognBench.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results across various benchmarks and LLMs.
    *   **Plug-and-Play Design:**  The architecture-agnostic integration with different LLMs is a significant strength, simplifying adoption.
    *   **Clear Technical Details:** The paper thoroughly explains the technical details of the proposed framework, including the dynamic context optimization mechanism, bidirectional reasoning layers, token critic mechanism, and window-parallel inference.
    *   **SOTA Results:** Demonstrates new SOTA performance on Ruler-128K and InfiniteBench
    *   **Practical Use Case:** Demonstrates the applicability to real-world applications that require extended context processing abilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The system is relatively complex, involving multiple components and training stages. While the paper provides sufficient detail, replicating the results and implementing the framework might require significant effort.
    *   **Generalization Claims:** The paper makes strong claims about the generalizability of the framework. However, it would be good to see a clear theoretical analysis of when QWENLONG-CPRS is expected to work well, and when it will not.

*   **Justification for Score:**

QWENLONG-CPRS presents an innovative approach to long-context management in LLMs with great impact on the efficiency and performance of such models, resulting in impressive experimental results. The comprehensive evaluations, plug-and-play design, and clear technical details contribute to its value.
However, the framework involves several components. Therefore:
