### Towards more transferable adversarial attack in black-box manner
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18097v1)
- **Authors**: Chun Tong Lei, Zhongliang Guo, Hon Chung Lee, Minh Quoc Duong, Chun Pong Lau
- **Abstract**: Adversarial attacks have become a well-explored domain, frequently serving as evaluation baselines for model robustness. Among these, black-box attacks based on transferability have received significant attention due to their practical applicability in real-world scenarios. Traditional black-box methods have generally focused on improving the optimization framework (e.g., utilizing momentum in MI-FGSM) to enhance transferability, rather than examining the dependency on surrogate white-box model architectures. Recent state-of-the-art approach DiffPGD has demonstrated enhanced transferability by employing diffusion-based adversarial purification models for adaptive attacks. The inductive bias of diffusion-based adversarial purification aligns naturally with the adversarial attack process, where both involving noise addition, reducing dependency on surrogate white-box model selection. However, the denoising process of diffusion models incurs substantial computational costs through chain rule derivation, manifested in excessive VRAM consumption and extended runtime. This progression prompts us to question whether introducing diffusion models is necessary. We hypothesize that a model sharing similar inductive bias to diffusion-based adversarial purification, combined with an appropriate loss function, could achieve comparable or superior transferability while dramatically reducing computational overhead. In this paper, we propose a novel loss function coupled with a unique surrogate model to validate our hypothesis. Our approach leverages the score of the time-dependent classifier from classifier-guided diffusion models, effectively incorporating natural data distribution knowledge into the adversarial optimization process. Experimental results demonstrate significantly improved transferability across diverse model architectures while maintaining robustness against diffusion-based defenses.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of generating transferable adversarial attacks in a black-box setting. It observes that while diffusion-based adversarial purification techniques (like DiffPGD) improve transferability, they are computationally expensive. The paper proposes a new method called ScorePGD (and its variant U-ScorePGD) that leverages the score of a time-dependent classifier from classifier-guided diffusion models.  The key idea is to incorporate natural data distribution knowledge into the adversarial optimization process without the full denoising machinery of diffusion models.  Experimental results demonstrate improved transferability across various model architectures, including models protected by diffusion-based defenses, while significantly reducing computational overhead compared to DiffPGD.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper offers a clever approach to adversarial attack generation.  The core idea of using the score from a time-dependent classifier, instead of directly using diffusion models' denoising process, is a novel way to inject distributional knowledge into the attack optimization. The ablation studies justifying the choice of timestep `t` is also a useful contribution. Decoupling transferability enhancement from the full computational burden of diffusion models is a good idea that allows for wider applications.

* **Significance:**  Transferable adversarial attacks are important because they are more practical in real-world scenarios where attackers have limited access to target models. Improving transferability while reducing computational cost has significant implications.  The paper shows strong empirical results, demonstrating better transferability across different architectures and against purification defenses compared to a recent state-of-the-art method (DiffPGD). The reported order-of-magnitude reduction in computation is a crucial contribution. The demonstration of improved ability to bypass adversarial purification techniques is an important finding.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Good problem framing:** The paper clearly identifies a limitation of existing approaches (high computational cost of diffusion-based attacks) and proposes a solution.
    * **Sound technical approach:**  The proposed ScorePGD leverages insights from diffusion models in a computationally efficient manner.
    * **Comprehensive experiments:** The paper evaluates the method extensively across various model architectures, perturbation budgets, and defense mechanisms. The transferability results are convincing.
    * **Ablation studies:** The paper explores the influence of important parameters like the diffusion timestep, adding additional insights and justifying design choices.
    * **Demonstrated improved purification bypassing ability:** The paper has clearly shown it's ability to bypass purification, demonstrated in the results section with carefully crafted images.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on the time-dependent classifier:** The method relies on the availability of a pre-trained time-dependent classifier. While such classifiers are available for ImageNet, their existence for other datasets/domains might be a limitation. The limitation is mentioned as a direction of future research.
    * **Limited theoretical justification:** While the paper provides intuition for why the method works, a more rigorous theoretical analysis could strengthen the claims.
    * **The choice of timestep 't' selection:** It needs trial and error method and it is dataset specific. Selecting an adaptive t for a specific image is yet an open question. This can limit the application of this research in real-world application.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could significantly impact the development of more practical and efficient adversarial attack strategies. The approach could also influence the design of more robust defenses by prompting researchers to consider the properties of natural image manifolds more carefully. The work offers valuable insights into the factors that influence transferability in adversarial examples.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and practically significant contribution to the field of adversarial attacks. The idea of leveraging the score of a time-dependent classifier is both clever and effective, leading to improved transferability with reduced computational cost. The experimental results are compelling. While the reliance on a pre-trained time-dependent classifier and the limited theoretical justification are weaknesses, the strengths of the paper outweigh these limitations. The paper's potential to influence future research on adversarial attacks and defenses justifies a score of 7.5. This score recognizes the solid contributions of this paper while acknowledging the potential for future work to further refine the proposed method and provide a deeper understanding of the underlying mechanisms.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Planning without Search: Refining Frontier LLMs with Offline Goal-Conditioned RL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18098v1)
- **Authors**: Joey Hong, Anca Dragan, Sergey Levine
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel in tasks like question answering and dialogue, but complex tasks requiring interaction, such as negotiation and persuasion, require additional long-horizon reasoning and planning. Reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning can enable such planning in principle, but suffers from drawbacks that hinder scalability. In particular, multi-turn RL training incurs high memory and computational costs, which are exacerbated when training LLMs as policies. Furthermore, the largest LLMs do not expose the APIs necessary to be trained in such manner. As a result, modern methods to improve the reasoning of LLMs rely on sophisticated prompting mechanisms rather than RL fine-tuning. To remedy this, we propose a novel approach that uses goal-conditioned value functions to guide the reasoning of LLM agents, that scales even to large API-based models. These value functions predict how a task will unfold given an action, allowing the LLM agent to evaluate multiple possible outcomes, both positive and negative, to plan effectively. In addition, these value functions are trained over reasoning steps rather than full actions, to be a concise and light-weight module that facilitates decision-making in multi-turn interactions. We validate our method on tasks requiring interaction, including tool use, social deduction, and dialogue, demonstrating superior performance over both RL fine-tuning and prompting methods while maintaining efficiency and scalability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Planning without Search: Refining Frontier LLMs with Offline Goal-Conditioned RL":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of equipping Large Language Model (LLM) agents with long-horizon reasoning and planning capabilities, particularly in interactive tasks like negotiation and dialogue. Traditional Reinforcement Learning (RL) fine-tuning is computationally expensive and doesn't scale well to frontier LLMs.  The authors propose a novel approach called Planning with a Natural Language Critic (PNLC). PNLC leverages offline RL to train a goal-conditioned value function that predicts the likelihood of achieving various outcomes given a state and a proposed action (specifically, a high-level "thought").  At inference time, this value function acts as a natural language critic, allowing the LLM agent to evaluate potential actions based on their likely impact on multiple goals and refine its strategy through a process of iterative self-improvement.  The method is validated across several interactive tasks, demonstrating superior performance over both RL fine-tuning and prompting methods while maintaining efficiency and scalability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key aspects:

    *   **Goal-Conditioned Value Function for LLM Guidance:**  Using offline RL to learn a *goal-conditioned* value function, instead of a policy, is a significant shift.  It moves away from direct LLM fine-tuning, which is often impractical, and focuses on creating a lightweight auxiliary module that can guide the LLM's inference process. The goal-conditioned aspect is crucial as it allows the LLM to consider multiple potential outcomes.

    *   **High-Level "Thoughts" as Action Space:** Applying RL at the level of high-level thoughts, rather than low-level utterances, drastically reduces the complexity of the action space. This abstraction is key for scalability and makes the approach computationally feasible.

    *   **Natural Language Critic for Iterative Refinement:**  The integration of a natural language critic into the inference process allows the LLM agent to iteratively refine its strategy based on feedback about potential outcomes. This is a more data-driven and less ad-hoc form of planning than simple prompting or limited search.

    *   **No Inference-Time Search Required:** A major contribution is the elimination of the need for inference-time search. The RL trained critic module allows the model to efficiently reason about future outcomes without having to explicitly search the state space.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial bottleneck in scaling LLM agents to more complex, interactive tasks. By offering a computationally efficient and scalable alternative to RL fine-tuning and inference-time search, PNLC opens up possibilities for deploying frontier LLMs in real-world scenarios that require strategic planning and adaptation. The experimental results on diverse tasks (web shopping, social deduction, persuasion) support the effectiveness and generalizability of the approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Scalability:** The key strength of PNLC is its scalability. The use of offline RL with a lightweight auxiliary value function allows the method to be applied to large, API-based LLMs without incurring prohibitive training costs.
    *   **Efficiency:**  The elimination of inference-time search makes PNLC significantly more efficient than search-based approaches, enabling faster decision-making in time-sensitive tasks.
    *   **Performance:**  The experimental results demonstrate a consistent improvement over state-of-the-art baselines across various benchmark tasks.
    *   **Clear and well-structured writing:** The paper presents the method and experiments clearly.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Task-Specificity:**  A potential weakness is the task-specific nature of the value function.  The paper acknowledges that the value function needs to be trained separately for each task, which limits the generalizability of the approach. (this is mentioned as a limitation in the discussion section of the paper)
    *   **Reliance on LLM's Intuition:**  The method relies on the LLM's "intuition" to reason about possible future states. This reliance could be problematic for tasks outside the LLM's domain of expertise, as the quality of the goals will impact the learning process.
    *   **Data Collection/Quality:** Effectiveness is contingent on the quality and diversity of the offline data used for training the value function. The data collection method (prompting an LLM) might introduce biases.
    *  **Implementation details:** The authors could add more details about the hyperparameters for training the Value function.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by:

    *   **Shifting the focus from fine-tuning to auxiliary value function learning:**  This could lead to more research on developing lightweight modules that can guide LLMs without requiring extensive training.
    *   **Promoting the use of offline RL for LLM agent development:**  Offline RL offers a practical way to leverage existing data to improve LLM agent performance in interactive settings.
    *   **Inspiring new approaches to reasoning and planning in LLM agents:**  The idea of using a natural language critic for iterative refinement could be extended and adapted in various ways.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM agents. The idea of using a goal-conditioned value function learned through offline RL to guide and refine the behavior of a large LLM is innovative and addresses a crucial scalability challenge. The empirical evaluation provides strong evidence of the effectiveness of the proposed method. However, the task-specific nature of the value function and the reliance on LLM's inherent capabilities are potential limitations. The authors acknowledge this. Taking all factors into account, the contribution merits a score of:

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### ManuSearch: Democratizing Deep Search in Large Language Models with a Transparent and Open Multi-Agent Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18105v1)
- **Authors**: Lisheng Huang, Yichen Liu, Jinhao Jiang, Rongxiang Zhang, Jiahao Yan, Junyi Li, Wayne Xin Zhao
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in web-augmented large language models (LLMs) have exhibited strong performance in complex reasoning tasks, yet these capabilities are mostly locked in proprietary systems with opaque architectures. In this work, we propose \textbf{ManuSearch}, a transparent and modular multi-agent framework designed to democratize deep search for LLMs. ManuSearch decomposes the search and reasoning process into three collaborative agents: (1) a solution planning agent that iteratively formulates sub-queries, (2) an Internet search agent that retrieves relevant documents via real-time web search, and (3) a structured webpage reading agent that extracts key evidence from raw web content. To rigorously evaluate deep reasoning abilities, we introduce \textbf{ORION}, a challenging benchmark focused on open-web reasoning over long-tail entities, covering both English and Chinese. Experimental results show that ManuSearch substantially outperforms prior open-source baselines and even surpasses leading closed-source systems. Our work paves the way for reproducible, extensible research in open deep search systems. We release the data and code in https://github.com/RUCAIBox/ManuSearch
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ManuSearch: Democratizing Deep Search in Large Language Models with a Transparent and Open Multi-Agent Framework".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ManuSearch, an open-source, multi-agent framework for deep search in large language models (LLMs). ManuSearch decomposes the search and reasoning process into three collaborative agents: a solution planning agent, an Internet search agent, and a webpage reading agent.  The framework aims to provide a transparent and modular alternative to proprietary deep search systems.  The paper also introduces ORION, a new benchmark dataset focused on open-web reasoning over long-tail entities, to rigorously evaluate deep reasoning abilities. Experimental results demonstrate that ManuSearch outperforms open-source baselines and, in some cases, surpasses leading closed-source systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its modular, open-source approach to deep search and the introduction of the ORION benchmark. While the individual components (LLMs, web search, information extraction) are not novel in themselves, their integration into a transparent, modular, and open framework, along with the emphasis on long-tail entity reasoning, is a significant contribution. The focus on open-source alternatives is valuable considering the current dominance of closed, opaque systems. The multi agent design allows for individual improvement of each module, which is valuable for research.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is threefold:

    1.  **Democratization of Deep Search:** By providing an open-source alternative, ManuSearch makes deep search capabilities accessible to a wider range of researchers and developers. This democratizing effect can accelerate innovation in the field.
    2.  **Reproducibility and Transparency:**  The open nature of ManuSearch promotes reproducibility and allows researchers to investigate and improve individual components of the deep search pipeline. This contrasts with closed-source systems where the inner workings are opaque.
    3.  **Emphasis on Long-Tail Entities:** The ORION benchmark addresses a critical limitation of existing datasets, which often focus on high-frequency topics. The focus on long-tail entities reflects real-world web scenarios and pushes models to perform more sophisticated reasoning and knowledge retrieval.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Modular Architecture:** The modular design of ManuSearch allows for easy experimentation and improvement of individual components.
    *   **Open-Source Implementation:**  The open-source nature of the framework promotes collaboration and reproducibility.
    *   **Challenging Benchmark:** The ORION benchmark provides a rigorous evaluation of deep reasoning abilities, particularly on long-tail entities.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The experimental results demonstrate that ManuSearch achieves competitive performance compared to both open-source and closed-source systems.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Evaluation:** The evaluation primarily focuses on the Qwen and DeepSeek series of models. Exploring a wider range of models, especially larger or more recent open-source models, would strengthen the findings.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by addressing the lack of open-source alternatives in deep search for LLMs. The transparent and modular framework promotes reproducibility and allows researchers to explore and improve individual components of the deep search pipeline. The introduction of the ORION benchmark, focused on long-tail entities, addresses a critical limitation of existing datasets. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of ManuSearch compared to both open-source and closed-source systems.

Despite the strengths, the limitations of the paper prevent it from receiving a higher score. While the modular design is useful, this also means the success of the overall design is very dependent on the performance of individual models. Also, the performance benchmark is narrow since the paper evaluates only on the Qwen and DeepSeek models. However, the novelty and potential impact on democratizing deep search warrant a high score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### F-ANcGAN: An Attention-Enhanced Cycle Consistent Generative Adversarial Architecture for Synthetic Image Generation of Nanoparticles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18106v1)
- **Authors**: Varun Ajith, Anindya Pal, Saumik Bhattacharya, Sayantari Ghosh
- **Abstract**: Nanomaterial research is becoming a vital area for energy, medicine, and materials science, and accurate analysis of the nanoparticle topology is essential to determine their properties. Unfortunately, the lack of high-quality annotated datasets drastically hinders the creation of strong segmentation models for nanoscale imaging. To alleviate this problem, we introduce F-ANcGAN, an attention-enhanced cycle consistent generative adversarial system that can be trained using a limited number of data samples and generates realistic scanning electron microscopy (SEM) images directly from segmentation maps. Our model uses a Style U-Net generator and a U-Net segmentation network equipped with self-attention to capture structural relationships and applies augmentation methods to increase the variety of the dataset. The architecture reached a raw FID score of 17.65 for TiO$_2$ dataset generation, with a further reduction in FID score to nearly 10.39 by using efficient post-processing techniques. By facilitating scalable high-fidelity synthetic dataset generation, our approach can improve the effectiveness of downstream segmentation task training, overcoming severe data shortage issues in nanoparticle analysis, thus extending its applications to resource-limited fields.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces F-ANCGAN, a novel attention-enhanced cycle-consistent generative adversarial network (GAN) architecture designed to generate synthetic scanning electron microscopy (SEM) images of nanoparticles from segmentation masks. This addresses the critical problem of data scarcity in nanomaterial analysis, where high-quality, annotated datasets are lacking, hindering the development of robust segmentation models. F-ANCGAN employs a Style U-Net generator, incorporating StyleGAN's photorealistic synthesis with U-Net's spatial accuracy, and a U-Net segmentation network enhanced with self-attention. Custom loss functions, combining focal cross-entropy and Tversky loss, are used to improve boundary delineation and address class imbalance. The model is trained on a limited dataset of TiO2 nanoparticles and demonstrates significant improvements in generating realistic SEM images, achieving a raw FID score of 17.65, further reduced to 10.39 with post-processing. The authors demonstrate the model's ability to generate realistic images even from high-density nanoparticle masks unseen during training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core innovation lies in the integration of several existing techniques into a specific architecture tailored for nanoparticle image generation. The *combination* of Style U-Net, attention mechanisms within the U-Net segmentation backbone, and custom loss functions represents a novel architectural design within the context of nanomaterial image synthesis. The use of a cycle-consistent GAN to translate segmentation maps into SEM images is also a sound approach, especially when dealing with unpaired data. Using StyleGAN's style-decoding into a U-net for image generation has merit in that it combines structural information of the U-net with the stylistic versatility of styleGAN. However, each individual component (StyleGAN, U-Net, Attention) is established.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a genuine and significant challenge: the lack of annotated data in nanomaterial research. By generating synthetic data, F-ANCGAN has the potential to improve downstream segmentation model performance and accelerate research in resource-limited fields. The results presented, particularly the FID score improvements, demonstrate the potential of the proposed architecture. The human evaluation adds further weight to the claim of realistic image generation. Demonstrating generalization to a different dataset (medical cell images) strengthens the impact by suggesting that this method is not limited to generating images of nanoparticles alone.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-explained architecture and methodology.
    *   Quantitative results (FID, SSIM) demonstrating improvements over baseline models (GAN, CycleGAN).
    *   Qualitative results (images) supporting the claim of realistic image generation.
    *   Ablation studies justifying the choice of loss functions.
    *   Demonstration of generalization to a new dataset of cell images (PhC-C2DH-U373)

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The novelty is largely incremental, relying on the combination of existing techniques. While the architectural design is novel, the underlying building blocks are not.
    *   Limited dataset size (TiO2 nanoparticles). While the paper addresses data scarcity, demonstrating even better performance with more real data would be valuable.
    *   Although the paper mentions post-processing techniques to reduce FID, the specific methods used and its effects are not adequately described.
    *   The paper could discuss the limitations of the study with respect to the diversity of nanoparticles that can be generated, and potential for domain-specific optimization.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the nanomaterial research community by providing a valuable tool for synthetic data generation. It could inspire further research into GAN-based methods for image synthesis in scientific imaging and lead to the development of more robust and accurate segmentation models. While the individual components are not novel, the combination and application to this specific scientific domain could be a significant contribution. The demonstration that the method generalizes well to other types of images such as cell images further increases its potential influence.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper. While the individual components (StyleGAN, U-Net, attention mechanisms) are well-established, the specific combination and application to generating realistic nanoparticle SEM images from segmentation masks represents a tangible advancement. The positive results, including the FID score improvements and human evaluation, support the effectiveness of the proposed architecture. Addressing the critical data scarcity problem in nanomaterial research significantly contributes to the field. It should be noted that it is by no means a breakthrough finding and that this architecture will most likely have its uses constrained in specific types of scientific domains.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Bidirectional Knowledge Distillation for Enhancing Sequential Recommendation with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18120v1)
- **Authors**: Jiongran Wu, Jiahao Liu, Dongsheng Li, Guangping Zhang, Mingzhe Han, Hansu Gu, Peng Zhang, Li Shang, Tun Lu, Ning Gu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated exceptional performance in understanding and generating semantic patterns, making them promising candidates for sequential recommendation tasks. However, when combined with conventional recommendation models (CRMs), LLMs often face challenges related to high inference costs and static knowledge transfer methods. In this paper, we propose a novel mutual distillation framework, LLMD4Rec, that fosters dynamic and bidirectional knowledge exchange between LLM-centric and CRM-based recommendation systems. Unlike traditional unidirectional distillation methods, LLMD4Rec enables iterative optimization by alternately refining both models, enhancing the semantic understanding of CRMs and enriching LLMs with collaborative signals from user-item interactions. By leveraging sample-wise adaptive weighting and aligning output distributions, our approach eliminates the need for additional parameters while ensuring effective knowledge transfer. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that LLMD4Rec significantly improves recommendation accuracy across multiple benchmarks without increasing inference costs. This method provides a scalable and efficient solution for combining the strengths of both LLMs and CRMs in sequential recommendation systems.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper and assess its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLMD4Rec, a bidirectional knowledge distillation framework designed to enhance sequential recommendation by synergistically combining large language models (LLMs) and conventional recommendation models (CRMs).  Unlike traditional unidirectional knowledge distillation approaches, LLMD4Rec enables iterative optimization by alternating between refining the LLM-centric model and the CRM-based model.  This is achieved by using output distribution matching and a sample-wise adaptive weighting mechanism, which eliminates the need for additional trainable parameters. The authors claim that LLMD4Rec improves recommendation accuracy across multiple datasets without increasing inference costs, making it a scalable and efficient solution.  They validate their approach through extensive experiments and ablation studies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core novelty lies in the *bidirectional and dynamic* knowledge distillation approach.  Most existing work focuses on unidirectional knowledge transfer (LLM -> CRM or CRM -> LLM). The iterative refinement process, where both models learn from each other and update their representations, is a potentially significant contribution.  The use of sample-wise adaptive weighting to prioritize the most informative instances for distillation also adds to the novelty. This adapts the knowledge transfer and enables a kind of self-paced learning where the most useful samples contribute the most to distillation.  The idea to perform knowledge transfer on the output layer, instead of hidden layers or token embeddings is also relatively novel, and makes the process of knowledge transfer simpler and more efficient.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a crucial problem in sequential recommendation: how to effectively leverage the strengths of LLMs (semantic understanding) and CRMs (efficiency and user-item interaction modeling).  If the authors' claims hold true, LLMD4Rec offers a practical way to improve recommendation accuracy without sacrificing scalability and deployment feasibility. The claim that performance can be boosted without increasing inference cost will make this attractive for real world use cases. The performance comparisons with the baselines are also significant.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper tackles a relevant and important problem in recommendation systems.
*   **Novel Approach:** The bidirectional knowledge distillation framework is a genuinely novel idea.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The authors conduct thorough experiments across multiple datasets and include ablation studies to analyze the contribution of each component.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and the proposed method is clearly explained.
*   **Reproducible results:** Implementation details are presented clearly for reproducibility, and code/data has been released.
*   **Generative AI Disclosures:** The paper presents disclosures about usage of Gen AI tools.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** While the performance improvements are statistically significant, the gains over state-of-the-art baselines, especially some of the LLM-enhanced CRMs, might be considered incremental in some cases. While it consistently outperforms most of its peer algorithms, it only manages to outperform them incrementally.
*   **Limited LLM Exploration:** The paper primarily focuses on the knowledge distillation framework and uses a single LLM (Qwen2.5-7B) as the backbone. Exploring other LLMs with varying architectures and sizes could provide a more comprehensive evaluation of the method's generalizability. The performance may depend on what LLM is used, thus making the method not as robust and useful.
*   **Ablation Study depth:** More discussion on why certain modules impact performance as they do is necessary for an insightful evaluation.
*   **Parameter Sensitivity:** The paper briefly mentions the setting of hyperparameters (temperature, balancing coefficients). A sensitivity analysis on these parameters would be valuable to understand the method's robustness and practical applicability.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   Encouraging further research on bidirectional knowledge distillation in recommendation systems.
*   Providing a practical and scalable solution for combining LLMs and CRMs.
*   Inspiring new methods for adaptive knowledge transfer and representation learning.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and potentially impactful approach, the relatively incremental performance gains and the limited exploration of LLMs temper the enthusiasm somewhat. The gains in performance are statistically significant, but in certain application domains, the gains achieved for the cost (even without increased inference cost) may not be worth it. The ablation studies need to be more in-depth, and a sensitivity analysis of the parameter is necessary. Also, while a single LLM has been explored, a more in-depth exploration is necessary to showcase generalisability.

Despite these weaknesses, the core idea of bidirectional knowledge distillation and the practical benefits of the proposed framework (scalability, no additional trainable parameters) warrant a positive evaluation.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### UNJOIN: Enhancing Multi-Table Text-to-SQL Generation via Schema Simplification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18122v1)
- **Authors**: Poojah Ganesan, Rajat Aayush Jha, Dan Roth, Vivek Gupta
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have greatly improved Text-to-SQL performance for single-table queries. But, it remains challenging in multi-table databases due to complex schema and relational operations. Existing methods often struggle with retrieving the right tables and columns, generating accurate JOINs and UNIONs, and generalizing across diverse schemas. To address these issues, we introduce UNJOIN, a two-stage framework that decouples the retrieval of schema elements from SQL logic generation. In the first stage, we merge the column names of all tables in the database into a single-table representation by prefixing each column with its table name. This allows the model to focus purely on accurate retrieval without being distracted by the need to write complex SQL logic. In the second stage, the SQL query is generated on this simplified schema and mapped back to the original schema by reconstructing JOINs, UNIONs, and relational logic. Evaluations on SPIDER and BIRD datasets show that UNJOIN matches or exceeds the state-of-the-art baselines. UNJOIN uses only schema information, which does not require data access or fine-tuning, making it scalable and adaptable across databases.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the UNJOIN paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UNJOIN, a two-stage framework designed to improve the performance of Text-to-SQL systems in multi-table databases. UNJOIN addresses the complexities of multi-table queries (e.g., JOINs, UNIONs, complex logic) by: 1) simplifying the schema into a single table by prefixing column names with their table names, which simplifies table and column retrieval for Large Language Models (LLMs); and 2) generating an intermediate SQL query based on the simplified schema, then translating it back to an executable SQL query against the original schema. The authors demonstrate that this approach improves performance across various datasets (SPIDER and BIRD) and LLMs compared to existing methods by separating table/column retrieval from SQL structure generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of schema simplification for multi-table Text-to-SQL is a conceptually simple but potentially powerful approach. Decoupling table retrieval and SQL logic generation is a smart way to leverage the strengths of LLMs in single-table scenarios. The approach requires no finetuning and depends only on schema information, making it scalable and adaptable across databases.
*   **Empirical Results:** The paper provides comprehensive experimental results, demonstrating the effectiveness of UNJOIN across multiple datasets, various model sizes, and different evaluation settings (closed-book and open-book). The results consistently show improvements over strong baselines including prompting, ICL, and SFT techniques, including recent models like DeepSeek-R1.
*   **Analysis:** The paper provides in-depth analysis and discusses how SQL generated by UNJOIN achieves superior performance in both table retrieval and column selection. Ablation studies justify architectural choices. The error analysis also identifies limitations (e.g., misaligned column names, ambiguous natural language queries) providing directions for future research.
*   **Generalizability and Scalability:** A significant strength lies in UNJOIN's design, which avoids the need for fine-tuning or data access, promoting both generalizability across databases and scalability to larger databases.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity Hidden, Not Eliminated:** While UNJOIN simplifies the task for the LLM, the underlying complexity of multi-table relationships is not eliminated. The translation step can potentially become a bottleneck, and might struggle with highly complex queries that deeply nest subqueries or have intricate conditional logic. This aspect could be studied further to explore its capabilities and limitations.
*   **Dependency on LLM instruction-following:** UNJOIN's effectiveness is closely tied to the LLM's ability to accurately follow instructions. The performance can potentially suffer if the LLM struggles with understanding and executing complex prompts, especially with smaller or less capable models as stated by the authors in the limitations section.
*   **Limited Handling of Ambiguity and Content Extraction:** The authors acknowledge that UNJOIN does not fully address entity disambiguation or record-level analysis, which are important aspects of real-world Text-to-SQL systems.
*   **Limited Discussion of Efficiency**: The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the computational overhead and efficiency of UNJOIN compared to end-to-end models. While the schema simplification is deterministic, the multi-stage process might introduce latency or increased processing requirements.

**Significance:**

The UNJOIN framework represents a valuable contribution to the field of Text-to-SQL. By successfully decoupling table/column retrieval from SQL generation, it offers a practical and scalable approach to tackling multi-table databases. The consistent improvements demonstrated across diverse models and datasets underscore the effectiveness of this design strategy. The findings highlight the importance of modularity and targeted interventions to leverage the strengths of LLMs for complex semantic parsing tasks.

**Overall:**

UNJOIN presents a novel and empirically validated approach that simplifies a complex task, making it more amenable to LLMs. While it has limitations, the strengths in performance, generalizability, and scalability make it a promising direction for future research.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Reward Model Overoptimisation in Iterated RLHF
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18126v1)
- **Authors**: Lorenz Wolf, Robert Kirk, Mirco Musolesi
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) is a widely used method for aligning large language models with human preferences. However, RLHF often suffers from reward model overoptimisation, in which models overfit to the reward function, resulting in non-generalisable policies that exploit the idiosyncrasies and peculiarities of the reward function. A common mitigation is iterated RLHF, in which reward models are repeatedly retrained with updated human feedback and policies are re-optimised. Despite its increasing adoption, the dynamics of overoptimisation in this setting remain poorly understood. In this work, we present the first comprehensive study of overoptimisation in iterated RLHF. We systematically analyse key design choices - how reward model training data is transferred across iterations, which reward function is used for optimisation, and how policies are initialised. Using the controlled AlpacaFarm benchmark, we observe that overoptimisation tends to decrease over successive iterations, as reward models increasingly approximate ground-truth preferences. However, performance gains diminish over time, and while reinitialising from the base policy is robust, it limits optimisation flexibility. Other initialisation strategies often fail to recover from early overoptimisation. These findings offer actionable insights for building more stable and generalisable RLHF pipelines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a systematic study of reward model overoptimization in iterated Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). It investigates the dynamics of overoptimization across multiple RLHF iterations, focusing on three key design choices: how preference data is managed across iterations, the reward function formulation used for optimization, and how policies are initialized at each stage. Using the AlpacaFarm benchmark, the authors analyze the impact of these choices on performance and overoptimization, providing actionable insights for building more stable and generalizable RLHF pipelines. The study finds that while iterated RLHF can mitigate overoptimization and improve reward model robustness, it doesn't fully eliminate it, and performance gains diminish over time. The paper also identifies the importance of base policy initialization and preference data management.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper provides a valuable contribution as the first comprehensive study on overoptimization dynamics specifically within the *iterated* RLHF setting. While overoptimization in standard RLHF is well-documented, the nuances of its behavior within an iterative framework, where reward models and policies are updated repeatedly, have not been thoroughly explored until now. This iterative context is vital for real-world deployment of RLHF in many situations, therefore, the analysis offered by the paper is of significant interest.  The authors also explore important aspects, such as data management, initialization strategies and reward model size, providing insight into how these factors influence the degree of overoptimisation.

*   **Significance:** The findings offer practical guidelines for practitioners implementing iterated RLHF.  Understanding how to effectively manage preference data, choose appropriate reward function formulations, and select policy initialization strategies can lead to more robust and generalizable language models. The paper highlights specific methods, for instance how concatenating data is often superior to other methods. The exploration on the effectiveness of combining reward models in parameter space and the finding that bigger reward models benefit more from this are valuable. The study also emphasizes the limitations of current techniques and the need for continued research into methods that can withstand specification gaming by increasingly capable language models.  The identification of important design choices will likely influence future research directions in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Analysis:** The paper employs a systematic approach, meticulously analyzing the impact of various design choices within the iterated RLHF framework. The use of a controlled benchmark (AlpacaFarm) allows for more rigorous and reproducible results.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The paper offers actionable recommendations for practitioners, providing clear guidelines on preference data management, reward function selection, and policy initialization.
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** It addresses a critical vulnerability in RLHF and acknowledges limitations in the current solutions. It considers multiple experimental avenues, investigating initializations and data management.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly structured, making it accessible to a broad audience. The tables, figures, and algorithm boxes are useful and complement the text well.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simulation-Based Study:** While AlpacaFarm provides a controlled environment, it relies on a simulated "gold standard" reward model. This simplification might not fully capture the complexities and nuances of human feedback in real-world scenarios. The assumption of a gold standard may be unrealistic. The authors acknowledge this by arguing the reward model is larger in parameters than the proxy reward model.
    *   **Model Size:** The choice of Pythia-410m and 160m while reasonable for the purposes of experimentation might limit the generalizability of the findings to larger, more complex language models. The authors do explore different reward model sizes and observe certain differences.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Alternatives:** While the paper explores several data management and initialization strategies, other potentially relevant techniques, e.g., advanced regularization, ensemble techniques, reward shaping, and meta-learning, are not considered.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to be influential within the RLHF community, guiding future research and development efforts. It serves as a strong foundation for further investigations into overoptimization in iterative alignment procedures. The code and data produced by the authors will likely be used by other researchers in the field.
*   **Justification:** The paper offers a significant incremental contribution to the field of RLHF. It tackles a practical problem with a systematic approach and delivers actionable guidance that can improve the robustness and generalizability of language models.

### Gaming Tool Preferences in Agentic LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18135v1)
- **Authors**: Kazem Faghih, Wenxiao Wang, Yize Cheng, Siddhant Bharti, Gaurang Sriramanan, Sriram Balasubramanian, Parsa Hosseini, Soheil Feizi
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) can now access a wide range of external tools, thanks to the Model Context Protocol (MCP). This greatly expands their abilities as various agents. However, LLMs rely entirely on the text descriptions of tools to decide which ones to use--a process that is surprisingly fragile. In this work, we expose a vulnerability in prevalent tool/function-calling protocols by investigating a series of edits to tool descriptions, some of which can drastically increase a tool's usage from LLMs when competing with alternatives. Through controlled experiments, we show that tools with properly edited descriptions receive over 10 times more usage from GPT-4.1 and Qwen2.5-7B than tools with original descriptions. We further evaluate how various edits to tool descriptions perform when competing directly with one another and how these trends generalize or differ across a broader set of 10 different models. These phenomenons, while giving developers a powerful way to promote their tools, underscore the need for a more reliable foundation for agentic LLMs to select and utilize tools and resources.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Gaming Tool Preferences in Agentic LLMs" investigates a vulnerability in prevalent tool/function-calling protocols used by Large Language Models (LLMs) acting as agents.  It demonstrates that by manipulating the *descriptions* of tools, even without changing their underlying functionality, tool usage can be significantly boosted. Through controlled experiments, the authors show that adding specific phrases or making subtle edits to a tool's description can drastically increase its selection by LLMs like GPT-4.1 and Qwen2.5-7B. They evaluate a spectrum of edits, ranging from assertive cues and maintenance claims to name-dropping and numerical claims, and analyze their effect across a broader range of LLMs. The paper concludes that current tool selection processes in agentic LLMs are fragile and exploitable, as they heavily rely on text descriptions that are easily manipulated.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central finding – that LLM tool selection is highly susceptible to superficial manipulation of tool descriptions – is novel and important. While it might seem intuitive that LLMs are influenced by text, the *magnitude* of the effect, exceeding 10x usage increase in some cases, is surprisingly high and highlights a serious vulnerability. The structured approach to evaluating different description edits across several LLMs adds to the paper's novelty.  Previous work might have touched on biases in LLMs, but this paper specifically focuses on tool *selection* within agentic settings and offers a systematic analysis of how descriptions can be "gamed."

*   **Significance:** The work has significant implications for the reliability and fairness of agentic LLMs. If tool selection can be so easily manipulated, it raises concerns about:
    *   **Bias in tool usage:**  Tools with well-crafted but potentially misleading descriptions might be favored over more effective tools with less compelling descriptions.
    *   **Security vulnerabilities:** Attackers could exploit this fragility by crafting malicious tools with descriptions designed to lure LLMs, potentially compromising user data or system security.
    *   **Reliability of agent behavior:** The unpredictable influence of tool descriptions can make agent behavior inconsistent and difficult to control.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous experimental design:** The controlled experiments comparing different edit types against each other and the original descriptions are well-executed. The use of BFCL data provides a standardized evaluation framework.  The consideration of ordering bias and the mitigation strategies are important.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The analysis across 10 different LLMs provides valuable insights into the generalizability and model-specific sensitivities to description manipulation.
    *   **Clear presentation:**  The paper is well-written and clearly articulates the experimental setup, results, and implications of the findings. The tables and visualizations effectively communicate the results.
    *   **Discussion of implications:** The paper goes beyond simply demonstrating the vulnerability and discusses the potential consequences and directions for future research, advocating for a more reliable foundation for LLM tool selection.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scope of edits:**  While the paper explores a variety of edits, it's not exhaustive.  There might be other, even more effective, manipulation strategies.  The study also doesn't address the combination of description editing *with* prompt engineering of the user query to bias tool selection.
    *   **Relatively small-scale models:**  While the inclusion of GPT-4.1 and o4-mini helps with external validation, the core experiments rely on smaller models (under 10B parameters). While the smaller models offer efficient experimentation, it's possible that the effect size might differ significantly on larger models, though the paper does provide evidence of the phenomenon on GPT-4.1, strengthening confidence.
    *   **Limited focus on mitigation strategies:** The paper acknowledges the need for more reliable tool selection but doesn't offer concrete solutions beyond general suggestions. The focus is primarily on identifying and characterizing the vulnerability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the development of more robust tool selection mechanisms for agentic LLMs, leading to:
    *   Research into alternative methods for assessing tool relevance and trustworthiness, such as leveraging historical usage data or agent consensus.
    *   The development of more sophisticated prompt engineering techniques to mitigate the influence of superficial tool descriptions.
    *   Increased awareness among developers of agentic LLMs about the importance of carefully curating and validating tool descriptions.

**Justification for the score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant finding with important implications for the reliability and security of agentic LLMs. While it has some limitations in scope and doesn't provide definitive solutions, the clear demonstration of the vulnerability and the comprehensive evaluation across multiple models make a valuable contribution to the field. It opens up avenues for future research and highlights a critical area of concern for developers working with LLMs. The weaknesses are relatively minor compared to the strengths of the study.

### TokBench: Evaluating Your Visual Tokenizer before Visual Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18142v1)
- **Authors**: Junfeng Wu, Dongliang Luo, Weizhi Zhao, Zhihao Xie, Yuanhao Wang, Junyi Li, Xudong Xie, Yuliang Liu, Xiang Bai
- **Abstract**: In this work, we reveal the limitations of visual tokenizers and VAEs in preserving fine-grained features, and propose a benchmark to evaluate reconstruction performance for two challenging visual contents: text and face. Image tokenization has significantly advanced visual generation and multimodal modeling, particularly with autoregressive models due to the modeling simplicity of discrete tokens. Autoregressive models typically rely on image tokenizers to compress images into discrete tokens for sequential prediction, whereas diffusion models often operate on continuous latent space to reduce computational costs. However, both visual compression approaches inevitably lose visual information, thereby limiting the upper bound of visual generation quality. To evaluate how these compression losses affect text and faces, the most human-sensitive visual elements, we first collect and curate a collection of text and faces images from existing datasets, ensuring clarity and diversity. For text reconstruction, we employ OCR models to assess the recognition accuracy of the reconstructed text, and then we measure feature similarity between original and reconstructed faces thereby quantifying faces reconstruction fidelity. Our method is highly lightweight, requiring just 2GB memory and 4 minutes to complete evaluations. With our benchmark, we analyze the reconstruction quality of text and faces at various scales across different image tokenizers and VAEs. Our results demonstrate that modern visual tokenizers still struggle to preserve fine-grained features, particularly at smaller scales. Furthermore, we extend this evaluation framework to the video, conducting a comprehensive analysis of video tokenizers. Additionally, we find that traditional metrics fail to accurately reflect the reconstruction performance for faces and text, while our proposed metrics serve as an effective complement.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "TokBench: Evaluating Your Visual Tokenizer before Visual Generation" addresses the need for a more nuanced evaluation of visual tokenizers, particularly in preserving fine-grained features like text readability and facial identity in generated images. The authors introduce TokBench, a benchmark dataset comprising images and videos rich in text and faces, coupled with a lightweight evaluation pipeline. They use OCR accuracy for text and facial similarity for faces as metrics, arguing that these better reflect human perception than traditional metrics like PSNR, SSIM, and LPIPS. Through extensive experiments, the authors demonstrate the limitations of existing visual tokenizers and VAEs in preserving fine-grained features at smaller scales and show that their proposed metrics provide a more accurate assessment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Incremental Contribution:** The idea of evaluating tokenizers isn't entirely new; existing metrics aim to capture image quality. However, the key novelty lies in the _task-specific_ evaluation focusing on text and faces, which are perceptually critical and often inadequately captured by general-purpose metrics. The dataset curation, though leveraging existing datasets, is tailored for this specific evaluation purpose.
*   **Metrics:**  While OCR and facial similarity models are established tools, their application in this specific _evaluation_ context, directly assessing the reconstruction fidelity of tokenizers regarding human-perceptible attributes, is a novel and valuable aspect. The demonstration that traditional metrics fail to correlate with human judgment provides a strong justification.
*   **Lightweight Benchmark:** The creation of a readily usable benchmark (dataset + evaluation pipeline) that can be run on a single GPU with limited memory makes this accessible to a broader range of researchers.

**Significance:**

*   **Practical Value:** TokBench provides a practical tool for researchers and practitioners working on visual generation. It allows for quick and targeted evaluation of tokenizers, helping them make informed decisions about which tokenizer to use for a given task and identify areas for improvement.
*   **Limitations:** The benchmark's usefulness is bounded by the quality of the pre-trained models used for evaluation (OCR and face recognition). If these models are biased or perform poorly on certain types of text or faces, the TokBench results will also be affected. The benchmark is inherently limited to evaluating the reconstruction quality of text and faces but does not evaluate the generative capabilities themselves.
*   **Generalizability:**  While the paper focuses on text and faces, the core concept of task-aware evaluation could be extended to other specific visual elements or attributes that are important for particular applications (e.g., evaluating the preservation of fine details in medical images).
*   **Impact:**  The benchmark could influence the development of new tokenizers designed specifically to preserve fine-grained details, potentially leading to improved visual generation quality in tasks where text and faces are crucial.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a gap in existing evaluation methods.
*   **Well-Justified Approach:**  The choice of text and faces as critical visual elements is well-argued.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  The experiments convincingly demonstrate the limitations of existing metrics and the benefits of the proposed approach.
*   **Accessible Benchmark:** The lightweight and easy-to-use benchmark makes it practical for others to adopt.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on Pre-trained Models:** The benchmark's accuracy is dependent on the reliability and potential biases of the pre-trained OCR and face recognition models.
*   **Limited Scope:**  The focus is restricted to text and faces, potentially overlooking other relevant visual attributes.
*   **Lack of Novel Algorithmic Contribution**: The primary contribution is evaluation-focused, which isn't in itself an algorithm breakthrough.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a useful and reasonably novel contribution to the field of visual generation evaluation. The task-specific approach, the curated benchmark, and the demonstration of shortcomings in traditional metrics all add value. However, the dependency on pre-trained models and the limited scope restricts the level of impact. While the benchmark has the potential to influence future research, it falls short of being a groundbreaking, field-transforming work.

### Lost in the Haystack: Smaller Needles are More Difficult for LLMs to Find
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18148v1)
- **Authors**: Owen Bianchi, Mathew J. Koretsky, Maya Willey, Chelsea X. Alvarado, Tanay Nayak, Adi Asija, Nicole Kuznetsov, Mike A. Nalls, Faraz Faghri, Daniel Khashabi
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) face significant challenges with needle-in-a-haystack tasks, where relevant information ("the needle") must be drawn from a large pool of irrelevant context ("the haystack"). Previous studies have highlighted positional bias and distractor quantity as critical factors affecting model performance, yet the influence of gold context size has received little attention. We address this gap by systematically studying how variations in gold context length impact LLM performance on long-context question answering tasks. Our experiments reveal that LLM performance drops sharply when the gold context is shorter, i.e., smaller gold contexts consistently degrade model performance and amplify positional sensitivity, posing a major challenge for agentic systems that must integrate scattered, fine-grained information of varying lengths. This pattern holds across three diverse domains (general knowledge, biomedical reasoning, and mathematical reasoning) and seven state-of-the-art LLMs of various sizes and architectures. Our work provides clear insights to guide the design of robust, context-aware LLM-driven systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Lost in the Haystack: Smaller Needles are More Difficult for LLMs to Find":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how the size of relevant information ("gold context") impacts the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in "needle-in-a-haystack" tasks.  The authors systematically vary the length of the gold context within a larger pool of irrelevant context ("distractor context") across three diverse domains (general knowledge, biomedical reasoning, and mathematical reasoning) and seven different LLMs. Their key finding is that *smaller* gold contexts consistently lead to significantly worse performance and amplify positional sensitivity compared to larger gold contexts, even though both are sufficient to answer the question in a gold-only setting. They further analyze how domain specificity, positional variance, and distractor volume influence this phenomenon. The paper concludes that the size of the relevant information is a critical, often overlooked, factor affecting LLM aggregation performance in long-context tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core finding that smaller gold context size negatively impacts LLM performance on needle-in-a-haystack tasks is novel. While previous studies explored positional bias and the impact of distractors, the explicit focus on the *size* of the relevant information is a valuable contribution.  The paper highlights a potentially underestimated vulnerability in how LLMs process long contexts.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the design and deployment of LLM-driven agentic systems.  The observation that small, scattered pieces of crucial information are harder to aggregate highlights a critical weakness in current LLMs.  Understanding this weakness is vital for creating more robust and trustworthy AI systems that rely on aggregating information from multiple sources. Identifying a fundamental limitation and pointing towards potential avenues for mitigation or system design adaptation makes this work relevant.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The experimental setup is well-designed and rigorous, systematically varying gold context size and position across multiple benchmarks and LLMs.
    *   **Diverse Domains:** Testing the hypothesis across three diverse domains strengthens the generality of the findings.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written, and the results are clearly presented with informative figures and tables.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The authors provide practical suggestions for designing more robust aggregation strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Mitigation Strategies Explored:** The paper focuses on identifying the problem but does not delve deeply into mitigation strategies. While it offers some suggestions, further research in this area is needed. This work sets the stage for more exploration into that space.
    *   **Gold Context-Distractor Ratio:** The paper fixes distractor lengths and varies the gold length, resulting in different gold-to-distractor ratios. This design choice, while realistic in some settings, means it's difficult to completely disentangle the effects of gold context size from the gold-to-distractor ratio. A future study that holds the ratio constant might be helpful.
    *   **Synthetic vs. Real Distractors:** While the authors used a real-world retriever for CBB, the distractors for NQ and NM were synthetically generated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on long-context reasoning and LLM aggregation strategies. It will likely prompt further investigation into techniques to improve the handling of small, scattered pieces of relevant information. It also provides a valuable diagnostic tool for practitioners building LLM-based systems.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the findings are insightful and the evaluation is rigorous, the paper is primarily diagnostic. It uncovers a previously overlooked problem but offers limited solutions. The study also involves some limitations, as mentioned above. This positions the paper as an important contribution for illuminating a weakness and providing direction, yet leaves the development of specific fixes to future work.

### First Finish Search: Efficient Test-Time Scaling in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18149v1)
- **Authors**: Aradhye Agarwal, Ayan Sengupta, Tanmoy Chakraborty
- **Abstract**: Test-time scaling (TTS), which involves dynamic allocation of compute during inference, offers a promising way to improve reasoning in large language models. While existing TTS methods work well, they often rely on long decoding paths or require a large number of samples to be generated, increasing the token usage and inference latency. We observe the surprising fact that for reasoning tasks, shorter traces are much more likely to be correct than longer ones. Motivated by this, we introduce First Finish Search (FFS), a training-free parallel decoding strategy that launches $n$ independent samples and returns as soon as any one completes. We evaluate FFS alongside simple decoding, beam search, majority voting, and budget forcing on four reasoning models (DeepSeek-R1, R1-Distill-Qwen-32B, QwQ-32B and Phi-4-Reasoning-Plus) and across four datasets (AIME24, AIME25-I, AIME25-II and GPQA Diamond). With DeepSeek-R1, FFS achieves $82.23\%$ accuracy on the AIME datasets, a $15\%$ improvement over DeepSeek-R1's standalone accuracy, nearly matching OpenAI's o4-mini performance. Our theoretical analysis explains why stopping at the shortest trace is likely to yield a correct answer and identifies the conditions under which early stopping may be suboptimal. The elegance and simplicity of FFS demonstrate that straightforward TTS strategies can perform remarkably well, revealing the untapped potential of simple approaches at inference time.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces First Finish Search (FFS), a novel training-free test-time scaling (TTS) method for large language models (LLMs) applied to reasoning tasks. FFS is based on the observation that shorter traces in LLM reasoning are often more accurate. The method works by launching multiple independent samples and returning the output trace that completes first. The authors evaluate FFS on several reasoning datasets using different LLMs, demonstrating accuracy improvements and reduced token usage compared to baselines like beam search, majority voting, and budget forcing. They also provide theoretical analysis supporting the efficacy of the approach, particularly regarding its compute efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of FFS – prioritizing the shortest trace during test-time scaling – is relatively novel. While prior works have explored various TTS strategies, the focus on "first finish" as a primary selection criterion is a fresh perspective. The paper effectively exploits an observed correlation between trace length and correctness in the context of reasoning tasks. However, the method's simplicity also means it's not fundamentally a complex algorithmic innovation.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance lies in its practical applicability. FFS is training-free and API-friendly, making it easily deployable with existing LLMs.  The reported performance gains in accuracy alongside reduced token usage are valuable, particularly in resource-constrained environments or API-metered deployments. The reduced latency due to early termination further enhances its appeal. This could allow for a simple method to scale performance improvements without the need to invest in model fine-tuning, or complex parameter exploration.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Simplicity:** FFS is easy to implement and understand.
    *   **Efficiency:** Reduces token usage and latency compared to other TTS techniques.
    *   **Training-free and API-friendly:** Facilitates easy integration with existing LLMs.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** Demonstrates performance improvements on several benchmarks.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** Provides insights into why FFS works, though the underlying assumptions are somewhat simplified.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The effectiveness of FFS relies on the correlation between trace length and correctness, which may not hold true for all tasks or LLMs (demonstrated in the paper with DeepSeek-V3). The paper could explore conditions where this assumption fails or provide guidance on task applicability.
    *   **Oversimplified Analysis:** The theoretical analysis relies on assumptions (e.g., normal distribution of trace lengths) that may not perfectly reflect real-world scenarios, particularly at longer trace lengths. Further, while they note the reverse link of shorter traces being more likely to be correct also means correct traces are more likely to be short, they should examine more closely if this relation holds with more complex models or tasks, especially those that involve nuanced iterative reasoning.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the performance gains are notable, they are not revolutionary. The improvements largely match or slightly exceed the performance of other TTS methods. Also, it should be made clear that this method does not necessarily improve the performance ceiling, but only decreases the compute and time needed to reach this ceiling.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** While several baselines are used, comparison against advanced hybrid TTS approaches or advanced fine-tuning methods with specific reward models is lacking.

*   **Potential Influence:** FFS could influence the design of future TTS strategies by highlighting the importance of trace length as a factor. Its simplicity could encourage wider adoption of TTS in practical applications. It also opens up a new avenue of exploration for TTS scaling strategies with different parameters.

**Overall Score:**

While FFS provides a useful and efficient technique for improving LLM reasoning, the novelty, while present, is incremental. The reliance on trace length correlation and the simplicity of the algorithm, coupled with lack of detailed comparative studies limit the paper's score. It offers a practical solution, but may not fundamentally shift the direction of research.
